Measurement and Prediction of Rotor Noise Sources for sUAS in Outdoor and
Laboratory Environments
Jeremiah Whelchel
ABSTRACT
This work provides an experimental analysis of the acoustic footprint of a
hexacopter in hover and low speed forward flight, comparison of aerodynamic
performance and noise of eVTOL rotors operating in multiple facilities, and analysis of the
noise associated with an outrunner brushless DC motor. Empirical and low-order models
are used to predict aerodynamic performance, tonal noise, and broadband noise for isolated
eVTOL rotors. In addition, a low noise, swept rotor design was evaluated. The acoustic
footprint of a DJI Matrice 600 Pro hexacopter in hover and low speed forward flight was
measured in the Virginia Tech Drone Park. The noise radiated by this vehicle was found
to be dominated by tonal noise at low frequencies and dominated by broadband noise at
high frequencies indicating that both are important when assessing the noise of these
aircraft. Three distinct regions were observed in the frequency spectra of the noise. Aweighting measured acoustic spectra highlighted the importance of the mid-frequency
broadband noise, in particular. The radiated noise in hover was also found to be similar to
the noise of the vehicle during low-speed flyovers. Given this, significantly less complex
measurements of an aircraft in hover or those associated with a rotor at static conditions
may be used to assess the footprint of an eVTOL aircraft in low speed forward flight. The
total vehicle noise was then decomposed by studying the performance and noise of isolated
eVTOL rotors in multiple facilities and under different operating conditions.
Facility effects on performance and noise were first assessed by experimentally
studying two commercially available eVTOL rotors in an enclosed anechoic environment
and an open environment. For experimental measurements that were conducted in the
anechoic chamber, recirculation effects were shown to increase harmonic amplitudes more
than 8 dB. Varying solidity screens were placed in the downstream wake of each rotor to
delay the onset of recirculation. Placing the screens in the wake did not produce a noticeable
effect on or delay recirculation within the confined testing environment. Measurements of the
BPF and higher order harmonics of each rotor were found to be much more consistent in
time when testing outdoors in an open-air environment. Amplitudes of these tones were
also found to be like that of the spectral levels of the measurements conducted in the
anechoic chamber once recirculation had been established. While the tonal levels were
much more consistent throughout each measurement in the open-air environment, a
significant amount of background noise was present and made characterizing the noise at
low frequencies difficult. Environmental factors, mainly windspeed, were also found to
impact the noise measurements which also added difficulty in characterizing the noise of
the two tested rotors. In indoor facilities, the rotor inflow becomes contaminated due to
recirculation shortly after the rotor reaches steady state and spectral levels of tones
increased with increasing spectral averaging times. In outdoor environments, the inflow to
the rotor disc becomes distorted due to changing wind conditions and turbulence in the
atmosphere. Spectral levels of tones in the outdoor environment remained consistent in
amplitude but exceeded those of the anechoic chamber significantly. Given this,

environmental factors and recirculation were found to both increase the higher order
harmonics.
To mitigate these facility effects, measurements of force and noise were also
conducted for the same two rotors in an anechoic open jet. Additionally, measurements
were also conducted for a commercially available rotor along with a newly designed low
noise swept rotor. Each of these rotors were tested in the anechoic open jet facility at static
conditions and with the tunnel on. These measurements were accompanied with predictions
of aerodynamic performance and tonal and broadband self-noise. BEMT was used to
predict aerodynamic performance. Tonal noise associated with the rotor blade loading and
thickness was predicted using F1A and rotor broadband self-noise was predicted using the
model of BPM. The measured noise in this facility along with that from measurements in
the anechoic chamber and outdoor environment were separated into tonal and broadband
components by applying a phase averaging technique to the measured acoustic pressure
time history. These results also show that in the indoor facility that the noise produced at
the BPF is dominated by tonal sources, but the higher order harmonics can be attributed to
broadband interactions particularly at static conditions. Broadband noise was drastically
reduced by driving the tunnel at minimal inflow for the smallest rotor studied (𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 = 120
mm). For the larger rotors (𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 ≥ 267 mm) broadband noise associated with BWI or TIN
were not mitigated at low inflow speeds. Predictions of tonal noise at the BPF were within
3 dB for all observer locations when considering the smallest rotor studied. Predictions of
the measured directivity at the BPF for the larger rotors were inaccurate although
predictions of thrust agreed with the measured. The largest rotors tested were equal in
diameter to that of the open jet inlet. Thus, the limits of the testing facility were exceeded
and increased noise was produced as the rotor blades interacted with the shear layer of the
open jet. Directivity patterns of each rotor were also found to vary with increasing
rotational rate. Overall, these results show that for analyzing the noise at hover conditions,
introducing a small amount of inflow may be a good option when trying to understand the
tonal noise and allows one to characterize the tonal noise independent of the broadband.
However, this was also shown to be heavily dependent on the rotor diameter with regards
to the open jet inlet and experimentalist must take this into consideration. While these
measurements provide an analysis of the noise in hover and low speed ascent, they do not
assess the noise of the vehicle operating in forward flight. In forward flight the rotors are
subjected to edgewise flows which have an effect on the radiated noise thus analyzing the
noise of these rotors operating at an angle of attack to the incoming flow was assessed.
These effects were investigated by experimentally measuring the performance and
noise of the smallest rotor studied when operating at a yaw relative to the incoming flow.
For increasing yaw at the examined wind tunnel velocities, the measured thrust was found
to converge to the value for zero inflow. Contours of SPL as a function of yaw angle for
no inflow and an inflow speed of 8 m/s showed spectral levels to be minimal for an inplane observer from 5×BPF to 30×BPF. The broadband noise was found to increase
significantly for increasing yaw angle and tunnel inflow speed. These results show once
again that the broadband noise is especially important during forward flight and new
methods that consider wake interaction are needed to predict the noise in this flight regime.
The rotor geometric parameter of sweep was also assessed from measurements in
the anechoic open jet by comparing the aerodynamic performance and noise of a
commercially available 762 mm diameter CF30x10.5 T-motor eVTOL rotor to that of an

in house designed low noise swept rotor. The addition of sweep was found to reduce noise
associated with BWI or TIN as the separated broadband noise was found to be less than
that of the commercially available rotor. Comparison of thrust at static conditions and with
increasing advance ratios showed both rotors to have similar performance, thus the addition
of sweep was effective at reducing noise without sacrificing performance.
Lastly, the noise associated with the electric drive system of these aircraft which
consists of an ESC and brushless DC motor was analyzed. Acoustic measurements were
made with and without an acoustic enclosure installed on a brushless DC motor and was
found to be effective at reducing noise associated with the electric motor. The effects of
two ESC’s as well as their switching rates were also studied. The noise was found to be
similar for both ESCs at low frequencies. At high frequencies the measured noise spectrum
was found to be different when controlling the motor with different ESC’s and a higher
switching rate was found to reduce the noise with increasing switching rate although not
completely monotonically.

Measurement and Prediction of Rotor Noise Sources for sUAS in Outdoor and
Laboratory Environments
Jeremiah Whelchel
GENERAL AUDIENCE ABSTRACT
A new class of multi-rotor VTOL electric aircraft is becoming a dominant advanced
vehicle concept. Urban Air Mobility (UAM) vehicles are designed for short routes within
urban environments carrying only a few passengers during each flight. Other smaller
Unmanned Aerial Systems (UAS) are increasingly being used for delivery services or to
perform tasks which are more easily accessed with this technology like inspection or
photography. Thus, these vehicles are expected to operate in close proximity to the general
populace exposing it to aircraft noise which is currently limited to communities
surrounding airports. For successful integration into the airspace with minimal community
annoyance, the mechanisms responsible for generation of the noise must be understood.
Traditionally, for conventional rotorcraft (one main rotor), the tonal noise has been more
of a concern than the broadband component. eVTOL vehicles are often equipped with
multiple rotors that are lightly loaded and operate at lower tip speeds which can be time
varying. Thus, there is an increased significance of broadband noise. Lastly, these aircraft
are equipped with an electric drive system that gives rise to an additional noise source that
is not present for conventional aircraft. Best practices for measuring eVTOL noise are not
currently established. Measurement of eVTOL rotor noise is complicated by the increased
significance of the broadband sources. These have been shown to be facility dependent.
Given this, there is a need for high quality experimental data and an analysis of
experimental data in multiple facilities for these rotors and drive systems. Capabilities of
traditional models to predict conventional rotorcraft noise also need to be assessed for these
rotors. These two issues have been assessed in this work by first assessing the character of
an eVTOL aircraft in hover and low speed flyovers. Both tonal and broadband components
of the radiated noise were found to be significant. A-weighting, which is a metric used to
assess the response of the human ear to the radiated noise showed increased significance
of the broadband noise. This was followed by a characterization of the noise of isolated
eVTOL rotors in multiple environments. Facility effects were addressed, and a low order
prediction model was developed using methods that are traditionally used to predict noise
associated with conventional rotorcraft. Lastly, the noise associated with the electric drive
system of these vehicles was assessed and recommendations on how to reduce this source
of noise were made. These results can be used to guide experimentalists when performing
measurements of eVTOL rotor noise at static conditions and provide an eVTOL rotor noise
data set that can be used to validate existing and forthcoming aerodynamic and acoustic
prediction methods.

noise changed. This was shortly followed by the derivation of the fundamental
mathematical equations that described the reasons why the sound field changed, at that

xiv

Chapter 1 Introduction
1.1 Motivation
A new class of electric aircraft is becoming a dominant advanced vehicle concept that
intends to be used to transport people and goods as part of the urban and regional transportation
infrastructure. Given the broad range of intended use a range of aircraft types is expected which
include small and medium Unmanned Aircraft Systems (UAS), electric Conventional Takeoff and
Landing (eCTOL) aircraft, and electric Vertical Takeoff and Landing (eVTOL) aircraft. Compared
to conventional rotorcraft these vehicles vary significantly in configuration and flow regimes in
which they operate giving rise to many sources of noise. These vehicles are also intended to operate
near the general populace exposing it to aircraft noise that they have not been exposed to before.
This new source of noise exposure could hinder the integration of these aircraft into the urban and
transportation infrastructure. Experimental methods and noise prediction models do exist for
conventional rotorcraft. The effectiveness of traditional experimental methods and noise prediction
schemes have not been fully assessed with regards to this new class of electric aircraft and the
character of the noise is expected to be different. Thus, for effective integration of these aircraft
into the airspace the noise of these aircraft must be assessed and the primary noise generating
mechanisms must be understood. Understanding of these noise generating mechanisms will also
aid in low noise rotor designs. Traditionally experimental data sets are used to validate noise
prediction schemes for rotorcraft. Analyzing the character of eVTOL rotor noise is difficult due to
transient effects which make comparison of measurements difficult. Computational models also
struggle to compare well with experimental results particularly for a rotor in hover. High quality
experimental data sets are needed to validate existing rotor noise prediction methods. The effects
common facilities used to measure rotor noise have on aerodynamic performance and radiated
noise must be understood.
Many experimental and computational studies have shown that both the tonal and
broadband components are important for eVTOL applications. Given the increased number of
rotors, rotor distribution, rotor size, body geometry and lower disk loading the associated
broadband noise is expected to be greater than that of conventional rotorcraft. Fair comparison of
experimental data to computational results is complicated by these broadband noise sources.
Experimental methods to measure eVTOL noise are also not clearly defined and best practices are
not currently established, therefore comparison of experimental data sets from facility to facility
produce conflicting results. This dissertation focuses on the measurement and prediction of rotor
noise sources for sUAS in outdoor and laboratory environments.

1.2 Electric VTOL (eVTOL) Aircraft for Advanced Air Mobility
The term Advanced Air Mobility (AAM) was recently adopted by NASA to describe an
emerging air transport system that integrates new, transformational aircraft designs and flight
technologies into existing and modified airspace operations. This new emerging air transport
system is intended to provide transportation of people and goods with a broad spectrum of electric
Vertical Take-off and Landing (eVTOL) vehicles. Recently, Rizzi et al. [1] categorized these
vehicles based on the market they are intended to serve which are small/medium unmanned aerial
1

systems (sUAS), Urban Air Mobility (UAM), Thin/Short Haul, and Large UAS and HALE. The
sUAS market includes aircraft that would be used for operations such as package delivery,
reconnaissance, and infrastructure inspection. Thes vehicles will be remotely piloted or operate
autonomously at low to mid altitudes with payloads of 10 – 1000 lbs. The UAM market includes
vehicles for public transportation and package delivery. UAM strives to provide on demand,
affordable, quiet and fast transportation in both urban and rural environments. Vehicles within the
UAM category are intended to fly at altitudes less than 3000 ft and transport no more than six
people or equivalent cargo weight. The thin/short haul category encompasses vehicles intended
for flights less than 12,500 ft with 9-30 passengers or equivalent cargo weight. Lastly, vehicles
serving the large UAS and HALE markets are intended to perform long endurance missions at a
broad range of altitudes with no transportation of humans [1]. Thin/short haul aircraft and large
UAS are intended for use at high altitudes whereas, sUAS and UAM vehicles are envisioned to fly
at lower altitudes subjecting the general populace or local fauna to aircraft noise. Rizzi et al. [1]
states that these new noise exposure levels and annoyance could limit the successful integration of
sUAS and UAM vehicles into the transportation system. To inhibit the successful integration of
these aircraft into the existing airspace the noise generated by these vehicles must be understood.
In comparison to conventional rotorcraft, these vehicles contain multiple rotors that are lightly
loaded and operate at low tip speeds. Thus, the character of the noise is expected to be different
which could lead to inaccurate assessments of the radiated noise when conducting experiments, or
computational simulations. Total vehicle system noise could be inaccurately characterized if the
primary responsible mechanisms for noise generation are not understood. This could lead to
aircraft and aircraft component designs that are acoustically efficient. Given this, the noise radiated
by rotors equipped on vehicles intended for the sUAS and UAM markets is the focus of this work
and will be referred to as eVTOL aircraft from here on.
A traditional turbine powered rotorcraft is generally composed of a main and tail rotor,
whereas this new class of electric aircraft varies significantly in configuration and the flow regime
in which they encounter. These variations in configurations include the number of rotors, rotor
distribution, rotor size, and body geometry. Examples of eVTOL aircraft from small scale vehicles
used for package delivery to larger scale vehicles used for public transportation are shown in
Figure 1.1. The Wing Hummingbird sUAS, which is currently used for short range package
deliveries is composed of twelve rotors and four puller propellers that are closely spaced. Varying
configurations are also apparent for larger aircraft intended for UAM applications as shown in
Figure 1.1b and Figure 1.1c. Here the Moog Surefly is shown equipped with two counter rotating
rotors per arm yielding a total of eight along with the Joby S4 aircraft which is composed of six
tilt rotors that are used for both VTOL and forward flight.

2

Figure 1.1 – Examples of eVTOL aircraft, a) Wing Hummingbird, b) Moog Surefly, c) Joby S4
The flow regimes encountered by rotorcraft are commonly defined in terms of the tip Mach
number and chord-based Reynolds number at 75% span. Full scale conventional rotorcraft exhibit
advancing tip Mach numbers in the range of 0.7 ≤ 𝑀𝑡𝑖𝑝 ≤ 0.8, whereas eVTOL aircraft would
exhibit those in the range of 0.15 ≤ 𝑀𝑡𝑖𝑝 ≤ 0.3. Characteristic Reynolds numbers based on the
chord at 75% of the blade radius of a full scale helicopter is customarily on the order of
𝑅𝑒𝑐 (0.75𝑅) ~ 106, while that for eVTOL is 𝑅𝑒𝑐 (0.75𝑅)~ 104-105 [2]. With respect to
conventional flat plate aerodynamics chord-based Reynolds numbers of conventional rotorcraft lie
3

in the fully turbulent flow regime while those of eVTOL correspond to the laminar transitional
flow regime. This lack of dynamic similarity results in questions regarding the application of
conventional rotorcraft noise prediction tools to these varying configurations that operate at lower
tip speeds and representative Reynolds numbers. Analysis and prediction of conventional
rotorcraft has been primarily focused on the deterministic components. In contrary, the broadband
noise of eVTOL aircraft is expected have increased significance with respect to the human ears
sensitivity [2]. Broadband noise is also expected to have increased importance relative to the tonal
due to the rotors being lightly loaded and operating at lower tip speeds [3].
For smaller scale vehicles, like the Wing Hummingbird, the tip to tip spacing between
counter rotating rotors is small and each rotor is placed in the vicinity of an airframe component.
Design parameters like these have been shown to increase tonal noise due to periodic lift
disturbances and broadband noise due to rotor-rotor interactions [4–7]. The radiated noise also
varies according to the phase of flight. In steady flight, rotational speeds of the rotors are often not
controlled precisely and therefore vary independently producing irritating modulations both in
frequency and amplitude [8]. During loiter, an operating condition potentially necessary for
package delivery, rotors operate at vanishing advance ratio (high sectional angle of attack) causing
portions of the blade disk near the hub to become stalled. Increased noise is a product of this
inefficient operation [2]. eVTOL are also known for their ability to perform rapid maneuvers.
These maneuvers produce transient increases in noise levels. A similar effect is observed for
helicopters in maneuver such that custom flight planning operations have been suggested to
mitigate these effects [9]. As shown, the noise associated with eVTOL aircraft is different from
that of conventional aircraft due to the differences in design parameters and operational
characteristics [10]. These vehicles are also intended to fly numerous short missions at relatively
low altitudes over both urban and rural areas. These operational characteristics will expose
populated areas and local fauna to aircraft noise that they were not previously exposed to [1]. Flight
procedures for commercial civil transport aircraft are currently regulated by the FAA to minimize
emissions and noise exposure levels which impact local communities. As commercial services of
eVTOL become routine they are likely to have a similar focus.
Currently, the FAA uses the Aviation Environmental Design Tool (AEDT) for domestic
aviation system planning as well as for domestic and international aviation environmental policy
analysis. This comprehensive tool models aircraft performance in both space and time to provide
information to FAA stakeholders on the environmental impacts such as fuel consumption,
emissions, noise, and air quality consequences. Environmental impacts due to aircraft noise are
estimated in terms of noise exposure metrics. The FAA estimates noise exposure using the metric
Day-Night Average Sound Level (DNL) which is a measure of cumulative noise exposure over a
24-hour period. An adjustment is also included in the form of noise penalties where a single aircraft
flight at night is counted as ten flights, thus increasing the number of exposure events. AEDT
computes DNL according to the SAE AIR1845A standard, which is based on the evaluation of
experimentally derived Noise-Power-Distance (NPD) curves. The integrated quantity of sound
exposure level (SEL) is used to build NPD curves (SEL vs slant distance from flight path at
different power settings). Development of these curves begins with acoustic measurements of
flyover events at locations directly under the flight path with the aircraft at a certain slant distance
(altitude) for each NPD engine power setting [11]. These experimentally derived NPD curves are
then directly extrapolated to other aircraft operating at similar power settings allowing for the
estimation of DNL which signifies the impact aircraft noise has on the community. While the
current FAA model, AEDT, has been used successfully for conventional aircraft and rotorcraft, it
4

is not appropriate for eVTOL aircraft. First the direct extrapolation of NPD curves from one
eVTOL to another is not possible due to the significant variations shown previously in Figure 1.1.
AEDT assumes that the rotor or propeller states are constant, which is not true for eVTOL as rotor
RPM and sectional pitch angles vary continuously and independently. Both of these vary due to
control inputs that are required to maintain the orientation of the vehicle [12]. The rotors equipped
on these aircraft are not phase locked, thus the phase relationships between each rotor are
constantly changing producing directivities that vary with time. In certain flight conditions,
particularly hover, these rotors can operate at similar blade passage frequencies resulting in
increased tonal noise due to the coherence of radiated acoustic waves near the BPF. This coherent
addition can produce lobes of acoustic radiation that are tightly focused in certain directions [13].
As shown, the current FAA model used to estimate community impact during flyover
operations is not applicable to eVTOL as the radiated noise is a function of flight path and
maneuver. Tonal and broadband sources also shift in weight during a flyover event which is not
accounted for when computing NPD curves in this way. Lastly, the current FAA model allows for
the direct extrapolation of NPD curves as conventional aircraft exhibit a standard configuration.
In contrary, this new class of electric aircraft is composed of many different concepts. Therefore,
direct extrapolation of NPD curves between designs, although similar, may produce poor estimates
of the system noise if the primary responsible mechanisms for noise generation are not well
understood. These platforms are more integrated into the community as they are intended to fly at
lower altitudes, closer to observers, and exhibit a wider range of directivities [1]. Given this,
analysis of experimental data and basic modeling of the individual noise sources and directivity is
a must. Modeling of these individual noise sources must be validated by full-scale isolated
propeller/rotor noise experimental data across the anticipated flight regime [1]. Once modeling
techniques of the individual noise sources have been validated, extrapolation and estimation of the
noise for novel configurations without direct experimental measurement of all platforms can be
conducted. This ultimately leads to certification of these new aircraft and low noise rotor designs.

1.3 eVTOL Rotorcraft Noise Sources
The responsible mechanisms for the generation of noise by eVTOL aircraft can be
decomposed as shown in Figure 1.2. While eVTOL noise is composed of multiple sources, the
focus of this work pertains to that generated by the rotor and the electric drive system. Noise
generated by a rotor can be decomposed into tonal (periodic) and random (non-deterministic)
components. Tonal noise is caused by sources that repeat themselves exactly during each rotation.
These aerodynamically generated tones or sharp spectral features tend to dominate far field
acoustic spectra from these rotors and flight vehicles near the blade passage frequency (BPF) and
its first few harmonics. Low frequency discrete tones are resultant of the rotors steady thrust and
blade thickness. Although, these discrete tones are produced by the rotor’s steady thrust, Roger
and Moreau [14], find that the noise produced by small quadrotor systems is primarily a result of
inhomogeneities produced by interaction of support struts or periodic forcing produced by blades
advancing and retreating during rotation in forward flight. On some concepts, individual rotors are
mounted near airframe components such as support struts or a fuselage causing rotor airframe
interactions. These interactions have also been shown to generate additional tonal noise at the
higher order harmonics of the BPF [6,7]. In hover and forward flight one rotor’s wake that contains
tip or inboard originated vortices can become re-ingested by another. For eVTOL aircraft in hover
that are equipped with counterrotating coaxial rotors strong aerodynamic interactions occur
between the upper and lower sets of rotor blades resulting in highly impulsive loads, thus increased
5

tonal noise [15]. In forward flight, blade vortex interaction (BVI) has been shown to be the most
dominant aerodynamic interaction but to be much weaker than that of a conventional helicopter
[16]. Tonal noise sources associated with steady and unsteady blade loading radiate primarily
above and below the rotor plane while that associated with blade thickness radiate primarily in the
rotor plane.
Random non-deterministic noise can produce both narrow and broad spectral features.
Narrowband non-deterministic features become particularly important for eVTOL aircraft when
conducting static tests of rotors at hover conditions in both confined indoor and outdoor open-air
facilities due to the ingestion of turbulence. As the leading edge of rotor blades interact with the
ingested turbulence unsteady loads are produced on the blades of the rotor which is referred to as
turbulence ingestion noise (TIN). Outdoors, ingested atmospheric turbulence can become highly
anisotropic due to the strong stream tube contraction that is produced by the thrusting rotor . This
causes the length scales of the turbulence to increase in the rotor axial direction [17]. In confined
indoor facilities such as an anechoic chamber, the turbulent downwash of the thrusting rotor
recirculates within chamber and becomes re-ingested. This recirculation of the rotor’s own
turbulent wake does not occur in outdoor environments. This produces narrowband random
spectral features which are not indicative of an isolated rotor in hover [18]. Experimentalists
measuring the noise of small scale eVTOL rotors at hover in anechoic chambers should take caution
to ensure that the noise due to recirculation does not dominant their noise measurements [10]. Placing
multiple screens in the downstream wake of a rotor has been shown to delay the onset of
recirculation which effectively allows for longer acquisition times and is postulated to lead to a
more accurate characterization of the radiated noise [19]. In both facilities the axial time scales of
the ingested turbulence become significantly larger than the time scale of a blade to blade passage
resulting in blade to blade correlation [20]. This correlation in blade loads causes “haystacking” in
the noise spectrum where the noise forms spectral peaks around the BPF and its harmonics [21]. Nondeterministic noise with broad spectral features is also produced when the leading edge of one
rotor blade interacts with the turbulent wake of a previous blade and is commonly referred to as
blade wake interaction (BWI) noise. This interaction produces broad spectral features from mid to
high frequencies in hover and descending flight because the shed turbulent wake is composed of a
wide range of eddy sizes [3]. These two non-deterministic sources produce unsteady loads on the
rotor blades which is known to radiate primarily below the rotor plane [22].
In forward flight, noise sources associated with TIN and BWI become weak, and the
broadband self-noise becomes dominant at mid to high frequencies for conventional helicopters.
Broadband self-noise is due to the interaction between the rotor blades and the turbulence produced
in its own boundary layer and near wake [23]. Brooks and Burley (2001) listed the five
mechanisms responsible for the generation of broadband rotor self-noise under subsonic flow
conditions. These mechanisms were due to laminar flows that separate from the rotor blades,
vortex shedding due to trailing edge bluntness, boundary layer separation at the onset of deep stall,
the interaction of the turbulent boundary layer with the trailing edges of the rotor blades, and the
formation of a tip vortex at the rotor blade tips. Laminar boundary layer vortex shedding (LBLVS) can generate a broadband noise peak that is related to the shedding rates at the TE. Trailing
edge bluntness vortex shedding (TBL-VS) occurs in small, separated flow regions aft of the rotor
blades trailing edges. The turbulent boundary layer can separate near the trailing edge on the
pressure side of the rotor blades and at low advance ratios local sectional angles of attack can
become large resulting in stall. While the former results in increased noise from mid to high
frequencies and the latter results in increased broadband noise at low frequencies. These are
commonly referred to as separation-stall noise. The interaction of the trailing edge with turbulence
6

in the boundary layer produces broadband noise at mid to high frequencies as well and is
commonly referred to as turbulent boundary layer trailing edge (TBL-TE) noise. Lastly, the tip
vortices form on the rotor blade tips and produce trailing edge noise as they pass over the rotor
blade tips and is commonly referred to as tip vortex formation noise. Of these self-noise sources
turbulent boundary layer trailing edge (TBL-TE) noise is typically the most dominant [24]. Selfnoise associated with eVTOL aircraft has been shown to have significance with respect to the
human ears sensitivity, increase with increasing number of rotors, and vary during a single rotor
revolution [2,25,26]. In edgewise flight, rotor blades are subjected to an azimuthally varying
loading condition, were the advancing blade sections experience higher inflow velocities than the
retreating one as it rotates around its axis [27]. Broadband self-noise can also vary during one rotor
revolution due to turbulence ingestion from aerodynamic interactions or atmospheric turbulence
[10]. In hover, where the aerodynamic loading is steady, broadband noise variation has been shown
to occur with helicopters due to the change in blade positions, convective amplification, and
retarded time effects [28]. In these scenarios no blade-to-blade correlation occurs as the turbulence
that interacts with the boundary layer on the rotor blade’s surface is composed of a wide variety
of length scales. These turbulent interactions that occur on the blades surfaces cause sudden
changes of the local blade section velocities (and thus Reynolds number), which vary the
characteristics of turbulence that develops over the blade section, thus modulating self-noise [8].

Figure 1.2 – Categorization of eVTOL aircraft noise sources adapted from [10]
When considering conventional rotorcraft, less research effort has been devoted to
broadband noise compared to its tonal noise. Rizzi et al. [1] , explain that the design parameters
of the vehicles, in particular the low disk loading, low tip speeds, and proximity of rotors are likely
to increase the relative importance of broadband noise compared to traditional rotorcraft. Given
this, a renewed interest in broadband noise has emerged. Intaratep et al. [29], measured the noise
of a DJI Phantom Quadcopter and showed that the broadband noise increases significantly from
single to multiple rotors. Zawodny et al. [2], showed that single isolated quadcopter rotors generate
significant broadband noise above 1 kHz. Applying an A-weighted filter to these data indicated
7

the human ears high sensitivity to the radiated noise. In all cases this increased broadband noise
contributes significantly to standard metrics that quantify aircraft noise exposure such as DayNight Average Sound Level (DNL) or Community Noise Equivalent Level (CNEL) which do not
consider the different source types (tonal/broadband) and their associated directivities. A final
source of noise particularly important to eVTOL aircraft comes from the electric drive that consists
of an electronic speed controller (ESC) and a brushless DC motor. Noise generated by the electric
motor can surpass the broadband noise at higher frequencies therefore it must be considered when
assessing the total noise of these aircraft [2]. Acoustic electric motor noise is caused by vibrations
within the electric motor structure itself [30]. These vibrations are generated electromagnetically
and mechanically [31].
1.3.1 Summary of eVTOL Aircraft Noise
In recent years, there have been many experimental, analytical, and computational studies
conducted to assess the noise radiated by this new class of electric aircraft with the goal of
contributing to the development of a preliminary database for sUAS, eVTOL, and UAM aircraft.
Full scale vehicle aerodynamic performance and acoustic measurements have been conducted in
outdoor environments and indoor facilities such as wind tunnels and open jets [12,13,32–34].
Computational studies have also been conducted and compared to experimentally measured
performance and noise of these aircraft [16,35–38]. Cabell et al. [12] conducted acoustic
measurements of two small UAVs in hover and forward flight and showed the acoustic signature
of each vehicle to be multi-tonal and time varying during hover as well as during flyover. These
fluctuations in frequency were attributed to the vehicle being equipped with multiple rotors
operating at closely spaced frequencies and individual rotor speed variations due to the flight
control system [12]. Zawodny and Pettingill [33] conducted acoustic measurements of a small
quadcopter in hover and forward flight in the NASA Langley Acoustic Low Speed Aeroacoustic
Wind Tunnel (LSAWT). For hover conditions, a strong presence of spectral peaks at multiples of
the BPF were shown to occur and postulated to be from each rotor interacting with the airframe.
In forward flight, these spectral peaks at multiples of the BPF were shown to roll-off with
increasing frequency and the acoustic spectrum became primarily broadband in nature for
frequencies above 1 kHz [33]. Yoon [39] developed a RANS/LES hybrid solver and conducted
high fidelity CFD simulations on a DJI Phantom 3 and SUI Endurance quadcopter to study the
effects of rotor-airframe and rotor-rotor interactions in hover and forward flight. The threedimensional unsteady Navier Stokes equations were solved on overset grids using high-order
accurate schemes, dual time stepping, low Mach number preconditioning and hybrid turbulence
modeling. For each vehicle in hover, strong pressure waves were shown to form on the fuselage
with the passage of each of the rotor blades, whereas, in forward flight, strong rotor wake
interactions were shown to occur between the wake generated by the forward set of rotors that
became ingested by the aft set [37]. Jia and Lee [38] performed high fidelity computational
simulations using the HPCMP CREATE-AV high fidelity rotorcraft analysis and simulation tool
Helios. Acoustic predictions were also made using the acoustic prediction software PSUWOPWOP. They found BVI to be the most dominant aerodynamic interaction in forward flight
but to be much weaker than that of a conventional helicopter. Rotor-rotor interactions between
forward and aft rotors were also studied and found that they could be avoided by increasing the
vertical separation distance between the two sets of rotors, thus reducing the noise associated with
blade wake interactions. Pettingill and Zawodny [25] performed acoustic measurements and selfnoise broadband predictions of an SUI endurance quadcopter in simulated forward flight in NASA
8

Langley’s LSAWT. They showed the tonal components of the noise to be relatively unaffected,
while the broadband components increased more than 10 dB. This increased broadband noise was
attributed to the ingestion of the turbulent wake of the upstream rotor by the downstream rotor
[25]. These studies highlight the complexities of modeling and measuring the total noise of this
new class of electric aircraft. Traditionally, the tonal component of conventional rotorcraft noise
has been of more concern but as shown, the broadband component is of equal importance for
eVTOL aircraft. There is also very little experimental data available for validation of methods used
to predict total vehicle noise. Experimentally measuring the noise of a complete vehicle is difficult
and the total noise can be decomposed from multiple rotors to that of isolated rotors. This allows
the primary noise generating mechanisms associated with each of the individual rotors and rotorairframe components must be studied as well. As the loadings on the blade surfaces are responsible
for generating noise, experimentally measuring and modeling eVTOL rotor performance is a good
first step.
Performance measurements of low Reynolds numbers rotors have been conducted with the
goal of assisting with the characterization of rotor performance based on geometry, rotor
configuration, and operating conditions [40–42]. These performance measurements are then used
to validate existing rotor performance prediction methodologies such as blade element models,
blade element momentum models, lifting-line models, and vortex models. These measurements
serve as a database to compare existing aerodynamic and aeroacoustic modeling schemes.
Aerodynamic performance can be predicted using low order methods such as blade element
momentum (BEMT) models, lifting-line models, and vortex models. While these models are of
low order, they have been successfully implemented on wind turbines, helicopters, propellers, and
sUAS rotors. Gur and Rosen [43] did a comparison and found the BEMT model predictions to be
the closest to the experimentally measured thrust. The largest differences were shown to occur at
high thrust, low advance ratios or static operation. At these flight conditions, the magnitude of the
induced velocity increases [43]. BEMT models discretize the rotor blades into segments along the
span. This requires generation of lift and drag polars via panel method codes or High Fidelity
Reynolds-Averaged Navier Stokes flow solvers. Accurate generation of these is restricted due to
the presence of low blade section Reynolds numbers that lie in the transitional flow regime which
could lead to the formation of a separation bubble causing the local flow on the suction side of the
rotor blades to separate and reattach [44]. In hover, which is particularly important for eVTOL
aircraft intended for missions such as package delivery, inboard blade sections can become stalled.
XFOIL, a viscous 2D panel method code is commonly used to rapidly generate lift and drag polars
[45]. While accurate at high Reynolds numbers, it does not have the capability to accurately model
stall and post stall conditions and has been shown have difficulty converging for eVTOL rotors
operating in hover [46,47]. High fidelity CFD solvers have also been shown to have difficulty
generating accurate aerodynamic parameters associated with sUAS aircraft. Kallstrom [46] applied
the RANS solver OVERFLOW to low Reynolds number flows and illustrated its limitations for
Mach numbers less 0.4. Russel and Sekula [47] used the helicopter comprehensive analysis tool
CAMRAD II to predict the performance of a single T-Motor Carbon Fiber 15x5 rotor and
generated lift and drag polars using XFOIL and the RANS solver FUN3D. They found the solution
using XFOIL generated polars to be less accurate than the ones generated using CFD. This was
attributed to large, separated flow regions that occur on the 2D blade sections with trailing edge
bluntness comparable to the overall maximum thickness. CFD calculations have also been shown
to produce different results for eVTOL based on the choice of turbulence model [35]. While the
flow regime and flight parameters particularly hover do pose challenges when generating the
9

necessary inputs for BEMT codes they have been successfully applied to rotors operating at low
Reynolds numbers [48–51]. Models of this type commonly employ the tip and hub loss correction
of Prandtl to account for losses of lift at the blade tip and hub [52]. Coriolis and centrifugal forces
caused by blade rotation have also been shown to impact the local aerodynamic parameters at the
inward most sections of a rotor blade which BEMT lacks the ability of modeling as no spanwise
flow is assumed. Dumitrescu and Cardos [53] developed a conceptual model based on observations
of the flow of a wind turbine operating at low tip speed ratios (≤0.3). They claim that the highly
three dimensional flow at the innermost parts of a rotating blade delays the onset of stall due to the
generation of additional circulation around the innermost blade sections that were followed by
spanwise circulation decay due to dissipation within the twisting boundary layer on the upper
surfaces of the inward blade sections [53]. This inboard stall delay phenomenon results in flow
that differs from that of a two-dimensional airfoil flow for which lift and drag polars are commonly
used for BEMT performance predictions. These effects are accounted for in BEMT models by
applying various empirical 3D correction factors to either the local angle of attack, two
dimensional lift and drag or normal force coefficients for wind turbines and sUAS [54–56]. Snell
[54] derived a 3D correction method assuming the rotor acts as a centrifugal pump on the separated
volume of air based on the effects of trailing-edge stall. This correction was modified and
implemented into early wind turbine design codes BLADEMODE and PHATAS with the addition
of the ‘local speed ratio’ dependency and provided better performance predictions when compared
to experimental results [57]. McCrink and Gregory [48] developed a Blade Element Momentum
(BEM) model with corrections for tip losses, Mach effects, three-dimensional flow components,
and Reynolds scaling. The BEM model was then applied and compared to experimental wind
tunnel measurements of three 10” diameter APC rotors of varying pitch equal to 5”, 7”, and 10”
operating at chord-based Reynolds numbers at 75% of the blade span on the order of 104~105. The
model including all corrections was found to be in excellent agreement with the measured results
with the most significant discrepancies occurring at low and high advance ratios.
Using a simple strip theory approach like BEMT, the tonal noise associated with blade
loading and blade thickness can be predicted using analytical noise prediction methods based either
in the frequency or time domains for a rotor in hover and forward flight [58–62]. The earliest
formulations for ordered rotational noise and noise due to blade thickness in static conditions are
those of Gutin [58] and Deming [59]:

These formulations along with that of Hanson [61] have been shown produce reasonable
predictions at the BPF and a few of the higher order harmonics but do not take into account
unsteady loading that can occur across the rotor disk due to asymmetric inflow, the ingestion of
turbulence or blade wake interaction. More recently, Ffowcs Williams and Hawkings developed
an acoustic analogy to include the effects of general surfaces and solid body motion by modifying
Lighthill’s acoustic analogy [63,64]. Generalized functions were used to re-arrange the NavierStokes equations to a form of the inhomogeneous wave equation which is known as the Ffowcs
Williams and Hawkings equation (FWH). This result showed that noise generated by an arbitrary
surface in motion is composed monopole and dipole sources distributed along its surface and a
quadrupole source distribution that encloses the surface. In practice, the quadrupole term is
computationally expensive and is usually neglected as it can be shown that it does not radiate
efficiently to the far-field which is where the observer is normally located during rotorcraft
operations. A solution to the FWH equation was developed shortly after and is known as Farassat’s
Formulation 1A (F1A). F1A provides a solution to noise generated by steady loading and thickness
as well as noise generated by unsteady loading in both the near and far field [62]. This formulation
has been implemented into rotor noise predictions codes such as NASA’s Aircraft Noise Prediction
Program (ANOPP) and PSUWOPWOP with the former using aerodynamic inputs computed using
a strip theory approach and the latter using impermeable blade surface loadings generated from
high fidelity CFD calculations [65,66].
Zawodny et al. [2] conducted hover acoustic and performance measurements of a 9.4 in
diameter DJI 9443 carbon fiber rotor and an 11 in diameter APC-SF rotor at NASA Langley’s
Structural Acoustic Loads and Transmission facility (SALT). The acoustic signature of each rotor
was shown to be composed of tonal noise at the BPF and associated harmonics along with a
considerable amount of mid and high frequency broadband noise. Rotor performance and acoustic
predictions of the tonal noise were also presented using the aerodynamic surface loadings
computed using the high-fidelity CFD program OVERFLOW2 as well as those computed using
ANOPP. Both acoustic solvers were based on Formulation 1A of Farassat. For these hover cases,
tonal noise at the BPF was found to be the frequency of most dominant tonal acoustic energy and
was well predicted [2]. Studies like this illustrate the ability that current computational or analytical
tools have in characterizing the most dominant frequency of tonal acoustic energy for isolated
sUAS rotors in hover, the BPF which is generated primarily by steady loading and thickness noise.
Using Steady RANS calculations for generation of 2D lift and drag polars yield slightly more
accurate results than those generated using 2D viscous panel methods [47]. Unsteady 3D RANS
rotor performance predictions have been shown to achieve slightly better accuracy in comparison
to those that incorporate a 2D strip theory approach and allow one to compute the unsteady air
loads that occur on the blades surfaces [67]. These predicted unsteady air loads can then be used
as inputs to F1A to predict the noise associated with unsteady loading. While F1A coupled with
blade surface loadings has been successfully applied to eVTOL rotors to predict the tonal noise of
due to blade loading and thickness it does neglect the random non-deterministic components of
TIN, BWI, or self-noise. Experimentally characterizing and numerically predicting these sources
of noise are much more difficult than that of the tonal component. TIN sources are primarily
responsible for the generation of narrowband random noise when turbulent eddies become
stretched, and a rotor blade successively cuts through the same piece of turbulence. Modeling of
this noise source requires the statistics of the rotor inflow to be known [68]. Broadband random
11

noise is generated by BWI and self-noise sources. BWI noise is caused by the rotor blade
interactions with rotor wake turbulence and causes noise at mid-frequencies [3]. This source of
noise becomes a major contributor to the radiated noise at static conditions and low speed ascent
[69]. While this work does not attempt to model and predict the noise associated with these sources,
it is considered in the experimentally measured results. Analyzing these sources experimentally is
difficult and has been shown to be facility and weather dependent [12,18,19]. The last form of
broadband random self-noise considered in this work is that due to the interaction between a rotorblade and the turbulence produced by the boundary layer in the near wake (i.e., boundary layer
dependent).
In Brooks, Pope, and Marcolini [23] five primary mechanisms were defined as the
responsible mechanisms for the generation of self-noise. Of these self-noise sources those
associated with the interaction of turbulence with the trailing edge have received the most attention
as it contributes significantly to noise from mid to high frequencies. Ffowcs Williams and Hall
[70] predicted TBL-TE noise by solving the volume integral of Curle’s equation (Eq. 2.11) directly
via a tailored Greens function. This tailored Greens function was used to describe a volume that
encloses a semi-infinite flat plate, thus eliminating the surface integral from Curle’s equation. The
assumption that an airfoil acts as a semi-infinite flat plate holds true for acoustic wavelengths
smaller than the chord of the airfoil. Computing the volume integral showed two important results:
that the directivity of trailing edge noise is cardioid based, and that the far field noise scales on
free stream velocity raised to the fifth power [70]. While Ffowcs Williams and Hall’s method is
effective, in practice, it is difficult to measure or compute the content of the volume integral. Amiet
took a surface dipole approach to solve Curle’s equation, in which he obtained the acoustic power
spectrum for the trailing edge noise via the Schwarzschild’s solution to determine the unsteady
surface pressures accounting for finite chord [71]. Shlinker and Amiet [72], adapted this method
by applying the necessary coordinate transformations to predict helicopter rotor trailing edge noise.
Although the predictions agreed with measurements, they estimated the surface pressure spectrum
by assuming a flat plate aerodynamic profile. Predictions were improved by using better estimates
of the true surface pressure spectra on the blades. Li and Lee [73] developed a rotor-trailing edge
noise prediction method based on the aerodynamics and the turbulence wall pressure spectrum
near the trailing edge on airfoils through a combination of BEMT, a viscous boundary layer panel
method, and the adverse pressure gradient wall pressure spectrum model of Lee [74]. Coordinate
transformations were implemented to include the motion of the rotor blades such as blade twist,
azimuthal rotation, tilt motion, and flapping motion. These were combined with the model of
Shlinker and Amiet [72] to predict helicopter rotor trialing edge noise in hover. Through BEMT,
the local angles of attack and induced velocities were computed. These were then used as inputs
for viscous panel method calculations in XFOIL. Boundary layer parameters were then extracted
and used as inputs to the wall pressure spectrum model of Lee [74]. Using this newly developed
code named UCD-QuietFly, they analyzed the effect of rotor blade design parameters and
operating conditions [73]. Li and Lee [75], applied the UCD-QuietFly codes to predict the trailing
edge noise of multirotor vehicle concepts intended for urban air mobility applications. They
showed that the trailing edge noise increases with increasing number of rotor blades. The trailing
edge noise of UAM multirotor vehicle concepts was compared to that of conventional helicopters
and that of the background noise. This comparison showed benefits of these multi rotor vehicle
concepts when compared to conventional rotorcraft equipped with single rotors in terms of
amplitude modulation and annoyance. Trailing edge noise was also shown to have increased
importance and exceed the levels of the background noise especially during takeoff and landing.
12

While these studies focus only on the most dominant source of self-noise which is that of
the interaction of the turbulent boundary layer interaction with the trailing edges of the rotor blades,
they neglect the other four self-noise sources. These models are based on first principle acoustic
scattering methods. A well-known airfoil self-noise prediction model is that of Brooks, Pope, and
Marcolini [23]. This method is semi-empirical and was developed based on NACA 0012 airfoil
noise data and is widely used in the rotorcraft community. Brooks and Burley [76] applied the
BPM model to a single BO-105 main rotor model. Their predictions matched well with the
measured noise spectrum and showed noise sources associated with BWI and self-noise to be most
dominant from mid to high frequencies. Zawodny et al. [2] applied the BPM model to a 9.4 in
diameter DJI 9443 carbon fiber rotor at static conditions and showed the self-noise to be dominated
by sources associated with boundary layer separation from 1 kHz to 10 kHz. Above 10 kHz
bluntness vortex noise was found to dominate the broadband noise spectrum. Pettingill and
Zawodny [25] also applied the BPM model to NASA’s SUI endurance quadcopter which is
equipped with four 15-inch diameter and 5-inch pitch T-Motor carbon fiber rotors. Noise at mid
to high frequencies was shown to be dominated by self-noise sources and to significantly increase
with the addition of multiple rotors. The increased broadband noise with the addition of multiple
rotors was attributed to each rotor interacting with the wake generated by other rotors. Jung et al.
[77] applied the BPM model to the same DJI 9443 carbon fiber rotor as Zawodny et al. [2].
Boundary layer parameters were computed using the comprehensive analysis tool FLIGHTLAB
or RANS methods. Comparisons of self-noise predictions made using inputs generated from 3D
solutions were found to be less than that of the measured from 1 kHz to 3 kHz. In comparison,
inputs generated from a 2D solution were shown to be in better agreement with the measured over
this same range of frequency. Displacement thickness which is an input to the BPM model were
also compared for the DJI 9443 rotor and a rotor of the same diameter consisting of NACA 0012
profiles and were found to be similar along the span [77]. An unsteady 3D hybrid RANS/LES
model was also coupled with the BPM model and predictions of broadband noise were shown to
be in better agreement between 2×BPF to 3 kHz indicating the main contribution of mid frequency
noise is due to unsteady loading due to blade wake interaction in hover. This also agrees with that
of the study of Brooks et al. [3] who showed that BWI is responsible for broadband noise over
mid frequencies for a 40 percent scaled BO-105 main rotor in hover.
While the BPM model and that of Li and Lee [75] have been applied to sUAS rotors, the
operating conditions that they have been applied to are currently limited and must be further
assessed. These models are very sensitive to the required inputs which are commonly computed
from 2D blade sections which assumes no aerodynamic interactions occur between sections.
Existing RANS methods and viscous panel methods like XFOIL model these inputs poorly due to
the fact that these rotors operate in the transitional flow regime. Tonal noise associated with blade
steady loading and thickness has been shown to be modeled effectively using simple strip theory
approaches and F1A, while empirical and analytical methods for modeling the broadband selfnoise have been shown to produce less accurate results. Given this, the capabilities of these existing
methods need to be assessed when applied to eVTOL aircraft. As of now, the complexities
associated with modeling the aerodynamic sources of noise have been discussed. The additional
source of noise associated with this new class of electric aircraft and is assessed in this works is
the noise associated with the electric drive system.
The electric drive system consists of an electronic speed controller (ESC) and a brushless DC
motor. The type of ESC used to drive the motor has been shown to influence the radiated noise.
Henderson et al. [30] measured the noise of a DJI 2212 and a DJ2312 brushless DC outrunner
13

motor. Electric motor noise was also measured using three different ESC’s where one drove the
motor with a pulse width modulated sine wave-like current and the other two provided a pulse
width modulated square wave current to the motor. The sine wave controller was shown to produce
more tones on the acoustic spectrum that were not present when driving the electric motor with a
DJI 9443 rotor installed. The switching frequency of the ESC has also been shown to have an
effect on the electric motor noise [78]. The noise generated by the electric motor is generated by
electromagnetic and mechanical sources. Electromagnetic sources arise from the forces that
develop within the airgap in between the stator and motor rotor. This electromagnetic field contains
many harmonics, thus producing many discrete tones. These tones have all been shown to be
directly related to the motor RPM, thus directly related to the fundamental line frequency of the
supply current. When these tones align with those of aerodynamic sources increased noise is the
result [30]. Mechanical sources associated with the electric motor are due to resonance of the
electric motor structure. Cluts et al. [79] performed surface vibration measurements of two small
scale sUAS outrunner brushless DC motors and showed acoustic tones that were not generated
aerodynamically to align with those associated the motor rotor surface vibrations.
1.3.2 Limitations and Advantages of Common Facilities Used to Measure eVTOL Rotor
Noise
Existing rotor performance and prediction methods are validated by quality aerodynamic
and acoustic measurements of isolated rotors and full-scale vehicles. The measurements are
commonly performed in wind tunnels, anechoic chambers and outdoor facilities [24,29,34,80,81].
While wind tunnels such as NASA Langley’s LSAWT allow for both aerodynamic and
aeroacoustic testing of isolated eVTOL rotors, eVTOL rotor-airframe interactions, and full scale
eVTOL vehicles in hover and forward flight they lack the ability to simulate a true hover condition.
This inability is due to the induced flow in the wind tunnel test section by the thrusting rotor and
the lack of inflow turbulence [80]. The lack of inflow turbulence could result in an inaccurate
assessment of the acoustic signature at the higher order harmonics of the BPF as well as those from
self-noise sources.
This study focuses primarily on experimental measurements conducted in three laboratory
environments: anechoic chambers, anechoic open jet wind tunnels, and outdoor facilities. Laboratory
environments provide an opportunity to study the noise from these platforms at conditions in which
free flight would be unsustainable while acquiring large amounts of data in a cost and time
effective way, often not possible in field measurements. In anechoic chambers, a high signal to
noise ratio is achieved and the environmental conditions remain consistent. However, in static
conditions, accurate free-flight relevant measurement of tonal and broadband acoustic sources can
be difficult due to recirculation effects. Recirculation occurs when operating a rotor in an enclosed
facility. In this case, the turbulent downwash created by a thrusting rotor is re-ingested. This can
lead to an unreliable characterization of the noise as this ingestion of the rotor’s own turbulent
wake does not occur in an open environment. To the authors knowledge one of the earliest studies
documenting the effects of recirculation was conducted in [82], where a four bladed rotor in hover
was tested in an enclosed facility. Here the authors identified large random variations in mean
thrust and torque throughout each measurement. These results were also accompanied with smoke
flow visualizations of the rotor’s wake. These visualizations showed large scale turbulent
structures to become re-ingested by the rotor and were the cause of the large fluctuations in mean
thrust and torque that were observed. A large honeycomb sheet was installed circumferentially
14

around the rotor disk which was effective at reducing the large-scale turbulent structures that
initially were re-ingested by the rotor, leading to an accurate characterization of rotor performance.
Stephenson et al. [18] performed far field acoustic measurements of a two bladed, fixed
pitch, 9.4 in diameter rotor at hover conditions in NASA Langley Research Center’s Small Hover
Anechoic Chamber (SHAC). This facility measures 12 ft x 8.4 ft by 10.7 ft (L×W×H) from wedge
tip to wedge tip and the rotor was operated at 5200 RPM yielding an average thrust of 0.75 lb.
Spectrograms showed higher order harmonics of the BPF to suddenly appear within the acoustic
spectrum shortly after the rotor reached its steady state. Narrow band acoustic spectra for an
observer located well below the rotor plane showed harmonics above 3×BPF to increase more
than 15 dB once recirculation had established. This increased noise at higher order harmonics of
the BPF was not present for an observer located in the rotor plane. Additionally, these narrow band
spectra showed the first harmonic of the blade passage frequency to be relatively unaffected at all
microphone positions. They concluded that the increased noise is due to the rotor’s turbulent wake
becoming re-ingested. This increased higher order harmonic content for an observer located well
below the rotor plane is indicative of noise generated by unsteady loads that become correlated
across the rotor disk, which is specific to the non-deterministic narrowband source, TIN. Weitsman
et al. [83] conducted experimental measurements to identify the effects of flow recirculation on
the dynamic and acoustic emissions of an isolated rotor, operating in hover in a sealed anechoic
chamber. Recirculation was shown to be an artifact of testing rotary wing systems in an enclosed
environment. Recirculation was shown to occur shortly after the rotor reached its commanded
RPM. Placing two varying turbulence suppression screens downstream at multiple distances was
shown to delay the onset of recirculation and increase acquisition times [83]. Whiteside et al. [19]
conducted performance and acoustic measurements of two 6.25 in three-bladed stacked rotor
configurations in the same facility as Stephenson. The onset of recirculation was also delayed
which led to increased acquisition times by placing two screens with 51% and 35% solidity five
and nine rotor radii below the rotor plane [19]. In both cases, this increase in acquisition time
reduces the spectral uncertainties and allows for a more accurate characterization of the acoustic
signature of a rotor in hover. Nardari et al. [84] utilized the Lattice-Boltzmann method to model
the noise and performance of a lightly loaded rotor operating at hover condition in an enclosed and
open-air environment. Instantaneous snapshots of the flow field encapsulating the rotor showed a
significant amount of vortical structures to be re-ingested by the rotor for simulations conducted
when the environment was confined, therefore increasing the unsteady blade loading, thus the
emission of unsteady loading noise of the rotor [84].
Anechoic open jets provide control of the inflow conditions allowing the performance and
noise isolated rotors to be studied at multiple advance ratios and inflow angles. The effects of wake
reingestion are also mitigated as the wake is forced outside of the test section with the addition of
inflow. However, at static conditions the turbulent wake produced by the thrusting rotor can
become re-ingested. Recently, Whelchel et al. [85] showed that broadband sources associated with
BWI or TIN could be mitigated by forcing minimal inflow into the rotor disk without reducing
aerodynamic performance. They showed that the noise at the higher order harmonics was due to
these broadband sources alone [85]. However, testing of larger rotors within these facilities is
limited by the size of the open jet inlet. At this time it is not clear if the results of small-scale
experiments can be extrapolated to larger scale rotors that represent that of full scale vehicles [1].
Outdoor environments such as the Virginia Tech Drone Park eliminate confinement effects that
are a feature of anechoic chambers. In these facilities, the only boundary is the ground and testing
of larger rotors and full-scale vehicles in flight becomes feasible. However, environmental factors,
15

ever changing background noise levels, and constructive and destructive interference of the
radiated acoustic waves can occur from ground reflections [86]. Rotor interactions with unsteady
wind gust and turbulence result in unsteady loads across the rotor disk causing changes in the
measured noise and place limitations on repeatability. Rotational rates can also fluctuate due to
changes in wind loading or control causing variations in tonal content [12]. Changes in wind speed
and direction also influence the radiated broadband noise by subjecting a rotor to edgewise flow
[24].
Overall, methods to measure eVTOL rotor noise are not well defined and the effects each
facility has on the measured noise and aerodynamic performance is not well understood. Analyzing
the characteristics of the radiated noise is complicated by the broadband noise sources that are
artifacts of testing in each of the listed facilities. This makes comparison of experimental results
difficult and computational models struggle to compare well particularly at static conditions.
Experimentally measuring the noise outdoors is highly environmentally dependent and at low
frequencies could become corrupted by the background noise. In indoor facilities recirculation
occurs leading to an inaccurate characterization of the radiated noise. These facility effects are
currently not accounted for in computational models. Understanding these effects aids in the
analysis of the noise and provides explanations as to why computational models may not compare
well.
1.3.3 Objectives
A recent NASA report by Rizzi et al. [1] presents a set of high level goals for addressing
barriers with regards to noise that may impede the integration of this new class of electric aircraft
into the existing transportation system. They also discuss the current practice, gaps in the current
practice when applied to eVTOL aircraft noise and make recommendations to address the gaps to
achieve the high-level goals. While six high-level goals were presented, two that are directly
related to this work are: 1) Assess current rotor noise prediction capabilities when applied to
eVTOL aircraft, 2) Define measurement methods/procedures to support noise regulations and
assessment of community impacts. As this new class of electric aircraft is being developed, the
application of conventional rotor noise modeling tools could produce an inaccurate
characterization of the total vehicle noise. The authors also indicate the need for the definition of
best practices to experimentally measure eVTOL rotor noise to ensure quality experimental data
sets that can be used to validate tools to model the noise sources associated with these aircraft.
Measurements of eVTOL rotor noise produce varying results across multiple facilities and
are complicated by broadband noise sources. This makes fair comparison of experimental data to
computational results difficult. This direct comparison is not only needed for validation of existing
computational tools used to predict eVTOL rotor noise but also to infer the sources that are
responsible for the generation of noise itself. Which leads to the objectives of this work:
1. Assess the character of the noise of a hexacopter in hover and forward flight
2. Assess the character of the noise of commercially available eVTOL rotors in multiple
facilities and analyze the effects each facility has on aerodynamic performance and
noise

16

3. Implement a low order tonal and broadband noise prediction method to predict the tonal
noise associated with steady loading and blade thickness and broadband noise
associated with self-noise
4. Compare predictions using the low order method of each noise component to that of
the tonal and broadband components that have been separated from the total acoustic
pressure time history using a phase averaging interpolation technique
5. Analyze the noise associated with an electric drive system and investigate the impacts
of an ESC and the ESC switching frequency on the high frequency noise
In the following chapters these objectives are addressed. Starting with Chapter 2, the
fundamental theories of rotor noise and aerodynamic performance are discussed and followed by
a discussion of the methods used to model the aerodynamic performance and noise of a rotor. In
Chapter 3, an overview is given of the facility and apparatus and instrumentation used to measure
the noise of a hexacopter in hover and forward flight. This is followed by an overview of all
facilities and apparatus and instrumentation used to measure the noise of isolated eVTOL rotors
in this work. Moving forward to Chapter 4, the total noise of a hexacopter in forward flight is
analyzed and discussed. After this the noise from the total vehicle will be decomposed by analyzing
that of a single isolated rotor which leads to Chapter 5. First the effects of facilities used to
commonly measure eVTOL rotor performance and noise are discussed which is followed by the
analysis of the character of the radiated noise of eVTOL rotors operating under controlled inflow
conditions and at yaw. Here the measured noise of eVTOL rotors is separated into tonal and
broadband components and compared to predictions of rotor performance and noise using the
methods outlined in Chapter 2. This is then followed by a discussion of a newly designed low
noise rotor and comparisons are then made between the radiated noise and aerodynamic
performance of a commercially available rotor. This is followed by Chapter 6, which is an analysis
of the noise associated with the electric drive system and the effects the electronic speed controllers
and their switching rates have on the measured aerodynamic noise. Lastly, in Chapter 7,
conclusions are made on all findings within this work.

17

Chapter 2 Rotor Noise
This chapter introduces the fundamental principles of aeroacoustics and how they are
applied to rotorcraft noise prediction. This chapter begins by outlining the acoustic analogy which
is followed by a theoretical description of how noise is generated by flow which is then followed
by a description of noise generated by a moving surface. Lastly, this chapter concludes by
describing the methods used to predict eVTOL rotor noise in the current work.

2.1 The Acoustic Analogy
The generation and propagation of sound in a stationary medium where the time averaged
properties are uniform throughout the region of interest for an observer located at 𝒚 and time 𝝉 can
be described by the linear acoustic wave equation as:
1 𝜕 2 𝑝′ (𝒚, 𝜏) 𝜕 2 𝑝′ (𝒚, 𝜏)
−

This result provides a solution to the Lighthill’s wave equation. The only restrictions are that the
surfaces bounding the fluid are stationary and the medium is at rest outside the region of turbulent
flow. This solution results in 4 source terms that depend on the rate of change of mass flux on the
surface ((𝑛𝑗 𝜕(𝜌𝑣𝑗 )⁄𝜕𝜏), the force per unit area on the fluid by the surface (𝑝𝑖𝑗 𝑛𝑗 ), the momentum
flux across the surface (𝜌𝑣𝑖 𝑣𝑗 ), and the contribution from Lighthill’s stress tensor 𝑇𝑖𝑗 (𝒚, 𝜏) .
The formulation of Lighthill shows that the sources producing sound from a turbulent flow
can be described as a set of quadrupoles (volume integral) and a set of dipoles (surface integral).
Whereas, the solution provided using Curles theorem results in three source terms that are
responsible for generating sound within the flow and consists of a monopole, surface dipole, and
a quadrupole term. These terms can be simplified for an observer located in the acoustic farfield
(𝑘𝑟 ≫ 1) where 𝑘 = 𝜔/𝑐𝑜 . Looking at the first term, the monopole, for a surface on which the
fluid moves with a normal velocity 𝑣𝑗 𝑛𝑗 = 𝑢𝑛 sound is generated by the flux of mass across a
surface which is determined by the local density and the motion on the surface. If the surface is
impenetrable and stationary no sound is generated. Lastly, if the surface is sufficiently small,
retarded time effects can be ignored and the source can be considered acoustically compact, 𝑘𝐿 ≪
1. Where, 𝐿 is a characteristic length such as a chord of an airfoil. Assuming acoustic compactness,
the monopole term can be expressed as:

this shows that the sound radiation from a vibrating surface of arbitrary shape is the same as the
sound radiation from a spherical surface with the same net rate of mass flux injection. The second

2.1.1 The Ffowcs Williams – Hawkings Equation and Its Solution
While Lighthill’s analogy provides a solution for flow generated sound and for sound
scattered by stationary objects for an observer located in a stationary medium it does not account
for the effects of moving surfaces within the flow. In applications where the surfaces are moving
such as a rotor blade, the analysis needs to be modified to account for this motion. Ffowcs Williams
and Hawking (1968) derived an analogous inhomogeneous wave equation to describe the sound
generated by turbulence in the presence of an arbitrary moving surface. The FWH equation
provides an extension of Lighthill’s acoustic analogy by extending the concept of an ordinary
21

derivative to discontinuous functions. First consider a function 𝑓(𝒙, 𝑡) that defines a surface
bounding a volume such as a rotor in a stream tube as shown in Figure 2.1. Where, 𝑓 = 0 on the
surface of the rotor blades, 𝑓 < 0 inside the rotor blades, 𝑆𝑜 , and 𝑓 > 0 anywhere in the flow.

Figure 2.1 – Function 𝑓 that defines a surface bounding a rotor
Differentiation of the surface function, 𝑓, with respect to time and space yields

(2.16)

within the flow outside of the surface 𝑆𝑜 , 𝐻𝑠 (𝑓) = 1. Using the concept of generalized derivatives,
the time and space derivatives of the flow property, 𝜙 = 𝜙𝑜 + 𝜙′𝐻𝑠 (𝑓), are then defined as:

and the divergence of the momentum and subtracting the latter from the former. Lastly,

(2.18)

Equation 2.18 consists of three terms on the right-hand side that represent different rotor noise
sources. The first term is a mass flux term and is due to body thickness, the second term is a loading
noise source and is resultant from the steady and unsteady forces on the surface, and the last term
is a quadrupole term resulting from turbulence within the flow itself. These two additional terms,
the first and second, that were not present in Lighthill’s acoustic analogy only exist on 𝑓 = 0, i.e.
the surface. In low Mach number applications, noise due to surface loading and blade thickness
radiates much more efficiently than noise generated by turbulence within the flow, thus the last
term can often be neglected [87]. Rotors can be represented by a set of moving surfaces, thus the
Ffowcs Williams and Hawking (FWH) solution provides the basis of theory to understand rotor
noise. To solve the FWH equation, the free space Green’s function can be used to find an integral
representation of the solution to get an analytical solution. Using the properties of the generalized
functions, shifting the spatial derivatives to time derivatives, and the time derivatives from source
time, 𝜏, to observer time, 𝑡, the acoustic pressure for an observer located at 𝒙 is:
