2.1 Rotor Noise Sources
2.1.1 Discrete Frequency Noise
Discrete frequency noise (a.k.a. deterministic, rotational, or harmonic noise) refers to tonal
noise, represented by the blade passage frequency (BPF) and its harmonics. This deterministic
source can be decomposed into thickness, periodic, and steady loading noise. These sources are
computed using the monopole and dipole terms of the FW-H equation or Farasat’s Formulation
1A (F1A). Thickness noise, generated by the repetitive displacement of fluid, can be computed
using only the surface motion and geometry and radiates primarily in the rotor plane. Noise
generated by aerodynamic loading on the blade surfaces is commonly referred to as loading noise
and radiates primarily out of plane [66]. Loading on the blade surfaces can be steady and unsteady.
Deterministic tonal noise due to unsteady loading on the blade surfaces is caused by a variety of
sources which include periodic response to non-uniform inflow, blade vortex interaction (BVI),
and rotor-rotor or rotor-airframe interactions [10]. A non-axisymmetric mean inflow varies the
effective mean velocity and sectional angle of attack experienced by a blade during rotation. This
fluctuating blade load and resultant noise can be created when a rotor is yawed to the free stream
(e.g. rotorcraft in forward flight) or if a rotor is operating in a wake deficit from an upstream body
(e.g. fuselage, airframe component, etc.). Although the effect of a circumferential non-uniformity
24

in the sectional flow, the interaction is still periodic. Thus, all of the spectral content is tonal and
results in stronger higher order harmonics of the BPF than if the loading were steady [90]. BVI
noise is caused by a blade-tip vortex interacting with a following blade. While BVI noise does not
occur throughout all phases of flight, it becomes important during descent as the rotor is forced to
operate within its own wake. When these interactions occur BVI noise becomes a dominant source
and is highly annoying [27]. As stated previously, eVTOL aircraft are equipped with multiple
rotors with small tip to tip spacing that are in some cases mounted close to airframe components.
This small tip to tip spacing can result in strong periodic aerodynamic interactions, thus generating
additional tonal noise [5]. Additionally, like the effect of asymmetry in the inflow, the periodic
interaction of a rotor with an airframe component has been shown to produce additional tonal noise
at higher order harmonics of the BPF [6].
2.1.2 Broadband Noise
By definition, broadband noise (a.k.a. stochastic or non-deterministic noise) encompasses
a wide range of frequencies. Broadband noise is caused by turbulence induced surface pressure
fluctuations. These can be turbulence generated by external sources to the rotor or by viscous
interactions on the surface of the blade. As the length and time scales of turbulence vary, the
spectral features that are produced are broad [87]. Broadband noise due to interaction with ingested
turbulence from the operating environment can be predicted using the surface integration of the
loading/dipole term of Farasat’s Formulation 1A and has thus similar directivity as that of
deterministic loading noise sources. F1A can be used to predict broadband noise but requires the
surface loading to be resolved with sufficiently high spatial and temporal resolution. This is
difficult to know experimentally. It can be achieved through high fidelity computational fluid
dynamics such as Large Eddy Simuation (LES) or direct numerical simulation (DNS), but this is
often impractical due to its high computational expense. This cost increases with increasing
Reynolds number. Therefore, predictions of broadband noise are typically conducted separately
from discrete frequency loading noise using semi-empirical models like presented in Pegg [91].
Pegg computes the 1/3 octave band sound pressure level (𝑆𝑃𝐿1/3 ) spectrum using input parameters
based on static flight conditions and general rotor characteristics which are thrust, radius, total
projected blade area, blade-tip speed, and average blade lift coefficient. The Pegg model assumes
that the total broadband noise is dominated by turbulence ingestion noise (TIN), but also considers
vortex noise, tip vortex formation noise, and boundary layer noise [91]. This model is calibrated
to helicopter flight test data and only predicts the total broadband noise, thus lacking the ability to
decompose the noise contributions from the different broadband noise sources. Application of this
model to eVTOL aircraft may not be accurate. eVTOL aircraft are configured with multiple rotors
with smaller diameter and lower operational tip speeds, therefore the broadband noise is expected
to deviate substantially from that of conventional rotorcraft.
The most widely used broadband airfoil self-noise model is the Brooks, Pope, and
Marcolini (BPM) model [23]. This model is based on the Strouhal number scaling of analytically
derived similarity spectra, which are then correlated to measured airfoil boundary layer
characteristics for a NACA 0012 airfoil in a uniform freestream [10]. In contrast to the Pegg model,
the BPM model allows for decomposition of each noise source associated with blade self-noise,
but it does not address other sources like TIN. These self-noise sources are shown in Figure 2.2
and consist of turbulent boundary layer trailing edge (TBL-TE) noise, laminar boundary layer
vortex shedding (LBL-VS) noise, separation-stall noise, trailing edge bluntness vortex shedding
noise, and tip vortex formation noise.
25

Figure 2.2 – Airfoil self-noise sources accounted for by the BPM model [23]
For a given steady inflow condition, the BPM model computes 𝑆𝑃𝐿1/3 spectrum based on
scaling law theories derived from wind tunnel experiments of a NACA 0012 airfoil in a steady
uniform free stream, for Reynolds numbers from 4 ×104 up to 1.5×106, Mach numbers up to 0.21,
and angles of attack from 0° to 25.2° [23]. While the BPM model was initially developed for
airfoils in steady flow, it is widely used for modeling the self-noise associated with rotorcraft by
considering individual blade sections as noise sources [92]. Brooks and Burley [76] performed
coordinate transformations that allowed for the noise sources to be localized on the rotor blades
by including the rotor tilt angle, sectional radius, sectional pitch, and blade azimuthal angle. The
BPM model is typically applied to rotors using blade element analysis with a quasi-steady
assumption. Under this quasi-steady assumption, the BPM model computes 𝑆𝑃𝐿1/3 spectrum for
each blade segment at each instance in time assuming that the generated noise is equivalent to that
generated by an airfoil in an equivalent steady flow condition. The rotor total self-noise is then
computed, assuming the noise generated from each blade section is incoherent, by summing the
mean squared pressures generated by each blade section.
Empirical methods for TIN are typically unreliable for use in a broad range of applications
and operational environments. TIN is produced as the rotor blades interact with turbulence. This
creates unsteady loading on the blades surfaces which radiate as sound [93]. Particularly for
eVTOL aircraft, TIN is expected to arise from the ingestion of atmospheric turbulence and
turbulence generated by each rotor’s wake. At static conditions, the ingested turbulence becomes
extremely anisotropic due to the strong streamtube contraction [17]. This stretching of turbulence
can also result in correlated blade loading which can occur as rotor blades pass successively
through the same turbulent eddy. Mathematically speaking correlated blade loading occurs when
the axial time scales of turbulence are significantly larger than the time scale of a blade-to-blade
passage [20]. This results in “haystacking” in the noise spectrum which in some cases appears
tonal while the source is still fundamentally broadband. In edgewise flight, the mean flow into a
rotor becomes non-axial and the turbulent structures become less distorted, thus producing broad
spectral features [21]. Rotors on eVTOL aircraft not only ingest atmospheric turbulence, but also
wake turbulence that is shed from other rotors or airframe components. In comparison to
atmospheric turbulence, rotor wakes are non-axisymmetric and form in a helical pattern. The
turbulence within the shed wake is inhomogeneous, non-stationary, and anisotropic [10]. Wake
reingestion has also been shown to occur when conducting noise measurements of small eVTOL
rotors in enclosed facilities [18,19]. This reingestion of a rotor’s own turbulent wake produces
additional noise at the higher order harmonics of the BPF. While TIN is expected to be important
26

for these aircraft it is not the focus of this work but will be considered in the analysis of
experimental data sets of rotors operating in confined volumes. For accurate predictions of TIN,
inflow statistics are needed which are unavailable in field measurements and difficult to obtain in
enclosed static environments. TIN can also be predicted using various curve fits or analytically
derived inflow models, but again these can be applied less broadly then the self-noise prediction
methods [68,94].
While many noise sources are responsible for the generation of broadband noise, this work
considers the broadband noise associated with blade self-noise in particular. For eVTOL aircraft,
the broadband noise has been shown to be significant above 1 kHz and this is significant
specifically with respect to the sensitivity of human’s ears [2,6,25]. Intaratep et al. [29],
experimentally measured the noise of a DJI Phantom 3 quadcopter and showed that the broadband
noise dominates from mid to high frequencies and increases significantly from single to multiple
rotors. Recently, Li and Lee [73], combined Blade Element Momentum Theory (BEMT), XFOIL,
Lee’s wall pressure spectrum model [74], and Amiet’s edge noise prediction method [71] to predict
helicopter trailing edge noise in hover. Like the BPM model, coordinate transformations were
performed to account for the changing observer perspective of each blade section during the rotor
rotation. These transformations localized the noise sources at the trailing edge of each blade section
and account for blade twist angle, collective pitch angle, sectional radius, and rotor tilt angle. The
local sectional velocities were obtained using BEMT, which is essential for calculation of the
sectional angle of attack. The required boundary layer inputs to Lee’s wall pressure spectrum
model were obtained using XFOIL. Lee’s wall pressure spectrum model was used as it has been
shown to provide more accurate results for adverse pressure gradient flows [74]. Through Lee’s
code, named UCD-QuietFly, Li and Lee [75], predicted trailing edge noise for different urban air
mobility (UAM) concepts. UAM multirotor vehicle broadband noise was shown to be significant
and of large concern in residential areas during take-off or landing when compared to the ambient
background noise.
2.2 Aerodynamic Modeling and Noise Prediction
2.2.1 Rotor Performance
Rotor aerodynamics are well defined for nominal operating conditions in axial flow. For a
rotor operating in airflow normal to the propeller disk, a net thrust force and a net aerodynamic
torque is produced which act along the axis of rotation. Each of the individual blades can be
thought of as a set of rotating wings in which each produce a lift force per section of span that acts
perpendicular to the sectional inflow velocity vector and a drag force parallel to it [42]. The lift
forces acting on each blade section can then be summed to yield the net thrust, 𝑇. The summed
drag forces acting on each blade section result in a net moment that acts in the disk plane opposite
to the direction of rotation to yield the rotors aerodynamic torque, 𝑄. Traditionally, rotor
performance data are represented as non-dimensional quantities which are coefficients of
thrust 𝐶𝑇 , torque 𝐶𝑄 , and power 𝐶𝑃 as shown:

power imparted on the fluid by the rotor. Through dimensional analysis it can be shown that the
thrust and torque coefficients are a function of advance ratio 𝐽, Reynolds number 𝑅𝑒 , and tip Mach
number 𝑀𝑡𝑖𝑝 for a given rotor design [42]. Thus, the complete influence of operating condition
and scale are not completely captured by the coefficients listed in Equation 2.23. The advance
ratio, 𝐽, relates the linear distance travelled by the propeller per revolution and is defined as:

assumption that viscous and compressibility effects are negligible. The Reynolds number is a
dimensionless quantity corresponding to the ratio of inertial to viscous forces within a flow. For
full scale aircraft propellers and helicopter rotors, local Reynolds number effects are minimal and
can generally be neglected [42]. For smaller rotors like those of eVTOL aircraft, the Reynolds
number is lower increasing the importance of the viscous effects [2]. Given this, a thicker boundary
layer develops on the rotor surface and the flow can separate degrading performance [40]. In this
work the local rotor blade Reynolds number is defined as:

inertial forces to those associated with the forces related to the compressibility of the fluid. For
eVTOL aircraft, the rotor tip Mach number generally remains low (𝑀𝑡𝑖𝑝 < 0.3) allowing one to
neglect the effects of compressibility. Propulsive efficiency 𝜂𝑝 is defined as the ratio of usable
output power (thrust power) to supplied power (torque power). Usable output power is defined
as 𝑃𝑜𝑢𝑡 = 𝑇𝑉∞ and power input as 𝑃𝑖𝑛 = 2𝜋𝑛𝑄. Thus, 𝜂𝑝 is defined as:

2.2.2 Aerodynamic Modeling
In this work, the thrust, 𝑇 and torque, 𝑄 acting on a propeller are modeled using a blade
element momentum theory (BEMT) model. The forces acting on each of the propellers were
determined from discretizing each propeller blade into 𝑁 individual elements of width 𝛿𝑟 along
the span. A basic stream tube encasing a propeller rotating at Ω rad/s with a single blade element
located at the spanwise location 𝑟𝑒𝑙 , is shown in Figure 2.3. The forces acting on this single blade
element are shown in Figure 2.4.

Figure 2.3 - Stream tube encasing the 9450S rotor

Consider the single element of width 𝛿𝑟, length of chord 𝑐, at the radial location 𝑟𝑒𝑙 . This single
element moves azimuthally in the plane of rotation at the velocity 𝛺𝑟, whereas the fluid
surrounding the element is rotating in the same plane at the velocity 𝑎1 Ω. The in-plane velocity of
the element relative to the fluid is thus 𝑈𝑇 = Ω𝑟𝑒𝑙 (1 − 𝑎1 ). It can also be shown through a
momentum balance over the stream tube shown in Figure 2.3 for a propeller translating at a

and 𝑎1 are defined as the axial and radial induction factors. The magnitudes and direction of these
induced velocity components vary along the span of the propeller blade as a function of the
propeller’s aerodynamic, geometric, and inflow conditions. This variation leads to a non-existent
closed form solution for the axial and radial inflow factors, thus an iterative approach must be used
to approximate them over small discrete blade sections. 𝑎𝑜 and 𝑎1 can be derived by equating the
local aerodynamic forces acting on a blade with the net momentum transfer of the blade to the
stream tube surrounding the propeller resulting in the elemental thrust and torque per unit span as

[𝑟𝑜 𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜃𝑜 𝑟𝑜 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜃𝑜 𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜙𝑜 𝑟𝑜 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜃𝑜 ], where the angle 𝜙𝑜 is measured azimuthally from the
positive 𝑥2 axis and 𝜃𝑜 is measured from the positive 𝑥1 axis. Assuming acoustic compactness
along the chord of each blade section allows one to simplify the integral of pressure sources in the
chordwise direction. Therefore, the area integral can be reduced to a spanwise integration and the
gauge pressure 𝑝, can be replaced with the sectional thrust and torque acting on the 𝑘 𝑡ℎ blade
section. Given this, the steady loading term can be decomposed into simplified near field and far
field terms. The far-field term can be decomposed as:

2.2.4 Rotor Self Noise
This work implements the BPM method using the BEMT model and a quasi-steady
assumption, where the noise is computed for different blade elements, with the noise from each
blade section at any instance in time being assumed to be equivalent to the noise generated by an
airfoil in the same steady inflow condition. The noise from each blade section is assumed to be
incoherent so the summation of the mean square pressures of each individual section is performed
to compute the total rotor broadband noise. The global (𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , 𝑥3) and local (𝑧1 , 𝑧2 , 𝑧3 ) coordinate
systems for these predictions are shown in Figure 2.6, where a self-noise source is located at the
mid span of the trailing edge of a single blade element 𝑦𝑖 = [0 𝑟𝑒𝑙 cos 𝜙 𝑟𝑟𝑒𝑙 sin 𝜙] travels
around the rotational axis 𝑥1 at a velocity 𝑣𝑖 = [0 −Ω𝑟𝑒𝑙 sin𝜙 Ω𝑟𝑒𝑙 𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜙]. The noise is heard
by an observer at 𝑥𝑖 located at an angle 𝜃𝑜 below the 𝑥2 axis. For measurements and predictions
in this work the microphones were located in the 𝑥1 ,𝑥3 plane as shown in the top view diagram,
so 𝑥𝑖 = [−𝑟𝑜𝑏𝑠 sin 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 0 −𝑟𝑜𝑏𝑠 cos 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ]. The power spectral density for the 𝑖𝑡ℎ blade section
changes with the azimuthal angle 𝜙, therefore they are azimuthally averaged as shown in [73]:

Chapter 3 Apparatus and Instrumentation
3.1 Noise of a Hexacopter in Hover and Forward Flight Study
3.1.1 Drone
A DJI Matrice 600 Pro was used for all flyover measurements. This is a popular
recreational and industrial drone platform. The Matrice 600 Pro, shown in Figure 3.1, is a
hexacopter with six 2- bladed folding 533.4 mm diameter, DJI series 2170R rotors. These blades
have a pitch of 178 mm.

Figure 3.1 – DJI Matrice 600 Pro
The Matrice 600 Pro weighs 89.3 N and has a maximum take-off weight of 148.1 N. The maximum
forward flight speed and speeds of ascent and descent are 18 m/s, 5 m/s, and 3 m/s, respectively.
For free flight tests, the on-board DJI GPS logging module was time synchronized with the
acoustic ground instrumentation using a pulsing time synchronized signal triggered by the
registered GPS time. This GPS trigger was recorded on an additional channel of the microphone
data acquisition system and was produced by a Teensy 3.2 USB Board (Version: 3.2), a Sparkfun
Teensy 3.1 XBee Adapter (Model Number: BOB-13311), two XBee 1mW Wire Antennas - Series
1 (Model Number: WRL-08665), a SparkFun XBee Explorer USB (Model Number: WRL 11812),
an Adafruit Ultimate GPS Breakout (Model Number: ADA746), and a Pololu 6V 2200 mAH
NiMH Rechargeable Battery (Model Number: RB-Pol-160). This system allows the user to control
when the pulse signal is relayed to the data acquisition system. The time-stamps from the GPS onboard the vehicle are ultimately correlated with time stamps acquired with acoustic data on the
ground to synchronize location and noise data. The accuracy of the synchronization is limited by
the data recording rate of the drone which sampled flight statistics at 10Hz. The DJI GPS logging
module provides an altitude accuracy of ± 0.5 m and a horizontal accuracy of ± 1.5 m.
37

3.1.2 Virginia Tech Drone Park
The drone park is a purpose-built facility for research flights of sUAS. The netted facility
opened in April 2018 as the tallest netted drone park in the US. It has internal dimensions 91.4 m
long x 36.6 m wide x 25.9 m tall and is shown in Figure 3.2. This facility enables experimental
free-flight tests within a confined volume that is exposed to the surrounding environment. Acoustic
measurements are made in this facility using microphones located at the center of 1 m diameter by
1.3 cm thick HPVA maple plywood ground plates. Five ground plates are distributed directly
underneath the vehicle’s flight path and at lateral receiver locations as shown in Figure 3.3 and
Figure 3.4. The microphone plates were manufactured to the size of 1 m diameter to be in
accordance with the International Standard IEC 61400-11 for making acoustic measurements of
wind turbines. The five microphones are each shielded from environmental disturbances with
Auray WLF-012 foam windscreens. Environmental metrics were monitored with a weather station
located at the drone park.

Figure 3.2 – Virginia Tech Drone Park

Figure 3.3 – Drone and microphone configuration in drone park

3.1.3 Acoustic Measurement and Instrumentation
In this work, all acoustic measurements were made with Bruel & Kjaer ½” type 4190
microphones. A single ½” microphone is shown in Figure 3.5. This preamplifier unit provides
uncertainties of ± 1 dB over the acoustic range of 20 to 20 kHz. Acoustic data were synchonyzed
with the force and RPM data by sampling at 65536 Hz which is well above the Nyquist frequency
when considering the upper limit of the acoustic range of the microphone. Data were acquired at
65536 Hz. The microphones were positioned at the center of the ground plates on their side
pointing laterally normal to the flight path. All measurements consider the ground plate to be a
perfect reflector and therefore should be considered to double the acoustic pressure at the
microphone. The measurement period for each of these test are dependent upon the flight time.
The location of each microphone is listed in Table 3.1 in reference to the coordinate definition in
Figure 3.4 where h (height above ground) is assumed to be 9.14 m in order to calculate 𝜃𝑚𝑖𝑛 and
𝑅𝑚𝑖𝑛 for an example flyover.

3.3 Anechoic Chamber
3.3.1 Experimental Arrangement
The noise produced by two sUAS rotors as well as the torque, thrust and RPM for each
rotor was measured in an anechoic chamber at Virginia Tech. The chamber, designed by Eckel
Industries, is fully anechoic, providing a low frequency cut-off of 100 Hz, and has inner
dimensions from wedge tip to wedge tip of 4.5 (L) ⨯ 2.5 (W) ⨯ 2.5 (H) m. Given that recirculation
and turbulence ingestion effects were expected, two 1524 mm ⨯ 1524 mm varying solidity screens
were placed downstream from the hub of each rotor inside the anechoic chamber to dampen
turbulence in the rotor downwash similar to the method presented in Stephenson et al. (2019) and
Whiteside et al. (2019). The first screen, with a respective open area of 69% and cell size of 2.7
mm, was placed 1.82 meters from the 9450S disk plane. For the larger 2170R rotor the first screen
was placed 1.71 m downstream from the rotor plane. The second screen, with a respective open
area of 59% and cell size of 1.7 mm, for the 9450S was placed 4 and 7 rotor radii from the first,
whereas for the larger 2170R rotor, the second screen was placed 4 and 6 radii from the first. The
corresponding range of RPM’s tested for each rotor in the anechoic chamber are shown in Table
3.3.
Table 3.3. Anechoic chamber RPM range
Rotor

RPM Range

9450S

4235-6412

2170R

1825-4194

41

3.3.2 Instrumentation
3.3.2.1 Rotor Test Stand
For each rotor-motor configuration a JR3 Multi-Axis Force/Torque Sensor was mounted
behind a 3D printed motor sting manufactured from a high strength Digital ABS plus material.
The load cell is then attached to an aluminum adapter plate connected to a 31.75 mm diameter by
16.19 mm horizontal aluminum tube that is connected to a vertical support by an NPT aluminum
90-degree elbow. The 90-degree elbow is then connected to a vertical 31.75 mm diameter by
1187.5 mm aluminum tube which was anchored to a hard point in the anechoic chamber. The test
stand is maintained at a stable vertical position using four tensioned segments of 3.175 mm
diameter coated wire rope connected between the vertical support and hard points on the chamber
floor. Given that the diameter of the motor used to spin the 9450S rotor was smaller than the 2170R
rotor, an additional extension was 3D printed with the downstream end having the same diameter
as the E2000 Standard motor and the upstream end having the same diameter as the 2312S motor.
3.3.2.2 Thrust, Torque, Motor Control, and RPM Measurement
To measure the unsteady and steady thrust and torque produced by each of the rotors, a
JR3 Multi-Axis Force/Torque (Model No: 6122) sensor was installed between the static test stand
and the 3D printed sting. The load cell provides measurement uncertainties of ± 0.25 N and is
shown in Figure 3.7 for reference.

Figure 3.7 – 6 Axis JR3 Load Cell
The load cell provides a six-channel output of voltages which are converted into 𝐹𝑥 , 𝐹𝑦 , 𝐹𝑧 , 𝑀𝑥 , 𝑀𝑦 ,
and 𝑀𝑧 using calibration matrix supplied by the manufacturer. The voltage output from the load
cell was feed into a JR3 external electronics box (S/N 7097) shown in Figure 3.8. The 8-channel
offset outlined by the red box consists of potentiometers which provided a variable resistance to
the voltage supplied by the load cell. These were trimmed to under at least 5 𝑚𝑉 when each rotor
assembly was installed during each measurement campaign. The trimming was done by first
attaching a ground wire of a voltmeter to the ground pin outlined by the blue box. The positive
probe coming out of the voltmeter was then placed on the corresponding test points for each offset.
The voltage across each of the offsets was then adjusted by tightening or loosening the
corresponding set screws until the readout on the voltmeter was below 5 𝑚𝑉.

42

Figure 3.8 – JR3 external electronics box
Rotor rotation rates were controlled using a FLAME 60A HV ESC that received pulse width
modulated signals from an Arduino Uno. The rotation rates during each acquisition were measured
using an Adafruit 5 mW 650 nm red laser pointer projecting through the blade disk onto a
THORLABS FDS1010-Si photodiode. The Adafafruit laser pointer is shown on the left of Figure
3.9 and the THORLABS diode is shown on the right of Figure 3.9. The photodiode used was
capable of a 65 𝑛𝑠 rise time. All forces and the output of the photodiode were connected to a 24bit Bruel & Kjaer LAN XI DAQ, shown in Figure 3.10, via the UA – 2100 front panel with BNC
connectors and sampled at 65536 Hz.

Figure 3.9 – Adafruit laser pointer (left), THORLABS photodiode (right)

Figure 3.10 - 24-bit Bruel & Kjaer LAN XI DAQ

the anechoic chamber is shown in Figure 3.12.

3.4 Virginia Tech Drone Park
For a direct comparison of results between two facilities measurements of the 9450S and
2170R rotors were also conducted at the drone park. Measurements of noise, thrust, torque, and
RPM were conducted using the same instrumentation and microphone layout as the anechoic
chamber. Each mic’s location relative to each of the tested rotor’s disk was shown previously in
Section 3.3.2.3. The outdoor facility did not contain any hard points to fasten the test stand or
attach the coated wire ropes to. Therefore, the test stand was threaded onto threaded rod that was
mounted into a 5-gallon bucket filled with cement and the coated wire ropes were attached to spiral
ground stakes. The open environment of the park eliminates the confinement effects observed in
the anechoic chamber; therefore, screens were not placed in the rotor wakes for these
measurements. The corresponding range of RPM’s tested for each rotor in the drone park are
shown in Table 3.5. Given these measurements were subjected to environmental disturbances each
microphone was shielded with an Auray WLF-012 foam windscreen. The complete annotated
assembly including all instrumentation discussed prior in Section 2.2 with the 2170R rotor
installed is shown in Figure 3.13. Environmental conditions were monitored with a weather station
located at the drone park. Table 3.6 shows the weather conditions at the time of the presented
measurements over the two day period of outdoor testing. The temperatures were similar for both
days. The 1st day of testing was overcast while the 2nd day was clear and sunny. The primary
difference from each test day was the higher wind speeds and overcast conditions.

3.5 Virginia Tech Kentland Experimental Aerial Systems (KEAS) Laboratory
3.5.1 Experimental Arrangement
Outdoor measurements were conducted at the Virginia Tech KEAS Laboratory to also
study the sound without confinement effects. The laboratory located at Virginia Tech’s Kentland
Farm agricultural research center includes about 1800 acres of university owned farmland in a
sparsely populated area. Measurements were conducted on a 91.4 m by 21.3 m asphalt airstrip. No
measures were taken to mitigate the effects of constructive and destructive interference resulting
from the acoustic reflections on the hard surface although the frequencies of the expected
46

destructive interference have been calculated. Each rotor was mounted onto 304.8 mm (L) x 25.4
mm (W) x 25.4 mm (H) aluminum 8020 T-slotted sting attached to the 6-axis load cell. The load
cell was then attached to the same aluminum test stand used in the anechoic chamber and drone
park. To maintain the vertical position of the test stand, four segments of 3.175 mm diameter
coated wire rope were held in tension by attaching them to concrete cinder blocks. The 2170R and
CF30x10.5 rotors were attached directly onto the aluminum sting. Given that the diameter of the
motor used to spin the 9450S rotor was smaller than that of the larger rotors, an additional
extension was 3D printed with the downstream end having the same diameter as the E2000
Standard motor and the upstream end having the same diameter as the 2312S motor. The respective
assembly and associated dimensions are shown in Figure 3.14 equipped with the 9450S rotor. A
front view with the corresponding diameters of the load cell and 3D printed sting end cap is also
shown at the bottom right of Figure 3.14. The dimension from the end of the aluminum sting to
each rotor plane, denoted by L, for the 9450S rotor was 64.35 mm and 187.25 mm for the two
larger rotors.

Figure 3.14 – Aluminum test stand used for measurements in the anechoic chamber, drone park,
and KEAS Laboratory with aluminum sting extension used only for measurements at the KEAS
laboratory

47

The same 11 microphones that were used in the anechoic chamber and drone park were
used. To mitigate effects of environmental disturbances the same windscreens used in the drone
park were placed on all microphones. Initially the mic locations were set to the same observer
angle and absolute distance as those for the measurements conducted in the anechoic chamber and
drone park. However, wake interactions were found to occur when testing the larger CF30x10.5
rotor. Given this the absolute distance from each rotor’s hub was increased while holding the
observer angle constant. The corresponding observer angle and absolute distance referenced from
the disk plane and hub of each rotor is shown in Table 3.7. During the measurement campaign the
on-site weather station was not in operation. Therefore, the atmospheric conditions of wind speed
and direction, pressure, temperature, and density were recorded on two second intervals with a
Kestrel 5000 Environmental meter. The Kestrel anemometer was placed 2.95 m upstream of the
rotor plane and 5.74 m to the right of the rotor axis looking upstream. Average weather conditions
from each test day are shown in Table 3.8. To synchronize the atmospheric conditions with noise
and force measurements the time stamps of when the last data point was logged from the noise
files were found and then 45 seconds were subtracted from this. Atmospheric properties were then
averaged during this time interval. The wind direction was recorded with a handheld compass
mounted on the anemometer tripod that was equipped with a wind vane. For this analysis the wind
direction, 𝜙𝑤 , and average wind speed, ̅̅̅
𝑉𝑤 , will be presented relative to the rotor plane where for
example a wind heading angle of 90° would be classified as a head wind.

3.5.2 Mobile Support Arm Assemblies
To study the effects of a rotor interacting with an airframe, mock support arms in the form
of wooden dowel rods were added at multiple distances from each rotor plane during operation.
The diameters of the dowel used for the 9450S, and larger rotors were 15.9 mm and 25.4 mm,
respectively. For the larger rotors, a sleeve bearing carriage assembly was attached to the 8020aluminum sting. A bracket base was 3D printed and attached to the carriage assembly along with
a system of L-brackets and nylon spacers. The upper L brackets were inset 6.89 mm into a slot
towards one end of the dowel rod and secured with bolts. The corresponding configuration
equipped with the 2170R rotor and associated dimensions are shown in the top of Figure 3.15. A
system of L brackets and nylon spacers was also manufactured and attached to the 3D printed
extension sting with a vibration damping U-bolt for the 9450S rotor and is shown at the bottom of

indicated by the dashed red line. The complete annotated assemblies including all previously
mentioned components and assemblies with the R2170 rotor installed is shown in Figure 3.16.

3.6 Virginia Tech Anechoic Open Jet
3.6.1 Experimental Arrangement
Measurements were conducted at the Virginia Tech Open Jet Wind Tunnel (VTOJ). The
facility offers the ability to study the effects of varying inflow speeds and inflow angles relative to
the rotor plane on aerodynamic performance and noise. A secondary feature is the ability to
mitigate the effects of recirculation which has been shown to increase tonal noise at higher
harmonics of the BPF [4,8]. The facility and its associated dimensions are shown in Figure 3.17.

Figure 3.17 - Schematic and system components of the Virginia Tech Anechoic Open Jet Wind
Tunnel

52

Individual components of the open jet consist of a Twin City QSL axial fan, a 90° muffler,
acoustic baffles, a settling chamber, a 7.8:1 contraction section, a 0.762 m x 0.762 m open jet test
section, and a 1.27 m x 1.27 m outlet that exhaust into the room. Acoustic foam was applied to the
hard wall shown by the horizontal dashed line in the bottom of Figure 3.17 to alleviate noise
reflections. The facility is fully anechoic down to 420 Hz and has a maximum freestream speed,
U∞ , of 40 m/s. The static pressure was measured in the settling chamber and inside the test section
to calculate U∞ during measurements. The experimental campaign in the Open Jet was designed
to analyze the noise of rotors with different geometries and number of blades for different yaw
angles and inflow speeds as well as to study the influence of different ESCs and switching
frequencies. The complete experimental campaign with all tested rotors, rotation rates, inflow
speeds and inflow angles are shown in Table 3.9 below. Measurements for analyzing the effects
of ESC switching rates at zero yaw on the noise are outlined in at the bottom of Table 3.9. Noise
radiated by the T – U8 Lite KV150 brushless motor was also analyzed and identified by taking
acoustic measurements of the CF30x10.5 rotor with and without a noise barrier designed to house
the motor.

3.6.2 Rotor Test Stand
Each rotor was mounted to the end of a 38.1 mm (L) x 25.4 mm (W) x 25.4 (mm) piece of
80/20 aluminum T-slotted framing. The opposite end was secured to an 80/20 aluminum inline
pivot which had another 304.8 mm (L) x 25.4 mm (W) x 25.4 (mm) piece of 80/20 aluminum Tslotted framing on the other end. This sub assembly was then attached to six-component load cell
with aluminum L-brackets. The load cell was fastened to an aluminum stand fabricated from 31.75
mm aluminum tubing. The corresponding dimensions and annotated assembly with the 9450S
rotor installed is shown in Figure 3.18. The distance from the bottom of the test stand was chosen
53

so that each rotor’s rotational axis would be at the center of the inlet. For yawed rotor
measurements, the test stand was moved to keep the rotor hub at the center of the inlet. Wake
interaction effects were also mitigated for the 9450S rotor by installing the same 3D printed
extension used in all other measurement campaigns. These differences are shown in Figure 3.19
where the distance from the upstream side of the load cell to the rotor plane is 0.58 m for the 9450S
rotor and 0.42 m for the larger rotors, respectively.

Figure 3.18 - Aluminum test stand base top view (left), Side view of test stand, load cell, and
inline pivot with 9450S rotor installed (right)

54

Figure 3.19 – Rotor sting extension and load cell assemblies, 9450S rotor (right),CF30x10.5
rotor (left)
3.6.3 Motor Control and RPM Measurement
Thrust and Torque were measured using the same 6-Axis load cell as all other measurement
campaigns. In general, rotor rotational speeds were controlled using a FLAME 60A HV ESC. To
determine the effect of the ESC and the ESC switching frequency, a Phoenix Edge Lite HV 80
AMP ESC was used which allowed variable switching rates. PWM rates for this ESC were set to
8, 12, 16, and 24 kHz. PWM signals were transmitted from a MATLAB GUI to an Arduino Uno
which were then output to each ESC. Rotational rates were measured using a Monarch Instrument
Remote Optical LED Sensor (PN: 6180 – 057) shown in Figure 3.20. The ROS diode can measure
rotational rates up to 250000 RPM.

Figure 3.20 – Monarch Instruments ROS Diode
A short piece of 12.7 mm wide by 101.6 𝜇m thick 3M 7610 High Gain Reflective Tape (S –
19232) was placed on the pressure side of each rotor to reflect the light emitted by the sensor once
per revolution. This tape was placed coincident to each rotor’s hub. At this spanwise location the
reflective tape was assumed to have no significant effect on the aerodynamic performance of the
blades or add additional mass that could produce an imbalance. Unlike other measurement
campaigns, rotational rates were able to be tuned by an operator until the desired speed was
55

displayed on the MATLAB GUI. At this point data acquisition was started for a duration of 33
seconds. Forces and the output of the led sensor were recorded at the same sampling rate of 65536
Hz using the same DAQ’s used in all other facilities.
3.6.4 Acoustic Instrumentation
Acoustic measurements in the Virginia Tech Anechoic Open Jet were conducted with 15
microphones. Due to the size of the facility only some observer angles could be matched to the
three other measurement campaigns. The microphones were configured in a line array placed 1.94
m away from each rotor hub parallel to their respective rotational axes at zero-degree yaw. A top
view of the experimental setup in the open jet is shown in Figure 3.21. The distance from the face
of the inlet to each rotor disk plane 𝐷1 , was 9.8 cm for the 9450S rotor and 29.2 cm for the larger
rotors, respectively. Each observer angle and absolute distance from the rotor hub to the face of
each microphone are listed in Table 3.10. The complete annotated assembly with the CF30x10.5
rotor installed is shown at the top of Figure 3.22. The 30x10.5 rotor installed at a yaw angle, 𝜙𝑝 is
shown at the bottom of Figure 3.22.

58

Chapter 4 Assessment of the Acoustic Footprint of a
Hexacopter
The noise from a DJI Matrice 600 Pro Hexacopter was first analyzed in forward flight and
hover to assess the total sound of a vehicle in flight. This data also provides an observation of the
most important noise characteristics of these vehicles. Tones were found to dominate for
frequencies less than 800 Hz with broadband noise at higher frequency. To assess the human
response to noise, one potential metric is commonly used which is A-weighting. Although, it is
not the only potential metric, it does illustrate the human ear’s perception at different frequencies
and typically peaks around 2 kHz. A-weighting narrowband spectra of a hexacopter in flight
emphasize the importance of the broadband noise, and therefore, noise reduction strategies should
consider these sources as well as the tonal noise. The noise produced by the platform during lowspeed flyovers is also like the noise produced in hover. Therefore, estimates of flyover noise at
low flight speeds may be possible from data acquired in hover. Ambient weather conditions, in
particular, wind speed, were shown to have a significant impact on the measured noise influencing
both the tonal and broadband components. Given this, environmental conditions should be
carefully monitored to produce repeatable measurements in the field. Integrated OASPL levels as
a function of time were found to be consistent from day to day, and within ± 1.3 dB for a single
measurement with the drone in hover.

4.1 Noise of a Hexacopter in Flight
4.1.1 Flyover Noise
To assess the total sound of the DJI Matrice 600 Pro Hexacopter acoustic measurements
were taken during flyover and hover at the Virginia Tech Drone Park with five microphones placed
at the center of 1 m diameter by 1.3 cm thick HPVA maple plywood ground plates as shown
previously in Figure 3.3. Each microphone was shielded from environmental disturbances with
windscreens. The environmental conditions were monitored with a weather station located at the
drone park. Measurements were conducted on two different days. The environmental condition for
each day is shown in Table 4.2. For measurements of noise during flyover the hexacopter was
maintained at a constant altitude and speed and the flight path was directly over Mic. 1. For
measurements of noise with the vehicle in hover, the altitude was held constant and the hexacopter
was directly above Mic.1. The defined flight path and microphone positions were shown
previously in Figure 3.4. The time series acoustic pressure for a single flyover as measured by each
of the ground microphones is shown in Figure 4.1 for a flyover altitude of 7.5 m. The noise
increases as the drone passes directly across the spanwise location of the microphones. The ground
speed of the vehicle is overlaid on the time series for Mic 1 as well as indicated by the red line.
The average speed for the central 12.9s period of flight, indicated by the black line, is equal to 3.23
m/s respectively. Although the maximum speed of the drone is 18 m/s, the speed of the drone in
these flights was limited by the length of the cage.
Sound Exposure Level (SEL) is a common metric used to characterize transient events
such as an aircraft flyover where there is movement between the source and an observer rather
59

than a transient event like the interaction with a gust. It represents the acoustic level required to
produce the same amount of energy as the transient event over a period of 1s and is calculated as
𝑆𝐸𝐿 = 10 𝑙𝑜𝑔10 (

1 𝑡2 𝑝2 (𝑡)
∫
𝑑𝑡)
1𝑠 𝑡1 (20𝜇𝑃𝑎)2

(4.1)

Where the time interval from 𝑡1 to 𝑡2 is the time in seconds, from the beginning to the end of the
integration period for the sound produced during a transient event such as a flyover. According to
SAE AIR1845A, the time interval between 𝑡2 − 𝑡1 must be long enough to include all significant
contributions to the total sound exposure and the aircraft power setting must remain constant, i.e.,
constant translational velocity and rotor tip speed. It also states that sufficient accuracy is achieved
by integrating over the time interval during which the A-weighted sound level is within 10 dB of
its maximum value. To determine this, short time Fourier transforms (STFT) were computed over
the 12.9s window of constant velocity. OASPL (dBA) was then computed by integrating the
resultant squared acoustic pressure from the BPF of approximately 94 Hz to 20 kHz. A moving
average filter of 1 second was then applied to the integrated values of OASPL (dBA) and then
plotted vs time. These results are shown in Figure 4.2(a) for each of the five microphones. The
SEL in dB(Z) for the drone flyover was computed for this event by integrating the squared acoustic
pressure for each microphone over each of the time windows where OASPL (dBA) was within 10
dB of its maximum value is shown in Figure 4.1(b). The computed SEL values for each
microphone are listed in Table 4.1.

Figure 4.1 - Flyover acoustic pressure time history for all five microphones for a drone altitude
of 7.5 m and forward speed of 3.23 m/s

second period of constant flight, b) Integration windows where OASPL (dBA) is at most 10 dB

Spectrograms of the noise measured by Mic 1 are shown in Figure 4.3. These spectrograms
were processed with a bin size of 6555 such that the frequency resolution is approximately 10Hz
and the time resolution is 0.1 seconds. Figure 4.3(a) shows the spectrogram for the full
measurement period. The measurement begins with the drone in operation on the ground. As the
drone increases altitude prior to the flyover, the measured broadband noise above 1 kHz increases.
Forward flight begins at approximately 1.1 seconds into the measurement. At this point, tones
between the second and fifth harmonic of the blade passage frequency noticeably shift frequency
as the rotor blades change rotational speeds. The period of constant drone ground speed is shown
between the two dashed lines. Figure 4.3(b) is the same spectrogram with limits defined by this
period of constant forward speed. A dashed black line indicates the moment in which the drone is
directly overhead as measured by GPS. This point is approximately 0.3s behind the noise peak in
the spectrogram. The peak noise from helicopter rotors tends to project forward, but this is
primarily a result of BVI noise unlike the broadband signature observed in the drone spectrum.
Instead, unsteady loading broadband noise tends to have a dipole directivity which peaks below
the rotor disk plane [69]. The observed shift is more likely due to uncertainty of the GPS location,
which is ± 0.5 m in altitude and ± 1.5 m in the horizontal direction, and the accuracy of the
synchronized acoustic and GPS signals.
As the drone approaches the microphones the strength of the blade passage frequency tone
and its harmonics increase, but the peak frequency of each tone remains fixed. Zawodny et al
(2018) present similar spectrograms of flyovers for a DJI Phantom 2 and a Prioria Hex. Their data
are limited to frequencies below 1 kHz, but they show rotor tones dominate the noise spectrum in
this frequency range. They also show significant wandering of these rotor tones with time, whereas
for this larger vehicle the tones associated with the BPF, and higher order harmonics remain at
least within 40 Hz throughout the measurement period. The broadband noise shown in Figure
4.3(b) changes in both amplitude and frequency. There are two regions of significant broadband
61

noise. The mid-frequency region extends from 300 Hz to 5 kHz. A second higher frequency region
extends from 5 kHz to 20 kHz. In between these two regions spectral levels decrease suggesting
possible destructive interference because of each microphone being mounted on hard ground
boards. The peak frequencies, shown as the spectral humps, in both regions vary as the drone
passes the microphone. The peak frequencies shift lower as the receiver angle, θ, nears zero. The
high frequency region has a single hump, although the sound pressure level may rise again at
frequencies beyond 20 kHz. The mid-frequency region is composed of several humps which were
shown to be caused by scattering from the edge of the ground boards in anechoic chamber tests.
Narrowband power spectral density is computed from these spectrograms by averaging over time.
Figure 4.4 shows a 12s and narrower 2s average of these data centered about the point at
which the drone is directly overhead. During each of these time windows the drone traveled 38.8
m and 6.46 m. The spectra are dominated by the rotor BPF tones and harmonics from ~100 Hz to
700 Hz. The rotors are not synchronized and do not spin at the same RPM, so these tones are
scattered which is particularly noticeable at the 3rd harmonic or higher. The broadband humps at
mid and high frequencies are evident above this low frequency region. Averaging over a smaller
duration in time of 2 seconds reduces the spectral smoothing created by averaging over the spectral
features which shift and change magnitude with time. The 2 second average spectrum has a
stronger high frequency hump, and some scalloping is apparent in the mid-frequency region
between 1 kHz - 3 kHz. Of course, these characteristics are also obscured by the increasing
uncertainty due to the lower number of spectral averages.
As stated previously, measurements were conducted on two different days. The
temperature was similar on both days with wind speed factoring as the primary difference as shown
in Table 4.2. Therefore, data from measurements on Day 1 where the wind speeds were lower is
the ideal day for analysis. Day 1 was still, while Day 2 was blustery. A spectrogram of Mic 1 with
the hexacopter during flyover at an altitude and speed of 12.3 m and 5.9 m/s from Day 2 is shown
in Figure 4.5. In comparison to the flyover measurements on Day 1 for Mic 1 shown previously in
Figure 4.3(a) there is an increased significance of background noise at low frequencies and regions
of increased broadband noise at high frequencies as well. This increase in noise is due to the
differences in wind speed on each test day. Given this, environmental effects must be considered
when conducting acoustic measurements of these vehicles in flight. These effects also hinder the
success of repeat measurements. This inability to produce repeat measurements could result in
inaccurate comparisons of data sets from other experimental measurements and lead to an
unreliable assessment of noise of these aircraft. More comparisons of data from Day 1 to Day 2
will be made later in Section 4.2.

62

Full time series, b) Period of constant flight speed

64

4.1.2 Hovering Noise
Measurements were conducted with the drone hovering directly above Mic 1. The drone
was at a constant altitude of 9.18 m above the microphone. The spectrogram for Mic 1 is shown
in Figure 4.6. The drone’s operation is steady in this condition such that the peak of the rotor
related tones remain within ± 20 Hz or in terms of each rotor’s rotation rate ± 600 RPM
respectively. These remain consistent throughout the measurement as they do not wander across
frequency unlike that observed in Cabell et al. [12] for the DJI Phantom 2 and Prioria Hex at
similar altitude.

Figure 4.6 – Mic 1 spectrogram for hover at 9.18 m, Day 1
Since these data are constant with respect to time, the spectra are more easily compared through
time-averaged frequency spectra as shown in Figure 4.7. The BPF and harmonics are visible in all
spectra up to approximately 1 kHz. The strength of these tones decreases with increasing distance
from the drone. Above 1 kHz, the tones diminish into the broadband noise. The spectral shape of
the broadband noise changes significantly with position. At Mic 1, directly under the drone, the
spectrum has lumps from 1 kHz and 3 kHz spaced approximately 600 Hz apart peak-to-peak.
These spectral humps don’t appear to be a function of ground reflections between the mic center
and the hard surface of the ground board. For these measurements, each microphone was placed
at the center of wooden ground plates as described previously in Chapter 3 and no measures were
taken to mitigate acoustic reflections which cause constructive and destructive interference at
specific frequencies. This can lead to nulls in the measured acoustic spectrum. To analyze these
effects, the method of images was used for a microphone located 32.5 mm above the hard ground
plate. The height of the microphone is equal to half the measured diameter of the microphone with
the windscreen installed. These results show a null to occur at 5190 and 15660 Hz for Mic 1 and
are indicated by the black dashed lines in Figure 4.7. At Mic 2, slightly displaced from the center
of drone body, these lumps decrease in magnitude and the peaks shift to slightly higher frequency.
This trend continues with increasing receiver angle, 𝜃. The high frequency broadband noise, above
5 kHz, continues a similar trend. The peak magnitude decreases from Mic 1 to Mic 5 and the peak
65

frequency increases. For Mic 5, the peak frequency shifts beyond 20 kHz, the limit of the figure.
As indicated by the black dash lines in Figure 4.7, the mid frequency humps are believed to not be
a function of ground reflections between the Mic center and the hard surface below it.

Figure 4.7 – Narrowband spectra for the five microphone positions during hover above Mic 1 at
an altitude of 9.18 m, Day 1
Constructive and destructive interference of the measured acoustic pressure signal can also
occur as the radiated pressure wave interacts with the edges of the ground board. To analyze these
effects and infer the frequency response of the ground board and microphone combination
measurements were conducted in the Virginia Tech Anechoic Chamber previously mentioned in
Chapter 3 Section 3.3. White noise was radiated by a Bruel and Kjaer omnidirectional source
(OmniPower Sound Source Type 4292-L) from 50 – 5000 Hz and recorded by a single microphone
with and without the ground board at the two positions shown in Figure 4.8(a). For measurements
with a single microphone the radial distance from the bottom surface of the speaker was 𝐿𝑀𝑖𝑐 =
2.51 m. The corresponding vertical and horizontal distances for measurements with the Mic and
the ground board were 𝐿𝐺𝐵 = 2.64 m and 𝐿ℎ = 2.49 m respectively. For all measurements the same
Auray windscreens encased the microphones that were used during the flyover measurements
conducted at the Virginia Tech drone park. Narrowband acoustic spectra for measurements at each
location with and without the ground board corrected to a reference distance of 1 m is shown in
Figure 4.8(b). Comparison of spectra for measurements with and without the ground board directly
beneath shows constructive interference from 140 Hz to 580 Hz. At higher frequencies spectral
humps also appear between 670 Hz to 1.2 kHz, 1290 Hz to 1870 Hz, 2030 Hz to 2500 Hz, 2510
Hz to 3110 Hz, and 3120 Hz to 3660 Hz. For measurements with the Mic and ground board at the
lateral position constructive interference occurs from approximately 90 Hz to 1.5 kHz. At higher
frequencies destructive interference occurs from 1510 Hz to 4060 Hz and then adds constructively
once again from 4070 Hz to 4810 Hz respectively. Performing these measurements allows one to
determine the frequency response of the experimental arrangement when the microphone is placed
at the center of a hard ground plane. The frequency response is determined by subtracting spectra
66

with the ground board installed from that with the Mic only. This response is shown in Figure 4.9
and is defined as ∆𝐿𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 .

Figure 4.8 – Ground board reflection study experimental layout

Figure 4.9 – Frequency response of Mic and ground board at beneath and lateral positions
For measurements with the ground directly underneath spectral levels increase by at least
6 dB from 150 Hz to 520 Hz, 740 Hz to 1140 Hz, 1340 Hz to 1730 Hz, and 2070 Hz to 2320 Hz
with maximum levels of constructive interference of more than 8 dB. Destructive interference
greater than 3 dB is shown to occur at 1250 Hz and from 3770 Hz to 6660 Hz respectively. For
measurements with the mic and ground board at this location an increase of 6 dB would suggest
exact ground plane doubling. Assuming a monopole source and the half height of the microphone
this would occur at 5190 Hz and 15560 Hz as indicated by the black dashed lines in Figure 4.7.
67

However, the spectral levels exceed 6 dB over multiple ranges of frequency. For measurements
with the ground board at the lateral position, 𝐿ℎ , spectral levels increase more than 6 dB from 150
Hz to 1410 Hz, at 4560 Hz, and at 5990 Hz respectively. Significant spectral distortions more than
6 dB are shown to occur from approximately 1750 Hz to 3840 Hz. The ground board correction
factor ∆𝐿𝑐𝑜𝑟𝑟 is sensitive to the mic location and thus the corrections cannot be directly applied to
the experimental data, however these results do show that the scalloping that occurs over the mid
frequency region is due to edge scattering effects.
Similar spectral characteristics, shown in Figure 4.6 and Figure 4.7, have been found in
acoustic spectra of helicopter rotors. In the case of the drone, there may be interaction between
adjacent rotors and the drone body which contributes significantly to the unsteady blade loading
producing the broader spectral features. It has been suggested that the mid-frequency noise is
associated with unsteady interaction effects for helicopters, typically BVI. Impulsive BVI noise is
produced when the blades interact with tip or inboard-originated vortices that are of predetermined
positions with respect to any blade position. This allows for a deterministic solution to the BVI
blade loading, which results in noise being generated at the higher order harmonics of the BPF
[76]. In Intaratep et al. [29], the tonal noise at low frequencies was found to be relatively unaffected
when operating a quadcopter with multiple rotors. However, a significant increase in broadband
noise was shown to occur. At mid frequencies, the emitted noise is associated with broadband
noise sources such as turbulence ingestion or blade wake interaction. These sources can appear
tonal at the higher order harmonics of the BPF and are present in the measured spectrum with the
drone in hover as shown in Figure 4.7. At high frequencies, the generated noise is associated with
blade self-noise which includes sources such as separation noise, turbulent boundary layer trailing
edge noise, or laminar boundary layer vortex shedding noise [76]. In Pettingill et al. [25], the
broadband noise from a single isolated rotor and a quadcopter in hover and forward flight was
measured and accompanied with self-noise predictions. Predictions of each self-noise source for
the single isolated rotor in hover were found to agree with the measured results when imposing a
tripped boundary condition on each blade section. For an untripped boundary condition, spectral
features over the mid frequency range were under predicted. For both boundary conditions no
spectral peaks were predicted and were attributed to broadband sources associated with blade wake
interaction or turbulence ingestion. For multiple rotors operating on the quadcopter in forward
flight the measured noise was shown to be dominated by broadband sources associated with selfnoise. Given this, the noise of the DJI Hexacopter is expected to be dominated by noise sources
associated with turbulence ingestion, blade wake interaction, and self-noise sources at frequencies
above 1kHz. For comparison with the flyover data, SEL can be calculated for the hovering drone
by integrating over the same periods of time shown previously in Figure 4.2(b). Table 4.3 shows
the equivalent SEL for each microphone.

4.1.3 Comparison of Hover to Flyover
Similar trends are observed in the hover and flyover measurements particularly for the mid
and high frequency broadband noise. In the mid and high frequency regions, the broadband peaks
in the hover spectra decay and shift to higher frequency from Mic 1 to Mic 5 corresponding to
increases in 𝜃 and 𝑅, receiving angle and radial distance. Likewise, during the flyover, the same
68

trends with 𝜃 and 𝑅 are observed. On approach, 𝜃 and 𝑅 decrease reaching a minimum as the
drone is directly overhead. At this point, the frequency of the peaks in the broadband noise from
300 Hz to 5 kHz and from 5 kHz to 20 kHz are at a minimum and their magnitude is a maximum
as shown previously in. As 𝜃 and 𝑅 increase for the rest of the flyover, the peak frequencies
increase to their initial values and their magnitudes reduce. Figure 4.10 is a direct comparison of
the Mic 1 and 2 spectra from the hover condition and the 2s spectral average from the flyover. The
mid and high frequency regions are similar in magnitude although averaging of the flyover data
tends to smooth the lumps particularly in the mid-frequency region. As shown previously, in
Figure 4.10 with the hexacopter in hover, increased broadband spectral levels from 180 Hz to 450
Hz, 780 Hz to 1290 Hz, 1310 Hz to 1890 Hz, and 1900 Hz to 2530 Hz are due to edge scattering
by the hard ground plates. The spectral tones below 1 kHz differ between flyover and hover due
to the change in rotor rotational rate during forward flight. All rotors on the vehicle do not spin at
the same RPM during forward flight. Therefore, the maximum amplitudes of the tones appear
weaker, but there are more tones to account for in the flyover spectrum.

Figure 4.10 – Comparison of spectra from flyover and hover, Day 1
Differences in calculated SEL given in Table 4.1 and Table 4.3 are 2.1, 2.9, 3.2, 3.6, and 2.7 dB(Z)
for Mics 1 to 5, respectively. In all cases, the calculation of SEL in the hover condition is louder
because the source isn’t moving. The calculation of SEL in this case is inappropriate but is used
simply as a means of comparison. If the drone noise source is assumed to be a monopole, SEL can
be easily calculated for the hover and flyover conditions as well. The difference between the hover
and flyover conditions with this assumption is 2.1 dB at Mic 1 and 1.1 dB at Mic 5. This simple
calculation only accounts for the radial decay of noise from the source and the altitude difference
between the presented hover and flyover conditions. The experimentally observed decrease at all
microphones is larger due to the directivity of the drone noise sources which propagate efficiently
beneath the vehicle.

69

4.2 Estimation of NPD Curves and Acoustic Footprint During Flyover Operations
Analyses of the noise of these platforms often focus on the tones which are responsible for
the peak levels and dominate the low frequency portions of the spectra, but, as shown previously
in Figure 4.3, as the drone approaches an observer, the broadband noise can become significant.
In fact, if a simple A-weighting is used, as shown in Figure 4.11 for the same measurement, the
mid-frequency broadband noise is the dominant component, thus should not be ignored in the
analysis of these systems. This also suggests that possible noise reduction schemes and the
characterization of low-noise drones is highly dependent on the metric used to define “low noise”.
This may impact noise reduction strategies for different platforms Figure 4.12 (a) and (b) and show
corresponding spectrograms for Mic 5 for the same flyover unweighted and A-weighted,
respectively. The vertical dashed line in Figure 4.12 again references the time at which the drone
is directly over Mic 1.

Figure 4.11 - Spectrogram for Mic 1 in dBA/Hz for flyover, h = 7.5 and V = 3.21 m/s, Day 1
The low frequency blade passage tones dominate the broadband noise in the unweighted
spectrum but are much closer in magnitude to the broadband noise when A-weighted. The drone
has a minimal radial distance of 17.5 m to Mic 5 compared to 7.5 m to Mic 1 during the flyover.
Also, the radial distance and receiver angle to the source do not change as rapidly with respect to
the laterally displaced location of Mic 5 compared to Mic 1 as the drone performs the flyover.
Therefore, the broadband noise is reduced and the directivity effects, observed as a shift in the
peak frequencies of the broadband sources, are not as apparent. We can compare the results from
the two microphone positions directly by computing a 2s average of the spectrograms about the
peak acoustic levels of the flyover. During this time, the drone travels 6.4 m. This produces larger
variation in 𝑅 and 𝜃 at the Mic 1 position due to its closer proximity to the flight path. The average
spectra are shown in Figure 4.13. This figure also shows spectra from a measurement taken with
the drone hovering directly over Mic at an altitude of 9.2 m that have been corrected to the flyover
altitude of 7.5 m.

70

Figure 4.12 - Spectrogram for Mic 5 for flyover, h = 7.5 m and V = 3.21 m/s, Day 1, a) dB/Hz,
b) dBA/Hz

71

Figure 4.13 - 2s average spectra during flyover, h=7.5 m and V=3.21 m/s, compared to hover, h
= 9.2 m, Day 1
The Sound Exposure Level (SEL) for the flyover was calculated at all five microphone positions
for each of the time intervals shown previously in Figure 4.2(b). SEL is an integrated quantity
which is used to build NPD curves (SEL vs slant distance from flight path at different power
settings) used in the FAA’s AEDT and INM community annoyance prediction tools. NPD curves
provide the relationship between the sound level of a given aircraft at a reference flight and
atmosphere, and the slant distance from the flight path, for a certain aircraft configuration and a
few power settings. The procedure to analytically develop NPD curves is given in SAE AIR1845A
and were summarized in Synodinos et al. [11] as:
1. Atmospheric conditions are measured experimentally.
2. The terrain around the microphones is flat and unobstructed.
3. The test flight path is nominally level (parallel to the ground) at a nominal altitude of 100
to 800 m
4. Noise measurements are made directly under the flight path and so are unaffected by lateral
attenuation.
5. The aircraft configuration remains constant throughout each flyover duration.
6. Flyover effective duration is normally determined by the 10 dB down time (i.e., the period
during which the OASPL (dBA) is within 10 dB of the maximum level).
7. Measured SPL’s are corrected for instrument calibration and adjusted to account for
differences between actual and reference atmospheric conditions.
8. A flyover at the nominal height is repeated for each engine power setting.
9. The noise at the remaining NPD standard distances is evaluated using extrapolation,
accounting for the effects of spherical wave spreading, atmospheric absorption, and
differences on the effective duration.
The noise levels are provided at 10 standard slant distances in different single event noise metrics
which are SEL and the maximum A-weighted sound pressure level, 𝐿𝐴,𝑚𝑥 . Examples of SEL NPD
72

curves for an Airbus A330-301 during takeoff are shown in Figure 4.14(a), where each curve
describes a different engine power setting. A diagram representing a typical test flyover for
obtaining NPD data is shown in Figure 4.14(b), where the test aircraft flies at constant altitude, z
directly over a microphone located at O at a constant engine power setting 𝑗, yielding the sound
power level (PWL), 𝐿𝑤 (𝑗). Point 𝑃(𝑗) and polar angle 𝜃𝑝 (𝑗) represent the location where 𝐿𝐴,𝑚𝑥
occur at the distance 𝑅𝑝 (𝑗) away from the microphone. Objectively speaking, using this method
𝐿𝐴,𝑚𝑥 and SEL are determined experimentally for a set of predefined engine power settings 𝑗,
which yield the SEL values indicated by each cross in Figure 4.14(a). Then the noise levels are
propagated to other standard NPD distances, accounting for atmospheric absorption and the effects
of spherical spreading, yielding the points marked with a circle in Figure 4.14(a).

Figure 4.14 – Examples of NPD curves and coordinate system for conducting flyover
measurements both from [11] a) SEL NPD curves for a A330-301 at takeoff, b) Diagram
representing a typical test flyover for obtaining NPD data
SEL was calculated using an A-weighting filter, as typical for this quantity, as well as the
unweighted acoustic pressures. It is typical for helicopter noise to be modeled by INM using three
exposure level curves for ground positions 45° to the left and right of the flight path as well as
directly under the flight track. This is due to the asymmetric production of noise by single rotor
vehicles. Angles between available exposure level curves are interpolated while observer positions
at lateral receiver angles greater than 45° use the left or right NPD curves as appropriate.
Measurements were conducted with the drone passing the ground-based microphones to the left
and right of the vehicle’s flight path. A left/right asymmetry was not observed in these data and
therefore SEL will only be analyzed based on lateral ground distance from the flight track
assuming all results are symmetric. Figure 4.15 shows the computed SEL for all five microphones,
A-weighted and unweighted, for the flyover shown in Figure 4.3 and Figure 4.12 Several basic
directivity functions are compared against these values of SEL including static and moving
monopole and dipole directivity functions.

73

Figure 4.15 - Measured lateral directivity in SEL for flyover, h=7.5m and V=3.21 m/s, compared
to simple directivity functions
SEL values for the moving sources were calculated for a simulated source covering the same
distance of travel at the same speed and height. The dipole sources, both static and moving, were
oriented with their propagation axes normal to the ground. This is representative of compact
unsteady loading sources which radiate normal to the blade disk. Curve fits based on radius are
shown in comparison to the measured results, as well. The unweighted data show a lateral decay
−2.65

of 𝑅𝑚𝑖𝑛
and the A-weighted data produce a lateral variation of 𝑅𝑚𝑖𝑛
. Receiver angles for each
microphone relative to the drone at the minimum pass distance are given on the figure for
reference. None of the simulated directivity functions accurately represent the decay of the
unweighted SEL values. The moving dipole source does accurately fit the A-weighted SEL, but
this is considered to be coincidental and not due to fundamental aspects of the true source’s nature.
The difference between the measured weighted and unweighted SEL is due to the relative strength
of the low frequency tonal sources relative to the mid-frequency broadband at each microphone
location. The broadband source quickly becomes less significant in comparison to the tonal noise
at the furthest lateral positions. The dipole fit does not attempt to accurately model this change in
significant source strengths but does fit the observed variation in A-weighted SEL. As a crude
estimate, without including environmental conditions or terrain, the measured lateral variation can
be used to generate ground contours for this steady, level flight operation as shown in Figure 4.16
to determine its impact on the community. Higher speed flyovers were conducted as well. Figure
4.17 shows an A-weighted spectrogram for a flyover at a height of 7.9 m traveling at approximately
double the forward velocity, 6.21 m/s.

This spectrogram displays similar peak levels to Figure 4.11(b). Also, the duration of the
event is similar if normalized to account for the increased velocity. The same spectral features are
apparent in this figure as well indicating there is little change to the noise source at these low
velocities. SEL values for the event shown in Figure 4.11(a) and Figure 4.11(b) were 84.8 dB or
82.7 dBA. The time window for this event was approximately 7.6 seconds for Mic 1. Over this
duration of time, the OASPL (dBA) levels were found to be no less than 10 dB of their maximum.
For the faster flyover presented in Figure 4.17, SEL is reduced to 80.8 dB or 78.8 dBA mainly due
75

to the reduction in event timescale. The period of integration for this calculation was scaled
according to the average flight speed such that the calculation was conducted for similar distances
of travel. Therefore, 7.6 s at 3.21 m/s is equivalent to a period of 3.9 s at 6.21 m/s, and thus SEL
was computed over a period of 3.9 s, the range shown in Figure 4.17. This illustrates a common
issue for rotorcraft vehicles operating at low speed or hover. The SEL metric rises rapidly as the
total exposure time increases. Attempting to rescale the SEL calculation by adding
7.6𝑠
10 log10 (3.9𝑠 ) = 2.9 dB to account for the change in total duration explains much of the observed
difference between the two flyovers. This is interesting because it suggests, through comparison
of Figure 4.3(b), Figure 4.13, and Figure 4.17, that the noise produced at low speeds is similar to
that generated during hover, particularly in the broadband region. There are some differences in
the low frequency tone noise as described in the discussion of Figure 4.13, but these differences
may not considerably influence integrated quantities like SEL (dBA) for a single flyover event.
Figure 4.18shows a comparison of acoustic spectra for hover over Mic 1 at similar altitude
measured by all five microphones on the two separate days. The wind significantly influences
these data at all microphone positions, thus demonstrating the difficulty of producing accurate
predictions of community annoyance on any day but also the underlying difficulty in simply
characterizing the noise of a specific vehicle outdoors and in-flight. The tonal amplitude and
number of harmonics are reduced dramatically on Day 2 at all microphone locations. Also, there
is a significant difference in the mid-frequency broadband, more notably at large lateral
displacements. There is a reduction in the mid-frequency levels with some possible shifting to
higher frequencies at larger lateral distances, and the humps observed by Mic 1 are absent on Day
2. Integrated OASPL levels as functions of time are shown in Figure 4.19 for each microphone for
the measurements shown in Figure 4.18. Comparison of Day 1 to Day 2 shows similar spectral
levels in terms of OASPL that remains within ± 1.3 dB. In terms of a total noise metric such as
OASPL little variability is observed, but when considering tonal and broadband noise sources
separately significant variability is observed as illustrated by comparison of the spectra in Figure
4.18. Thus, it is difficult to accurately characterize and produce repeatable measurement of the
noise from these vehicles in an outdoor environment. It is much easier to evaluate the noise of a
vehicle in a controlled laboratory setting (anechoic chamber), where the operating condition is
steady and known. It is also more time efficient to control vehicle variables such as thrust, and
thus propulsive power. If these data can be shown to be representative of hover in an open
environment, then the relatively weak dependence for low forward flight speeds indicates that
estimates of community annoyance may be produced by these measurements.
