77

Chapter 5 Aerodynamic Noise of eVTOL Rotors
In Chapter 4, noise associated with the total vehicle was discussed. It was shown that both
the tonal and broadband components are important and must be considered when analyzing the
total noise of these vehicles. To fully understand the radiated noise the individual sources from a
single isolated rotor must be characterized. The effects facilities have on the measured noise must
also be assessed to ensure fair comparison between other experimental and computational studies.
Therefore, this chapter focuses solely on the performance and noise of each of the isolated rotors
previously mentioned in Chapter 3. First, the noise of the 9450S and 2170R rotors are analyzed
when operating at static conditions in the anechoic chamber and drone park. These are then
compared to measurements that were performed in the anechoic open jet. Through this comparison
the effects each facility has on the analysis of the tonal and broadband sources are analyzed. The
tonal and broadband sources of the 9450S, CF30x10.5, and VT Swept rotor are then characterized
from measurements conducted in the open jet only. In this facility, the effects of increasing inflow
speed, rotational rate, and yaw on the measured noise of the 9450S rotor are first assessed. This is
followed by a comparison of the aerodynamic performance of the CF30x10.5 and VT Swept rotors
operating at advance ratios of 0 ≤ 𝐽 ≤ 0.5. The radiated noise of these larger rotors is then
compared at static conditions to validate the design of a low noise VT Swept rotor. These
experimental results are then compared to aerodynamic performance and noise predictions using
the methods outlined previously in Chapter 2.
Aerodynamic performance predictions are presented for the 9450S rotor using the BEMT
model. First the effects of the correction factors, previously mentioned in Chapter 2, on the
predicted aerodynamic performance are discussed. After this, results using the fully corrected
BEMT model are discussed for advance ratios of 0 ≤ 𝐽 ≤ 0.5. Predictions of tonal noise at the
BPF due to steady loading and thickness noise are performed using F1A and compared to the
extracted tonal spectra using aerodynamic inputs from the fully corrected BEMT model for the
9450S rotor operating at approximate hover RPM and inflow speeds of 0 m/s, 2, m/s, 5, m/s, and
8 m/s. These same predictions are conducted for the 9450S rotor operating at multiple RPM with
a constant inflow speed of 2 m/s. Self-noise predictions are then conducted with the 9450S rotor
operating at the same hover RPM and inflow speeds as those conducted for the tonal noise
predictions. These predictions are compared to the extracted broadband spectra that were separated
using the phase averaging interpolation technique. Lastly, predictions of tonal noise associated
with steady loading and blade thickness of the CF30x10.5 and the VT Swept rotor are conducted
and compared to the measured. These predictions use two different sets of aerodynamic parameters
as inputs to F1A. The first set is computed using the fully corrected BEMT model. The other set
of aerodynamic parameters are from the high fidelity CFD calculations of Pisharoti and Brizzolara
[95].

5.1 Investigation of Facility Effects at Static Conditions
5.1.1 Confined Anechoic and Open Environment
The average thrust and torque of the 9450S and 2170R rotors are plotted as a function of
RPM for all measurements conducted in the anechoic chamber and drone park and are shown in
78

Figure 5.1 and Figure 5.2. The dashed black lines on each figure indicate the takeoff thrust needed
for a single rotor installed on the Phantom 4 and Matrice 600 Pro drones which are 3.38 N and
15.53 N, respectively. For the 9450S rotor, the thrust and torque values compare well up to
approximately 6000 RPM. The differences observed at higher rotational speeds is due to unsteady
inflow conditions and the lack of precise motor control at the highest rotational speeds. For the
2170R rotor both thrust and torque compare well at all RPM in each facility. Overall, the quality
and controllability of the DJI E2000 motor used to power the larger 2170R rotor is superior to the
DJI 23 2312S used for the smaller 9450S. Figure 5.3 shows the average acoustic spectra measured
by Mic 2 in the anechoic chamber for both rotors at their take-off thrust. These measurements were
conducted without screens positioned in the wake to delay the onset of recirculation as discussed
previously in Chapter 3 Section 3.3.1.

Figure 5.1 - 9450S rotor performance vs RPM in anechoic chamber and drone park a) Thrust b)
Torque

Figure 5.2 – 2170R rotor performance vs RPM in anechoic chamber and drone park a) Thrust b)
Torque

79

Figure 5.3 – Mic 2 average acoustic spectra at approximate take-off thrust, 2170R (blue) and
9450S (red)
The 2170R and 9450S rotors are dominated by tonal noise from the BPF to 30×BPF
respectively. A strong tone at the shaft rate is present for the 9450S rotor as indicated by the first
spectral peak in the red spectrum above. Increased noise at this frequency is postulated to be from
the rotor being imbalanced. At higher frequencies, >3 kHz for both rotors, the broadband noise
dominates. The remaining analysis focuses on the noise produced at the take-off condition for the
9450S and 2170R rotors. First, the effects of recirculation inside the anechoic chamber are
investigated. It was shown in Stephenson et al. [18] that the acoustic effects of reingestion of the
turbulent wake are most prominent for observer locations well outside of the rotor plane.
Spectrograms were also presented showing that once recirculation was established the amplitudes
of the higher harmonic content were increased more than 15 dB. Given that Mic 2 on the line array
was the farthest out of the rotor plane, it was chosen for this analysis. A spectrogram of the 9450S
rotor operating at 5244 RPM and thrust of 3.26 N with no screens in the wake is shown in Figure
5.4.

80

Figure 5.4 – Spectrogram of Mic 2 for 9450S rotor with no screens
The spectrogram is dominated by tones of the BPF (175 Hz) and its harmonics up until
~3.3 kHz. Above this frequency, the broadband noise becomes dominant. The BPF of
approximately 175 Hz is clearly defined immediately after the rotor spins up to the desired
rotational velocity 4.65 seconds into the acquisition. The BPF and its harmonics were shown to
fluctuate in amplitude with time but do not reveal a clear instant when recirculation starts to occur
like observed in the results of Stephenson et al. [18]. Therefore, the spectral levels at the BPF and
its harmonics were observed independently with varying averaging time. This averaging time is
the total time considered in the calculation of spectra. By increasing the averaging time, more data
are considered in the analysis further from the time of the initial spin up. These integrated spectral
quantities were calculated over a 28 Hz band centered on the BPF and its corresponding harmonics.
Data before the initial startup were not considered in this average. As the averaging time increases
the number of records performed when computing narrowband spectra increases thus statistically
increasing the certainty of the measured noise. Given this, uncertainties were computed for each
averaging time window as follows. First, narrowband spectra were computed for the longest
averaging time of 28 seconds. The standard deviation was then computed at each frequency for all
records. Then the spectral uncertainty for the 𝑖𝑡ℎ FFT averaging time was computed as 𝜖𝑖 =
2𝜎⁄√𝑁𝑖 , where 𝑁𝑖 is the number of records for the 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 16, 20, and 28 second
averaging times. Given that these data were sampled at 65536 Hz and processed with a record
length of 16384, the number of records from the shortest to longest averaging times were 15, 31,
47, 63, 79, 95, 127, 159, 223. The 𝑖𝑡ℎ pressure spectrum fluctuation from the mean, 𝜖𝑓𝑙𝑢𝑥,𝑖 , was
then computed by subtracting that of the 𝑖𝑡ℎ averaging time from that of the total measurement.
Lastly, these quantities were converted to dB and subtracted from the power spectral density in
dB/Hz of the total time window.
The BPF and its first five harmonics for different total FFT averaging times are shown in
Figure 5.5. The uncertainties in the tonal levels for each time window are indicated by the colored
vertical lines overlayed on each of the markers. The spectral level at the BPF and its harmonics
are immediately observed to increase with time. As shown uncertainties decrease with increasing
81

FFT averaging times. Error bars have been plotted on the curve of the tone at the BPF and are
initially under 2 dB and decrease in width with increasing spectral averaging times. The BPF
increases by nearly 3 dB between the 2 s and 4 s sample window. The 1st, 2nd, and 3rd harmonics
increase more dramatically initially while the 4th and 5th exhibit a more gradual rise. Interestingly,
the noise at the 1st harmonic was observed to dip around 10 s and then rise again afterwards. The
BPF and its harmonics are all shown to increase by at least 7 dB with increasing averaging time
suggesting that the turbulent wake is being reingested. Additionally, the noise appears to be
converging towards a steady state after approximately 20s of run time. The method proposed by
Whiteside et al. [19] was examined to delay the onset of recirculation in the chamber. In this, they
positioned a series of screens 5 and 9 rotor radii in the wake of the rotor to dissipate and slow the
onset of observed recirculation effects in the chamber. Figure 5.6 shows the 9450S rotor operating
at 5202 RPM and corresponding thrust of 3.13 N with the addition of screens positioned 4 rotor
radii apart to delay the observed effects of recirculation in the chamber. This method was shown
to significantly delay the increase of spectral levels for the higher harmonics in Whiteside et al.
[19]. Unlike the configuration without screens, the BPF is relatively steady up to 6 seconds into
the run, although, the magnitude of the BPF is initially higher (~2dB) than without screens. In fact,
the magnitude of the spectral value at the BPF is the same in both cases after 6s with and without
screens. The first and second harmonic also exhibit steadiness at low averaging times, but the level
of higher harmonics increases immediately. The spectral levels in Figure 5.5 and Figure 5.6 are
consistent at the highest averaging time except for notable differences in the 3rd and 5th harmonics.
It appears that the BPF is relatively unaffected by recirculation as it remains consistent throughout
the duration of the measurement. Even at the lowest FFT averaging window of 2 seconds with 15
records the uncertainty is under 1 dB. The effect of the screen position was evaluated by increasing
the separation distance between the coarse and fine mesh screens to 7 rotor radii. Figure 5.7 shows
the 9450S rotor operating at 5231RPM and 3.20 N of thrust with the screens 7 radii apart.

Figure 5.5 – 9450S BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time in anechoic chamber
with no varying solidity screens in the rotor wake

82

Figure 5.6 - 9450S BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time in anechoic chamber
with 4 radii screen spacing

Figure 5.7 - 9450S BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time in anechoic chamber
with 7 radii screen spacing
The spectral level at the BPF is approximately constant for the first 8 seconds but increases
after this. Uncertainties at the BPF are also under 1 dB for all time windows. Again, the spectral
levels for each condition with and without screens are consistent at the BPF after approximately 4
s. The effect of the screens on the harmonics is less consistent. Their starting spectral level differs
in each case, but they consistently rise without an obvious cut-on point. Changes in the initial
spectral level may primarily be an effect of the low number of spectral averages. The harmonics
do not appear to remain constant for any notable duration of time and end at a similar spectral level
83

within 2dB for each measurement shown in Figures 5.5-5.7. None of the results showed a clear
delay like observed by Whiteside et al. [19]. It is likely that an effective screen configuration is
rotor and anechoic chamber dependent.
The effectiveness of the screens was also evaluated for the larger 2170R rotor. Figure 5.8
shows the 2170R rotor operating in the anechoic chamber at 2707 RPM yielding 15.29 N of thrust
with no screens in the wake. Figure 5.9 and Figure 5.10 show the rotor with screens located
downstream with separation distances of 4 and 6 radii. For these cases the 2170R rotor was
operating at 2696 RPM and 2716 RPM producing thrust of 15.21 N and 15.44 N respectively. Note
that the BPF at these rotational speeds is just below the anechoic limit of the chamber, 100 Hz.

Figure 5.8 - 2170R BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time in anechoic chamber
without varying solidity screens in its wake

Figure 5.9 – 2170R BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time in anechoic chamber
with 4 radii screen spacing
84

Figure 5.10 – 2170R BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time in anechoic
chamber with 6 radii screen spacing
The BPF and first five harmonics are all observed to start at different spectral levels. The
harmonics all increase with time, but the BPF is nearly flat over the observed period. Given that
the 2170R rotor is almost twice the diameter of the 9450S rotor, recirculation effects are expected
to be significant with this rotor, but results indicate that the screens have no beneficial effect on
recirculation and turbulence ingestion. In all cases, the final spectral levels for the BPF and
harmonics are similar, independent of the screen configuration in the chamber.
The same measurements for the 9450S and 2170R rotors were conducted in an open
environment at the Virginia Tech Drone Park for comparison. For these measurements, the rotors
are in an unconfined volume but are subject to environmental disturbances. In addition, there are
background noise sources. The microphones are positioned in the same relative locations to the
rotor for direct comparison. Figure 5.11 shows spectrograms from Mic 2 of the 9450S and 2170R
rotors in operation at the drone park. For these measurements the 9450S rotor produced 3.21 N of
thrust at 5304 RPM and the 2170R rotor produced 16.13 N of thrust at 2701 RPM. Tonal
amplitudes produced by both rotors fluctuate with time, which is possibly due to interaction with
wind gusts. Both spectrograms show strong background noise below approximately 200 Hz which
obscures the measurement at the BPF.

85

Figure 5.11 - Spectrograms for 9450S rotor (left) and 2170R rotor (right) at drone park
Figure 5.12 shows the normalized acoustic spectra of the 2170R and 9450S rotors and the
background noise present in the drone park for Mic 2. The background noise is significant up to
500 Hz, but overall the observed spectral characteristics are similar to that of the measurements
conducted in the anechoic chamber for each rotor.

Figure 5.12 - Average acoustic spectra for Mic 2 of the 2170R (red) and 9450S (blue) rotors
tested at the drone park at approximate take-off thrust
Figure 5.13 shows the BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time for the 9450S rotor
operating in the drone park at 5304 RPM producing 3.20 N of thrust. The BPF (177 Hz) remains
consistent at approximately 56 dB throughout the acquisition, but at this frequency, the rotor noise
is barely perceptible above the background noise and may affect the integrated levels. The higher
order harmonics initially rise but become approximately constant for FFT time windows greater
than 12 seconds. The spectral amplitudes are much more consistent than the measurements in the
86

anechoic chamber. Interestingly, the final spectral levels are similar in magnitude to the values
computed for the maximum FFT averaging time in the anechoic chamber where reingestion of the
turbulent wake is expected to influence the results. Similarly, Figure 5.14 shows the results for the
2170R rotor operating at 2701 RPM and 16.13 N of thrust.

Figure 5.13 - 9450S BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time in drone park

Figure 5.14 – 2170R BPF and first five harmonics as a function of FFT time in drone park
The BPF (90 Hz) and harmonics all remain approximately the same amplitude for the total
run duration. Although the BPF is at a lower frequency, the spectral levels of the rotor noise at this
frequency are ~10 dB greater than measurement of the background noise. This is significantly
louder than observed in the anechoic chamber by 15 dB respectively. Results at these low
87

frequencies should be evaluated with skepticism since the comparison may be affected by
environmental conditions and/or inaccurate representation of the background at the specific
moment of the measurement. The harmonics compare better with the results from the anechoic
chamber although the 1st harmonic is reduced by ~4 dB in the outdoor environment when
compared with measurements in the anechoic chamber for the full 28 second average. Others are
within 2-3 dB of the final spectral levels in the anechoic chamber.
Spectral levels were evaluated at all microphone positions considering different windows
of data to investigate changes in the directivity associated with changes in the operational
environment. Figure 5.15 shows directivity plots, for Mics 2 through 11 considering the overall
sound pressure level, the sound pressure level of the BPF, and its 1st harmonic for the 9450S rotor
operating at take-off thrust. The overall sound pressure was computed by integrating from 14 Hz
below the BPF to 20 kHz. The BPF and its 1st harmonic were integrated over the same 28 Hz band
centered on each tone as before.

Figure 5.15 - Directivity of 9450S OASPL (left), SPL BPF (middle), and SPL 1st harmonic
(right)
The results presented for the anechoic chamber were from measurements with the varying
solidity screens spaced 4 radii apart. Results are presented for both 4 and 28 second FFT total
average times. The OASPL for Mics 8 through 11 in the drone park, near the blade disk plane at
0° receiver angle, are similar in magnitude when compared to that of the 4 and 28 second FFT
window from the anechoic chamber. For observer angels between 33 degrees and 72 degrees the
28 second window are all slightly elevated from the 4 second window with Mic 2, near 90° on the
rotor’s axis of rotation, displaying an increased level of 1.6 dB. This agrees with the results
presented in Stephensen et al. [18] where microphones furthest out of the plane experienced the
greatest increase in amplitude due to the reingestion of the turbulent wake. The OASPL
directionality for the measurement conducted in the drone park exhibits a similar overall shape for
𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≤ 56°. Furthest from the rotor plane, the OASPL is increased by at least 5 dB when compared
to those of the 28 second averaging window in the anechoic chamber. The SPL of the blade passage
frequency exhibits the same shape for the 4 and 28 second FFT averaging periods, but Mic 2 shows
an increase of 6.2 dB in the downstream location near the axis of rotation for the 28 second average.
The SPL around the BPF exhibits a different directivity in the drone park. The noise in the disk
plane is reduced and near the axis of rotation it is increased although background noise may
influence these results. It is interesting to note the difference at the in-plane location. This
88

difference was unexpected since unsteady loading effects influence the noise closer to the axis of
rotation. For the 1st harmonic, there is more disagreement between the 4 and 28 second averaging
periods in the anechoic chamber. They both show an increase in spectral level closer to the axis of
rotation. The 28 second averaging period has levels more similar to those for measurements
conducted in the drone park, although they are still 10 dB below those from measurements in the
anechoic chamber. In all three directivity plots, measurements in the anechoic chamber, regardless
of averaging periods, are consistent in plane but differ as the observer position moves around
toward the axis of rotation. This indicates that the source of the diskrepancy is associated with
unsteady loading sources, which radiate primarily along the rotor axis and vary with operating
time in the anechoic chamber. The RPM and thrust time histories for the 9450S rotor operating in
the drone park and the anechoic chamber without the screens is shown in Figure 5.16. Comparison
of RPM as a function of time shows that of the drone park to fluctuate by approximately 300 RPM.
As for the measured thrust, that of the anechoic chamber remains consistent, whereas that of the
drone park shows impulse like increases in thrust throughout the measurement. These fluctuations
in thrust are attributed to unsteady wind gusts that were present during the measurement.

Figure 5.16 – 9450S rotor RPM and thrust steady state time histories from the anechoic chamber
with no screens in the downstream wake
Figure 5.17 shows the OASPL and SPL of the BPF and 1st harmonic for the 2170R rotor operating
at take-off thrust. The OASPL results in the anechoic chamber for both the 4 and 28 second FFT
averaging periods exhibit the same shape and nearly the same amplitude. Directivities for
measurements conducted in the drone park are somewhat similar in shape although the levels at
each microphone are increased by approximately 3 dB when compared to those of the anechoic
chamber. This increase in spectral levels may be attributed to the presence of background noise at
the lowest frequencies of the integration range which was 14 Hz below the BPF to 20 kHz. The
spectral levels are greatest near the blade disk plane at the BPF in the anechoic chamber. In the
drone park, the spectral levels are nearly constant in all directions at the BPF, this is due to the
presence of similar levels of background noise at low frequencies. At the 1 st harmonic, the noise
radiates primarily along the axis of rotation for the 28 second averaging time and drone park
measurements. Like the 9450S measurements, the increased averaging time for anechoic chamber
measurements tends to increase levels along the axis of rotation while in the blade disk plane
remains constant.

Figure 5.17 - Directivity of 2170R OASPL (left), SPL BPF (middle), and SPL 1 st harmonic

As stated previously, the variability of the measured thrust, torque, and noise was also
assessed. To analyze this, the standard deviation was computed for the steady state operation of
the measured RPM, thrust, and torque for each rotor. The standard deviation was also computed
for the BPF and its first five harmonics. Throughout each measurement the rotational rate of each
rotor varies, thus causing tones associated with the BPF and its harmonics to wander across
frequency. Given this, the standard deviation was computed as illustrated by the spectrogram
shown in Figure 5.18. This spectrogram shows the steady operating period for the 9450S rotor in
the anechoic chamber from Mic 2 which was shown previously in Figure 5.4. First, the measured
RPM time history was interpolated onto the same time series as the spectrogram. The BPF and
higher order harmonics are then tracked throughout the measurement using the interpolated RPM
as indicated by the green markers. Integration is then performed over a 30 Hz band at each instance
in time as indicated by the upper and lower black lines that encase each tone. This yields the SPL
as a function of time which is shown in Figure 5.19(a). A moving average is then used over a time
window of 3 seconds indicated by the green line in Figure 5.19(a). This moving average is then
subtracted from the measured SPL yielding the fluctuation of SPL as a function of time as shown
Figure 5.19(b).

throughout the measurement

Figure 5.19 – SPL at BPF as a function of time a) SPL at BPF throughout measurement (black),
moving average with window size of 3 seconds (green) b) SPL at BPF throughout measurement
less the moving average

5.1.2 Anechoic Open Jet
The average thrust and torque of the 9450S and 2170R rotors plotted as a function of RPM
for all measurements conducted in the Virginia Tech anechoic open jet without inflow are shown
in Figure 5.20 and Figure 5.21. For comparison the measured thrust and torque from measurements
conducted in the anechoic chamber and drone park are shown by the grey markers. For the 9450S
rotor, the thrust and torque values are within 1 N and 0.1 N⋅m up to approximately 6000 RPM.
Measured torque for the 2170R rotor in the open jet shows excellent agreement when compared to
measurements in the anechoic chamber and drone park. Overall, the measured thrust for each rotor
is slightly less than that from the measurements conducted in the anechoic chamber and drone
park. For this measurement campaign tare files were taken at the beginning of an RPM sweep. The
rotor was brought up to its steady spin state using the Matlab GUI previously mentioned in Chapter
3 Section 3.6.3. Once the desired rotational rate was achieved, the acquisition of noise and force
data started. Achieving the desired RPM took longer at times than others. Given this the tare values
with each rotor may have deviated slightly. Nonetheless, the measured thrust and torque for the
2170R rotor compare well up to approximately 3200 RPM. Spectrograms from Mic 1 in the open
jet tests are shown in Figure 5.21. The 9450S rotor is shown in Figure 5.22(a) and the 2170R rotor
is shown in Figure 5.22(b). Strong tones are present at the BPF and its higher order harmonics up
until approximately 8 kHz for the 9450S rotor. Above this the broadband noise dominates. Tonal
amplitudes are also shown to fluctuate in amplitude as they did in the anechoic chamber and drone
park. For the 2170R rotor strong tones are present from the BPF up until approximately 2.7 kHz.
Above 2.7 kHz, the acoustic signature becomes dominated by broadband noise. For both rotors
the tones remain consistent in frequency. Some background noise is present below 150 Hz which
is below the anechoic limit of the facility of 400 Hz.

93

5.1.3 Separation of Tonal and Broadband Components
The periodic and random noise components are separated in the time domain using a phase
average interpolation technique like that of Zawodny and Boyd [6]. An overview of this technique
using the RPM signal and measured acoustic pressure time history from a single microphone with
the 9450S rotor operating at 5715 RPM is outlined as follows. First the rotor position is tracked in
time using the measured pulse from the RPM signal acquired with the laser diode. This pulse signal
is shown in black in Figure 5.23. By selecting a well-defined point on the square wave which
indicates a rotor blade cutting through the photodiode, the absolute rotor position can be defined.
The point used to define the rotor location is indicated by the magenta dots, where 𝑇𝑟𝑒𝑣 is the time,
it takes for a single blade to complete a full revolution. 𝑇𝑟𝑒𝑣 is not constant as the RPM varies
throughout the measurement which requires the measured acoustic pressure time history to be
interpolated onto an evenly spaced grid of 𝑛𝑖𝑛𝑡 points. To ensure minimal losses of frequency
60
content within the broadband component 𝑛𝑖𝑛𝑡 = 3 ∗ (𝑅𝑃𝑀) for every 𝑁𝑟𝑒𝑣 interpolation region.
These interpolations are indicated by the grey lines shown in Figure 5.24. An average of these is
then computed which yields the phase averaged acoustic pressure shown in red. This resultant
phase averaged acoustic pressure is then replicated 𝑁𝑟𝑒𝑣 times yielding the mean acoustic pressure
rotor revolution time history. The total acoustic pressure is then rebuilt by reassembling the 𝑁𝑟𝑒𝑣
rotor revolution acoustic pressure time histories into consecutive segments. The phased averaged
acoustic pressure is then replicated 𝑁𝑟𝑒𝑣 times to ensure that each of the residual time histories
were processed with a common frequency resolution [6]. The mean acoustic pressure rotor
revolution time history is then subtracted from that of the total acoustic pressure time history
yielding the broadband component. This results in acoustic pressure time histories of the tonal and
broadband components. Note that the phase-averaging procedure uses the rotor blade location
measured by the optical RPM sensor and the acoustic pressure measured at the observer. These
data are not corrected to account for source emission time and do not represent the phase angle at
the time of emission. Therefore, the phase angle should only be considered as an unraveling of the
periodic component observed through one revolution rather than a result produced at any absolute
rotor phase position. These acoustic pressure time histories, shown as a function of rotor azimuthal
angle in Figure 5.25 are representative of the waveform associated with noise due to steady loading
and thickness and the broadband component for a single rotor revolution.

94

Figure 5.23 – RPM Signal with rotor blade position identified

Figure 5.24 - 𝑁𝑟𝑒𝑣 interpolated acoustic presssure time histories (grey) and resultant phase
averaged acoustic pressure (red)

95

Figure 5.25 – Tonal (a) and broadband (b) acoustic pressure time histories for one 9450S rotor
revolution at 5715 RPM
Narrowband spectra of the total, tonal, and broadband can then be computed using a
Hanning window of 50% overlap and a frequency resolution of 4 Hz. These spectral processing
parameters are equal to that of those used to compute total narrowband spectra measured from a
microphone. High frequency oscillations associated with the electric motor were included during
the initial calculations of the phase-averaged acoustic pressure and were removed by applying a
low pass filter to the mean rotor revolution time series data in the form of a moving average. The
moving average window was designed to remove these high frequency tones which corresponds
to a window size of approximately 4.2° of rotation or approximately 7144 Hz at 5715 RPM. Total
narrowband acoustic spectra along with the tonal and broadband component separated using the
phase averaging technique is shown in Figure 5.26.

Figure 5.26 – Total, tonal, and broadband spectra computed for 9450S rotor at 5715 RPM using
a phase averaging technique
96

To better understand the source of the observed noise and compare the three facilities, the total
measured acoustic spectrum is separated into broadband and tonal components using the
interpolation method. Narrowband acoustic spectra of the tonal component for measurements
conducted in all three facilities for the 9450S rotor is shown in Figure 5.27(a) and the broadband
component is shown in Figure 5.27(b). For each of these presented spectra the x-axis has been
normalized on the BPF. The observer angles were matched for direct comparison of measurements
conducted in each facility at 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 48°. For measurements in the anechoic chamber and drone
park this corresponds to Mic 5 and Mic 1 for the open jet.

Figure 5.27- Separated narrowband acoustic spectra of 9450S rotor at approximate hover thrust
in anechoic chamber, drone park, and open jet, a) tonal component, b) broadband component
For the 9450S rotor, spectral peaks are present in the spectra up to 30×BPF in the open jet.
After this the spectrum becomes dominated by broadband noise. This transition from tonal to
broadband features occurs earlier at approximately 10×BPF in the anechoic chamber and drone
park. Tonal spectral levels associated with the BPF and from 3×BPF to 10×BPF are similar
between the open jet and drone park. For measurements in the anechoic chamber, tonal spectral
levels at the BPF are more than 5 dB greater than the other two facilities but are approximately the
same in amplitude at the 1st harmonic. The effect of increased background noise and environmental
disturbances at the drone park are evident when compared to the broadband noise. For these
measurements the spectral levels increased by 7 dB respectively from the BPF to approximately
25×BPF. In the anechoic chamber, broadband levels are like that of the open jet from the BPF to
approximately 5×BPF and above 25×BPF. For all facilities, strong discrete spectral peaks are
present in both the tonal and broadband spectrum from 6×BPF to 8×BPF. A dominant tone is also
present at the 21st harmonic of the BPF for measurements in the open jet. Lastly, a dominant tone
at approximately 103×BPF is present for measurements in all three facilities and is equal to the
switching frequency of the ESC which is 18 kHz. Directivity plots of the tonal component for
measurements conducted in all three facilities for the 9450S rotor is shown in Figure 5.28(a) and
the broadband component is shown in Figure 5.28(b). For downstream measurement locations the
drone park shows more broadband noise. Tonal components from measurements in the anechoic
chamber are stronger than those of the open jet and drone park for all observer positions. The open
jet (at static condition) performed well for both broadband and tonal noise at all observer positions.
The open jet tunnel seems to suppress the strong tones observed when operating in the anechoic
chamber and the broadband components observed in the outdoor environment.
97

Figure 5.28 - Directivities of 9450S rotor at approximate hover thrust in anechoic chamber,
drone park, and open jet, a) tonal component, b) broadband component
Although the open jet appeared to perform well when considering the smaller 9450S rotor,
results from the larger 2170R rotor show the opposite. Narrowband acoustic spectra of the tonal
component for measurements conducted in all three facilities for the 2170R rotor is shown in
Figure 5.29(a) and the broadband component is shown in Figure 5.29(b). For each of these
presented spectra the x axis has been normalized on the BPF. The observer angles were matched
for direct comparison of measurements conducted at each facility which corresponds to 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 =
45°. For measurements in the anechoic chamber and drone park this corresponds to Mic 5 and Mic
1 for the open jet. For the 2170R rotor, spectral levels at the BPF (92 Hz) differ by 3 dB when
compared to the drone park and by 10 dB when compared to the anechoic chamber. Tones
associated with the higher order harmonics are greater for the open jet when compared to the other
two facilities. Broadband levels in the open jet are similar in magnitude to those of the drone park
and are approximately 3 dB greater than those of the anechoic chamber from approximately
5×BPF to 27×BPF. The dominant tone at 192×BPF associated with the switching frequency of
the ESC is also present. All in all, the same spectral features are observed for frequencies below
32×BPF but vary significantly in amplitude. Above this to approximately 95×BPF broadband
humps appear for measurements in the open jet. In contrast, the broadband noise above 46×BPF
are similar for measurements conducted in the drone park and anechoic chamber.

98

Figure 5.29 - Separated narrowband acoustic spectra of 2170R rotor at approximate hover thrust
in anechoic chamber, drone park, and open jet, a) tonal component, b) broadband component
Directivity plots of the tonal component for measurements conducted in all three facilities for the
2170R rotor is shown in Figure 5.30(a) and the broadband component is shown in Figure 5.30(b).
The tonal noise is increased at all observer locations in the open jet primarily due to the increased
levels of the higher order harmonics in the measurement. Similarly, the broadband noise is greater
also due to an increased noise at higher harmonics of the BPF. This diskrepancy with the prior
results for the 9450S may be attributable to the large size of the rotor. The test section of the open
jet is smaller than the closed anechoic chamber. Thus, even though there is a collector intended to
diskharge flow from the chamber, significant recirculation is likely to occur and likely a factor for
the increased noise. This displays that there may not be a one-size fits all approach to testing rotors
like these at static conditions. Instead, it is best to assess the strength of higher order harmonics in
the broadband and tonal noise to determine the significance of recirculation/turbulence ingestion
on the results. In any case, the broadband features displayed in Figure 5.29(b) underneath the
harmonic peaks are similar in all three facilities.

Figure 5.30 - Directivities of 2170R rotor at approximate hover thrust in anechoic chamber,
drone park, and open jet, a) tonal component, b) broadband component

99

The corresponding standard deviations of RPM, thrust, the BPF and its first five harmonics
for each rotor operating at take-off thrust in each facility is shown in Table 5.2. The standard
deviations of the BPF and its harmonics are from Mic 1 in the open jet and Mic 5 in the anechoic
chamber and drone park. These were computed as shown previously at the end of Section 5.1.1.
As shown, the BPF is more variable in the drone park compared to the other facilities. There is
also more variation in RPM that varies the least for measurements in the open jet. Electrical noise
was found to be present in the load cell data for measurements in the open jet and is the cause for
the higher variability in thrust. If the fluctuations in thrust were 5 N like shown for the 9450S, the
noise at the BPF assuming it is dominated by steady loading and thickness would increase by
approximately 9 dB when compared to the anechoic chamber where 𝜎𝑇 = 0.61 N. Overall, it is
difficult to observe consistent trends in the higher order harmonics between the three facilities
making comparisons of the measured noise difficult.

5.2 Examination of Tonal and Broadband Components Under Controlled Inflow
Conditions
As shown, measurements of rotor noise particularly at static conditions can vary
significantly between facilities. For measurements conducted in a confined volume such as the
anechoic chamber each rotor’s wake circulates around the room and becomes re-ingested. This
results in increased broadband noise at the higher order harmonics. For measurements conducted
outdoors the background noise is significant below 200 Hz and changes throughout time. This
makes it difficult to characterize noise at low frequencies. An additional artifact of outdoor
measurements is the time variation of the flow field ingested by each rotor. This also results in
increased broadband noise at the higher order harmonics of the BPF. These factors of increased,
non-stationary background noise that occurs when conducting measurements outdoors and
recirculation that occurs in the anechoic chamber is eliminated when performing measurements
under controlled inflow conditions in a facility such as the open jet. This section focuses on
measurements of the 9450S rotor performed in the open jet. First the effects of increasing inflow
velocity on performance and noise are investigated for the 9450S rotor. These are followed by an
100

analysis of the measured noise of the 9450S rotor operating at multiple RPM with a constant tunnel
velocity of 2 m/s. Lastly, the effects of yaw with increasing inflow speeds on the measured thrust
and noise of the 9450S rotor are accessed.
5.2.1 Effects of Increasing Inflow Velocity
The effect of a mean inflow velocity on the noise produced by the 9450S rotor will be
considered in this section. The inflow speed, average RPM, thrust, and torque for each of the
presented measurements are listed in Table 5.3. Phase averaging is performed on the acoustic
pressure time history of each microphone to separate the tonal and broadband components. The
phase averaged acoustic pressure for the 9450S rotor is shown in Figure 5.31(a) as measured by
Mic 1 (𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 48°). Each color represents a different inflow speed where red is 0 m/s, blue is 2
m/s, black is 5.1 m/s and green is 8 m/s respectively. At zero inflow velocity, this rotational speed
corresponds to the approximate take-off thrust of a single 9450S rotor equipped on the DJI
Phantom 4 Quadcopter, 3.38 N. The associated broadband acoustic pressure time history of a
single rotor revolution for the same measurements are shown in Figure 5.31(b). These data do not
repeat for each revolution as the phase average solution does. Interestingly, the magnitude of the
pressure fluctuations associated with the broadband noise are greater than the phase-averaged
solution. Thus, when combined, the total acoustic pressure time history for one rotor revolution,
as shown in Figure 5.32, looks similar to the broadband noise solution.
Table 5.3 – Average RPM, thrust and torque 9450S rotor with increasing inflow velocity

Figure 5.32 – Total acoustic pressure time history at Mic 1 over one revolution for 9450S rotor
As shown, the broadband noise is clearly a significant contributor to the overall noise
produced by this rotor. Narrowband acoustic spectra for the 9450S rotor are shown in Figure 5.33
for each of the tested inflow speeds. These measurements correspond to the complete time series
of the data represented in Figure 5.31 and Figure 5.32. The BPF tone at 190 Hz varies by
approximately 4 dB for all cases and is reduced in amplitude as the tunnel speed is increased
corresponding to the decreasing thrust of the rotor. The higher order harmonics of the BPF are at
least 10 dB greater up to the 7th harmonic without the tunnel driven inflow. For cases with the
tunnel on, the tones from 8×BPF to approximately 27×BPF nearly vanish. At 𝑈∞ = 0, the rotor’s
wake was not driven downstream by the tunnel velocity. Instead, it’s possible that the wake is
recirculating in the chamber producing TIN, or the increased tonal noise may be due to BWI.
Operating the rotor at the lowest speed, 𝑈∞ = 2 m/s, appears to eliminate these effects while
providing approximately the same average thrust and torque as the 𝑈∞ = 0 m/s case. The tones at
approximately 27×BPF and 37×BPF are due to motor noise. For no inflow and 𝑈∞ = 2 m/s, broad
spectral features are similar from the BPF to approximately 20×BPF. For an inflow speed of 5
m/s, spectral levels are greater than all other cases except for the tones associated with motor noise.
Increasing the tunnel velocity to 8 m/s, spectral levels from 2×BPF to 10×BPF become similar to
the no inflow and 𝑈∞ = 2 m/s case. Above 30×BPF, the shape and amplitude of the broad spectral
features vary significantly for each of the tested inflow speeds.

102

Figure 5.33 – Total noise spectra at Mic 1 for the 9450S rotor at multiple inflow speeds
Acoustic spectra for the tonal component are shown in Figure 5.34(a) and the broadband
component is shown in Figure 5.34(b). There are clear tone-like features that rise above the
underlying broad spectral features at the BPF and its harmonics, most notably for 𝑈∞ = 0. With
flow on, the computed broadband noise at the BPF is reduced by more than 18 dB when compared
to the no inflow case. At higher frequencies there are more sharp spectral features apparent in the
computed broadband noise for 𝑈∞ = 0 m/s. Again, the presence of this noise in the broadband
calculation and its near disappearance when a small freestream velocity is forced through the test
section confirms that the noise is due to either TIN or BWI. Both inflow speeds of 5 m/s and 8 m/s
result in increased levels from the BPF to 10.5×BPF respectively when compared to the 𝑈∞ = 0
m/ and 2 m/s cases. Mixed results are shown for frequencies above 10.5×BPF. For the tonal
component shown in Figure 5.34(a), the BPF tone decreases by 4 dB with increasing inflow speed.
In comparison with Figure 5.34(b), the tonal spectra for 𝑈∞ = 0 fall below the sharp peaks present
in the broadband calculation for frequencies above the BPF. For measurements with a freestream
velocity, the computed tones are strong enough to rise above the broad spectral features up to
5×BPF. For all measurements with a tunnel driven inflow, the spectral peak at the BPF of 190 Hz
in the broadband spectra is reduced by more than 8 dB. Additionally, strong spectral peaks at the
higher order harmonics of the BPF are present for the no inflow case. These vanish for all cases
with inflow suggesting that the increased higher order harmonic content of the total noise spectrum
in Figure 5.33 is indeed due to blade wake interaction or turbulence ingestion, both broadband
sources. Together these data show that the true tonal noise, produced by steady loading and
thickness noise, is the dominant source responsible for noise at the BPF. Although, at higher
frequencies, the broadband components which can appear tonal are dominant in the absence of a
mean axial flow.

103

Figure 5.34 – Separated noise spectra at Mic 1 for 9450S rotor at multiple inflow speeds a) tonal
b) broadband
5.2.2 Effects of Increasing Rotational Rate
In this section the tonal and broadband noise associated with the 9450S rotor is assessed
for increasing rotational rates with a constant tunnel velocity of 2 m/s. The average RPM, thrust
and torque from each measurement are listed in Table 5.4. Narrowband acoustic spectra with the
frequency normalized on the BPF are shown in Figure 5.35. At the BPF, spectral levels increase
by 9 dB over the considered range of RPM. Spectral levels at 2×BPF increase by approximately
18 dB with increasing RPM. From 24×BPF to 57×BPF, a broadband noise hump is present for
the two lowest rotational rates. Strong tones are also present at 7×BPF, 21×BPF and 28×BPF. As
shown previously in Section 5.2.1 noise generated aerodynamically at the higher order harmonics
with tunnel driven flow is the result of either TIN or BWI sources. Given that the tunnel was on
for these measurements these tones are attributed to the brushless motor.
Table 5.4 – Average RPM, thrust and torque 9450S rotor with increasing RPM

Figure 5.35 – Total narrowband acoustic spectra from Mic 1 in the open jet of the 9450S rotor
with increasing RPM
As the brushless DC motor operates a time varying magnetic field produces radial and
tangential forces. These forces cause structural vibrations and ultimately lead to acoustic radiation.
In Lee et al. [78], it was shown that noise sources can be directly related to the rotational rate of
the brushless motor. They showed that the noise generated by a brushless DC motor would produce
frequency components associated with the local force, 𝑓𝑙𝑓 , the torque ripple, 𝑓𝑡𝑟 , and the cogging
torque, 𝑓𝑐𝑡 . Henderson et al.[30], also showed frequency components of the noise radiated by a
brushless DC motor to appear at the fundamental line frequency 𝑓𝑙 and its harmonics (See Chapter
6 Section 6.1). Each of these frequency components are a function of RPM, number of slots, and
number of poles on the electric motor. For these measurements the DJI 2312S brushless motor was
used and consisted of 14 poles and 12 slots. Narrowband spectra plotted from 500 Hz to 7 kHz for
each of the presented cases above is shown in Figure 5.36. For each case, frequencies associated
with the local force, torque ripple, and the cogging torque are labeled with the black arrows.

105

Figure 5.36 – Narrowband spectra of 9450S rotor from Mic 1 with tones associated with the DJI
2312S motor identified a) 4925 RPM b) 5631 RPM c) 6774 RPM
106

For each case the second harmonic of the fundamental line frequency and the loading force
frequency are equal to 7×BPF. At 21×BPF, spectral levels are increased and correspond to 𝑓𝑡𝑟 as
well as 5× 𝑓𝑙 , and 3× 𝑓𝑙𝑓 . At higher frequencies, a strong tone is present at 28×BPF and is equal
to the 7th harmonic of 𝑓𝑙 and the 3rd harmonic of 𝑓𝑙𝑓 . As shown here, when these tones are coupled
with aerodynamically or mechanically generated tones of the rotor, the noise is increased. At the
highest rotational rate, a strong tone also appears at 6.5×BPF, that does not align with those
associated with either aerodynamic or electromagnetic sources. In Henderson et al. [30], resonant
frequencies of a DJI 2212 brushless motor were examined through FEA analysis. They showed
these frequencies to occur at 1390 Hz and 4650 Hz. Given this, it is postulated that the tone at
6.5×BPF is indeed due to the vibrations of the motor structure itself. The phased averaged acoustic
pressure and the broadband response for a single rotor revolution for Mic 1 at each condition is
shown in Figure 5.37. The magnitude of the phase averaged acoustic pressure increases with
rotational speed, but the form of these results looks slightly different than those observed in
Zawodny and Boyd [6]. These authors presented phase-averaged acoustic time series spectra
which have two distinct smoothly varying humps corresponding to each blade passage. In Figure
5.37, there are two humps, but they are unequal suggesting some imbalance in the system.
Comparison of the broadband component at each RPM shows increased fluctuations with
increasing RPM. The fluctuations of the broadband acoustic pressure can be assessed by
computing the respective mean square for each RPM. Computing these yields 0.01 𝑃𝑎2 , 0.016
𝑃𝑎2 , and 0.012 𝑃𝑎2 for 4925 RPM, 5631 RPM, and 6774 RPM, which implies that the broadband
levels increase with increasing RPM. The extracted acoustic spectra of the tonal and broadband
components for each measurement are shown in Figure 5.38. At the BPF, spectral levels of the
tonal component are at least 6 dB greater than that of the broadband component. For RPM ≤ 5631,
the 1st, 7th and 21st harmonics of the BPF in the tonal component are greater than the broadband.
As shown previously, these are tones associated with electromagnetic sources. At 6.5×BPF the
strong tone possibly associated with the resonant frequency of the motor housing are present for
the 6774 RPM case and exceeds the broadband a noise by approximately 10 dB. Above 16× BPF
spectral levels of the broadband component exceed those of the tonal. Although the tip velocities
vary from 62 m/s to 85 m/s the broad spectral features remain within 5 dB above 4.5×BPF
respectively. At frequencies below this to approximately 2×BPF the broadband noise increases
for the 6774 RPM measurement by approximately 8 dB when compared to those for 4925 and
5631 RPM.

107

Figure 5.37 – Tonal and broadband response from Mic 1 in the open jet of 9450S rotor with
increasing RPM a) Phase averaged acoustic pressure, b) 1/rev broadband response

Figure 5.38 - Tonal and broadband spectra from Mic 1 in the open jet of 9450S rotor with
increasing RPM a) Tonal, b) Broadband
Directivity plots of the BPF extracted from the phase-averaged tonal noise spectra for each
measurement are shown in Figure 5.39. For the lowest rotational speed, the noise peaks near
𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 39°. For 5631 RPM, the peak levels in directivity shift to 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 29°. Increasing the
rotational rate further to 6774 RPM, peak levels shift back to 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 39° and a minimum appears
in the directivity at 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 29°. Overall, noise at the BPF increases with increasing RPM below
the blade disk. Near the rotor plane, there is a distinct increase in spectral levels at the BPF when
increasing RPM from 4925 to 5631 that is not present for the highest RPM case. Similar trough
like features were observed in Zawodny et al. [2] at the first harmonic for a similar rotor. Though,
these features were attributed to the out of phase components of loading and thickness noise. The
directivity angle of the trough in their measurements moved towards the axis of rotation with
increasing RPM. They showed it to have greater effect for positions above the rotor disk plane
𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 < 0 rather than below 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 > 0 where our microphones are located.

108

Figure 5.39 - Directivity of BPF for increasing RPM for 9450S rotor
Contour plots of the broadband spectra for all observer positions are shown in Figure 5.40. At 4925
RPM, broadband noise increases from 21×BPF to 71×BPF for 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≥ 25°. This region of noise
becomes more dispersed across more frequencies with increasing RPM and the spectral levels
increase in amplitude towards the rotor plane. At the lowest rotational rate of 4925 RPM, spectral
levels of the lower frequency broadband noise reach a minimum for 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≤ 20°. The broadband
noise around the BPF increases with RPM. The most significant changes to broadband noise occur
closer to the rotor plane with increasing RPM.

m/s a) 4925 RPM, b) 5631 RPM, c) 6774 RPM
110

5.2.3 Effects of Yaw
To study the effects of yaw on aerodynamic performance and noise, measurements were
conducted in the open jet facility with the 9450S rotor operating with its axis of rotation at multiple
angles to the freestream and multiple inflow speeds.
5.2.3.1 Assessment of Aerodynamic Performance of a Yawed Rotor
When operating the 9450S rotor at yaw with inflow, an additional drag force acts on the
face of the load cell. Given this, additional steps had to be taken to correctly compute thrust which
acts normal to the plane of the rotor. A top and isometric view of the forces acting on the load cell
with a rotor at yaw is shown in Figure 5.41. When the load cell was installed on the test stand it
had to be rotated by an angle 𝜃 as shown on the right of Figure 5.41 where the prime superscript
indicates the measured forces, 𝐹𝑥 ′ and 𝐹𝑦 ′, in the 𝑥1 ′ and 𝑥2 ′ directions. For these measurements
the load cell rotation angle, 𝜃 was equal to 45°.

Figure 5.41 – Force diagram of load cell with rotor at yaw
Thus, the force 𝐹𝑖 measured by the load cell with respect to the standard coordinates 𝑥1 , 𝑥2 , and 𝑥3
is equal to:
𝐹𝑖 = [𝐹𝑥′ 𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜃 − 𝐹𝑦′ 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜃

𝐹𝑋′ 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜃 + 𝐹𝑦′ 𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜃

𝐹𝑧 ]

(5.1)

Two forces are present when the rotor is at yaw that act in the directions of 𝑛̂ 𝑇 and 𝑛̂𝐷 which are
normal and parallel to the disk plane, where the force acting in the direction of 𝑛̂ 𝑇 is thrust. These
are defined as:
𝑛̂ 𝑇 = [𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜙𝑝 0 𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜙𝑝 ]
𝑛̂𝐷 = [𝑐𝑜𝑠𝜙𝑝 0 𝑠𝑖𝑛𝜙𝑝 ]

(5.2)

The thrust and force parallel to the disk plane were computed by taking the dot product between
the resultant force, 𝐹𝑖 and their respective unit normal vectors above. The average RPM, thrust,
and force in the direction of 𝑛̂𝐷 for each measurement are shown in Table 5.5 where each of the
111

colored cells in the top row indicates the tunnel velocity of the open jet. These forces are also
shown as a function of yaw angle 𝜙𝑝 in Figure 5.42

Figure 5.42 – Thrust and 𝐹 ⋅ 𝑛̂𝐷 as a function of yaw angle for 9450S rotor (𝑈∞ = 0 m/s (red),
𝑈∞ = 2 m/s (red), 𝑈∞ = 5 m/s (black), 𝑈∞ = 8 m/s (green)
To validate the computed rotation of the measurement forces, thrust was calculated at zero
inflow speed for all yaw angles. This value should ideally be invariant but displays an
approximately 12% maximum variation across all yaw angles and is 0.5 N greater for the 𝜙𝑝 = 0°.
Increasing the tunnel velocity at zero yaw tends to reduce thrust as expected. With inflow, thrust
is reduced for 𝜙𝑝 ≤ 20° and then slowly converges to the mean value for the zero-tunnel velocity
as the yaw angle approaches 90°. The force acting parallel to the disk plane increases up to yaw
angles of 50° at which it becomes maximum. Increasing the yaw angle further the magnitude is
reduced. The force acting in the disk plane for no yaw and 𝜙𝑝 = 90° are equal. At the largest yaw
angle the disk force is increased with the addition of inflow. For 10° ≤ 𝜙𝑝 ≤ 70°, the force in the
112

direction of 𝑛̂𝐷 is reduced in magnitude with the additions of flow. In edgewise flight, 𝜙𝑝 = 90°,
one blade is advancing into the freestream while the other is retreating. For these measurements
the rotor was spinning clockwise into the incoming flow thus increasing the force that acts in the
rotor plane in edgewise flight. There is also a greater possibility for blade wake interaction.
Therefore, the radiated noise from a rotor operating at an angle of yaw is expected to change. To
assess the effects of yaw on the radiated noise, Figure 5.43 shows contours of sound pressure level
as a function of 𝜙𝑝 and frequency. Presented acoustic data are from Mics 1, 7 and 12 for 𝑈∞ = 0
and 8 m/s. Each contour plot contains a dashed line which indicates the in-plane observer location,
𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 0°, for each yaw angle and observer location. For 𝜙𝑝 = 0, each microphone is at a position
below the rotor disk plane. As 𝜙𝑝 increases, the observer locations move to positions over the top
of the rotor disk plane. Here the acoustic spectra for the no inflow case are representative of a
sweep through directivity angles.

113

Figure 5.43 - Contours of SPL the 9450S rotor as function of yaw angle, 𝜙𝑝 and frequency, a)
Mic 12, b) Mic 7, c) Mic 1 (left column - 𝑈∞ = 0 m/s. right column - 𝑈∞ = 8 m/s)
As observed previously, the tonal content at all microphones is reduced when there is a
freestream velocity in the tunnel for zero yaw angle. For all microphones, the noise between
5×BPF and 30×BPF is a minimum around 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 0°. As the yaw angle increases beyond 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 =
0°, the observer position moves over the top of the rotor disk and the rotor is subject to increased
edgewise flow. All observer positions measure increased low frequency tones as the yaw angle
increased beyond 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 0°. There is also a significant increase in broadband noise. Above the
rotor plane, all microphones show increased high frequency broadband noise above 30×BPF at all
yaw angles. As yaw approaches 90°, the broadband noise in this range increases by approximately
114

10 dB for Mic 12. In addition, Mics 7 and 12 show increased lower frequency broadband noise at
frequencies below 30×BPF. For all microphones, as the yaw angle is reduced, and the
microphones move below the rotor there is a narrow region of increased broadband noise between
80 and 100×BPF with the flow on. At Mic 1, for yaw angles less than 30 degrees, the broadband
noise increases by approximately 15 dB at 𝑈∞ = 8 m/s.

5.3 Validation of a Quiet Propeller Design
In this section the aerodynamic performance and noise of a newly designed swept rotor,
identified as VT Swept, is compared to that of the commercially available CF30x10.5. First, the
aerodynamic performance of each rotor operating in the open jet is analyzed over multiple advance
ratios. After this, the radiated noise from each rotor is analyzed at the respective take-off thrust of
31.4 N. This take-off thrust corresponds to that of a single rotor installed on a concept design
detailed by Bershadsky [96]. The VT Swept rotor was designed at Virginia Tech with the goal of
achieving the same performance characteristics as the CF30x10.5 rotor, while producing less
noise. The VT swept rotor is composed of NACA 16 series blade sections and has the same
diameter as the CF30x10.5. The thickness of each blade section is also the same and the chord
distribution is similar. The design parameter of sweep causes acoustic signals from the tip to lag
in phase and interfere with signals from other radii and has also been shown to be effective at
reducing the spanwise coherence of the ingested turbulence. Both result in reduced noise, in
particular noise from sources associated with turbulence ingestion which are especially important
for eVTOL aircraft in hover. These results are presented knowing that the presented rotors are
beyond an appropriate size for the open-jet wind tunnel in which they were tested. The rotor
diameter is the same as the square dimension of the face of the open jet inlet. Nevertheless, these
results will show a noticeable noise reduction while highlighting some of the challenges and effects
of testing rotors that push beyond the appropriate size limitations of the test facility.
5.3.1 Aerodynamic Performance
Each rotor’s thrust and torque coefficients are plotted as a function of advance ratio in
Figure 5.44. For each of these figures the marker color indicates a different RPM, the solid circle
markers are for the CF30x10.5 rotor, and the x markers are for the VT Swept. The rotational rates
were considered the same if their averaged values were within ± 15 RPM. As advance ratio is
plotted on the x-axis, overall trends in otherwise equivalent data points for different rotational rates
can be interpreted as rotor blade Reynolds number effects. For the CF30x10.5 rotor minimum
dispersion of 𝐶𝑇 and 𝐶𝑄 occurs for RPM ≥ 2125 which corresponds to a chord Reynolds number
at 75% of the blade span, 𝑅𝑒 (0.75R) = 2.04×105 at 2125 RPM. In contrast, Reynolds number
effects are evident for the VT Swept rotor as 𝐶𝑇 and 𝐶𝑄 do not become self-similar for increasing
advance ratio for each of the tested rotational rates. For both rotors, both 𝐶𝑇 and 𝐶𝑄 decrease in
absolute magnitude for increasing advance ratio. Comparison of 𝜂, shown in Figure 5.45(a) shows
that both rotors achieve maximum efficiency for an advance ratio of 0.3 at 2125 RPM respectively.
The percent difference in efficiency between the CF30x10.5 rotor and the VT Swept rotor for each
rotor at the same advance ratio is shown in Figure 5.45(b). For these results a positive percent
difference would indicate that the CF30x10.5 rotor is more efficient and less efficient when
negative. At low advance ratio the CF30x10.5 rotor is more efficient. For advance ratios from 0.15
to 0.22 the VT Swept rotor exceeds the efficiency of the CF30x10.5 rotor by more than 3% and
becomes less efficient by more than 1.75% for advance ratios greater than 0.23. In general, it’s
115

observed that these rotors have very similar performance characteristics which was the design goal.
The VT Swept rotor was designed to achieve similar performance albeit with reduced noise.

116

5.3.2 Assessment of Measured Noise
Narrowband acoustic spectra with an observer located beneath the plane of each rotor
(𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 45°) of the tonal and broadband components of each rotor operating at takeoff thrust for
zero advance ratio is shown in Figure 5.46. The tonal component of noise is similar in magnitude
at the BPF for both rotors. Spectral levels are increased for the VT Swept rotor at the 1 st, 2nd and
3rd harmonic of the BPF and become similar in magnitude to that of the CF30x10.5 rotor at 4×BPF
through 6×BPF. Above this the tonal component of the measured noise for the CF30x10.5 exceeds
that of the VT Swept. The broadband noise for each rotor is similar in magnitude up until
approximately 1 kHz as shown in Figure 5.46b. Above this the broadband noise of the CF30x10.5
rotor exceeds that of the VT Swept, with the most significant reductions in broadband noise of
more than 5 dB from 13×BPF to 58×BPF. To assess the reduction in noise the acoustic spectrum
of the most dominant tone associated with aerodynamic sources, the BPF, and the broadband
spectrum from each microphone in the open jet were integrated to produce directivity plots. These
results are shown in Figure 5.47. The VT Swept rotor produces similar noise at the BPF when
compared to the CF30x10.5 rotor for an observer located towards the rotational axis and near the
rotor plane. For 10° ≤ 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≤ 30° a trough in the directivity shape is shown for the CF30x10.5
rotor that become approximately 4 dB less than that of the VT Swept for 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 20°. As for the
broadband component, the noise is reduced by at least 3 dB for all observer positions for the VT
Swept when compared to the CF30x10.5. These results highlight the effectiveness of the swept
design in reducing the broadband noise sources associated with TIN and self-noise sources. Even
if facility effects are contributing to unsteadiness or inhomogeneity in the inflow to the rotor due
to its scale, the swept design produces reduced noise at similar thrust. As the observer moves
towards the plane of each rotor the noise levels are similar at the BPF and reduced for the
broadband component. Thus, researchers must be aware of facility constraints when analyzing
rotors in this way. The aerodynamic performance agrees well in this facility although the rotor is
clearly oversized with respect to the open jet inlet. The measured noise indicates that turbulence
ingestion or inflow inhomogeneity may be influencing the results and will be discussed further
when compared to predictions using the BEMT model and the CFD model presented in Pisharoti
[97]. Comparisons with CAA may not agree well.

Figure 5.46 – Separated acoustic spectra from Mic 1 (𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 = 45°) for CF30x10.5 and VT Swept
rotors at zero advance ratio in anechoic open jet a) tonal b) broadband
117

Figure 5.47 – Directivities of CF30x10.5 and VT Swept in open jet a) BPF b) broadband
component

5.4 Comparison to Estimations from BEMT and CFD
5.4.1 BEMT Model
5.4.1.1 Rotor Geometries and Operating Conditions
The 9450S rotor geometry was acquired with high fidelity 3D scans. Meshlab, Rhino, and
MATLAB were used to smooth the point clouds produced by the 3D scans. Meshlab was initially
used to reduce the number of vertices present. This reduced file was then opened in Rhino and the
profiles along the blade span were extracted. These extracted profiles were then imported into
MATLAB and smoothing spline operations were performed on each blade section. Geometries of
the CF30x10.5 and VT Swept rotor were provided by the manufacturer. The blade section profiles
were extracted using Rhino and did not require smoothing spline operations. For all rotors the
sectional chord was calculated by finding the points where the maximum curvature occurs at the
leading and trailing edge. The sectional pitch was then calculated by computing the angle between
these two points. The leading edge of each profile was then shifted to the local origin, rotated about
the local origin by the computed pitch angle, and normalized on the computed chord. The
corresponding chord and pitch distribution of each rotor normalized on tip radius computed using
the previously described steps is shown in Figure 5.48.

118

Figure 5.48 – Geometries of each rotor, a) Chord b) Pitch
Viscid and inviscid calculations were performed using XFOIL. For these calculations each
blade section contained 160 panels. Lift and drag polars were computed every 0.5° from -20° to
15° angles of attack. In this work, the density, 𝜌, of the fluid was calculated using the ideal gas law
and the ambient temperature and pressure recorded during each measurement. The dynamic
viscosity, 𝜇, was calculated using Sutherlands Formula. These two quantities along with the local
chord and tangential velocity of each blade section were then used to calculate the local Reynolds
numbers which were input into XFOIL. When computing the non-dimensional sectional lift and
drag polars the density and viscosity were set to 1.1126 kg/m3 and 1.8342E-5 kg/(m⋅s).
Experimental coefficients of thrust torque were non-dimensionalized on the true values for each
measurement and then re-dimensionalized on the values used to generate the lift and drag polars
needed for BEMT. For predictions at hover conditions (no inflow), a small inflow speed of 0.01
m/s was used for input into the BEMT model.
5.4.1.2 Effect of Correction Factors
As stated previously in Chapter 2 Section 2.3.2, correction factors are incorporated into the
BEMT model. These correction factors are the Prandtl tip loss model (TL), Prandtl Hub Loss
model (HL), and the quasi-3D spanwise flow correction of Snell [52,57]. The predicted sectional
thrust and torque as a function of span with and without the corrections for the 9450S rotor
operating at 5715 RPM and an inflow speed of 2 m/s are shown in Figure 5.49. Sectional loads per
unit span with and without the tip loss correction are approximately the same below 70% blade
radius. Above this the TL correction takes effect and the loading is reduced moving towards the
tip as shown by the blue line. Combining the Snell correction with that of the Prandtl tip loss
model, the sectional loads results for 𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 >0.6𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 are comparable to those with the tip loss only.
This makes sense as the ratio of the tangential velocity to the induced velocity decreases moving
towards the blade tip, thus reducing the effect of the correction. Below this the inclusion of the
Snell correction is evident as both the sectional thrust and torque increase. This correction modifies
the local lift coefficients parabolically in the spanwise direction and has the most effect at inboard
blade sections as shown. At these inboard sections, the local lift coefficient is typically lower than
that calculated from a 2D viscid solution. Given this, local values of lift and drag components are
increased which corresponds to an increase in thrust and torque.

119

Figure 5.49- Sectional thrust and torque correction factor comparison 9450S rotor
Each of the curves above can then be integrated across the blade span to compute the
overall thrust and torque generated by the rotor. These results are compared with the measured
values and are shown in Figure 5.50. The predicted thrust differs by at most 5% with and without
the correction factors. The predicted torque with no corrections and those with the Prandtl tip and
hub loss corrections combined with that of Snell differ from the measured by 14%. Predicted
torque with only the Prandtl tip and hub loss corrections turned on differ by 18% respectively. The
increase in predicted torque with no correction factors makes sense as they assume no losses of lift
and drag occur at the rotor blades roots and tips, thus analytically the rotor produces more torque.
During the measurements both mechanical and aerodynamic torque were measured, whereas the
predictions only account for the aerodynamic torque generated by the rotor. Predictions of thrust
and torque with the Snell, hub, and tip loss correction are in closest agreement to the measured.
For the remainder of this work, predictions of steady loading and thickness noise are conducted
with loading predictions using the BEMT model with the Snell, hub, and tip loss correction factors
applied. For predictions of TBL-TE noise the local angle of attacks and velocities at each blade
section are needed. Applying these correction factors require the blade sectional angle of attacks
to be computed based on the converged BEMT solution. These are computed by interpolating the
lift and drag polars of each blade section at the corrected sectional lift coefficients. Given that the
Snell correction applies a 3D correction to an assumed 2D flow, inferring the correct angle of
attack becomes difficult. Given this, the BEMT model with only the tip and hub loss corrections
turned on is used.

120

Figure 5.50 – Predicted thrust (a) and torque (b) with each correction factor of 9450S rotor
5.4.2 9450S Rotor Predicted Performance
Thrust and torque as function of RPM for the 9450S rotor operating in the open jet
operating at 𝑈∞ = 0, 2, 5, 8 m/s are shown in Figure 5.51. The predictions using the BEMT model
with the Snell and tip loss corrections are plotted on top of these and are indicated by the solid
lines, where the color corresponds to each inflow speed. The shaded regions in Figure 5.51(a)
indicate the uncertainty of the measured thrust. For these results the coefficients of thrust and
torque were computed based on the ambient fluid density at the time of the measurement. These
coefficients were then transformed back to thrust using the average fluid density of all
measurements which was 1.1126 kg/m3. For the BEMT results the average density from all
measurements was used and the dynamic viscosity, 𝜇, was set equal to 1.8342E-5 kg/(m⋅s).

Figure 5.51 – Measured and predicted thrust (a) and torque (b) vs RPM at all inflow speeds
tested in the open jet for 9450S rotor
For 𝑈∞ ≤ 2 m/s the fully corrected BEMT model is in excellent agreement with the measured
thrust values at all RPM. At 5 m/s, the predicted thrust values are slightly less than the measured
at all RPM, but all lie just on the lower edge of the blue shaded uncertainty bounds. Increasing
inflow to 8 m/s the predicted thrust values lie outside of the uncertainty bounds and become closer
121

to the measured at higher RPM’s. At high advance ratios, local blade sections become stalled.
Locally, this causes the turbulent separation point to move forward from the trailing edge as the
local angle of attack increases [98]. This results in a sudden loss of lift and an increase in drag.
Using XFOIL to calculate the required lift and drag coefficients degrades significantly in accuracy
for angles of attack that yield coefficients of lift greater than the maximum coefficient of lift for a
given Reynolds number [45].
5.4.3 9450S Rotor Predictions of Total, Steady Loading, and Thickness Noise at the BPF
5.4.3.1 Increasing Inflow Velocity
As shown, in Section 5.2.1, the noise at the BPF under the prescribed inflow conditions,
produced by the 9450S rotor is generated primarily by purely tonal sources. Therefore, predictions
were made using BEMT and F1A to predict the pure tonal steady loading and thickness noise.
Predictions of thrust and torque including using the BEMT model with the Prandtl’s tip loss and
Snell’s 3D correction factor are shown in Table 5.6 for each of the experimental conditions shown
previously Table 5.3. The columns labeled ∆𝑇 and ∆𝑄 are the measured results less the predicted.

Predictions of thrust for all cases are in excellent agreement (within 1 N). For all cases the torque
is underpredicted and may be because the measurement included mechanical torque which isn’t a
component of the prediction. Overall, the agreement is reasonable. Predictions of the total tonal,
steady loading, and thickness noise at the BPF were then computed using the loading distributions,
like shown previously in Figure 5.49, from the fully corrected BEMT model. Directivity plots of
the predicted noise compared with the measured directivity at the BPF are shown in Figure 5.52.
For all cases, predictions of total noise are in excellent agreement (within 2 dB) of the experimental
results for 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≥ 24°. Near the rotor plane, for 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≤ 20° the total noise is over predicted by
approximately 3 dB. At the highest inflow speed, 𝑈∞ = 8 m/s, the predicted thickness noise
exceeds levels of the steady loading noise near the rotor plane. Predictions of the total noise are
within 2.7 dB for all observer locations with the most significant disagreement occurring for
observer positions furthest away from the rotor plane. Additionally, the predictions exhibit the
same trend of reduced noise at the BPF for increasing inflow speeds as observed in Figure 5.34.
The largest sources of disagreement occur in-plane for 𝑈∞ ≤ 2 m/s and closer to the rotational axis
as the inflow velocity increases. Overall, this shows that noise at the BPF can be well characterized
using simplified calculations and is dominated by true tonal sources in this arrangement. In other
facilities in which environmental flow (e.g. the drone park) or recirculation (the anechoic chamber)
effects are significant, this simplified method would produce poor agreement with the measured
values. The noise at the BPF differed by 5 dB and 8 dB compared to measurements in the drone
park and anechoic chamber, respectively, for observer locations near 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≈ 45°. Simplified
122

prediction methods like those presented here may be used as a first check to assess the influence
of the facility on acoustic measurements.
