The predicted blade sectional angles of attack and lift coefficient per unit span are plotted vs
normalized radius in Figure 5.54. For 𝑈∞ ≤ 2 m/s local angles of attack and coefficients of lift
remain constant from the inward blade sections up until 0.85𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 respectively. Beyond this the
angles of attack are reduced. The application of Prandtl’s tip loss factor ensures that the local lift
goes to zero at the blade tips, thus the local angles of attack are reduced. This loss of lift towards
the blade tip is shown in b. Increasing 𝑈∞ = 5 m/s the local angle of attack at the blade root
becomes negative and increases moving towards the blade tip. At the highest inflow speed, 𝑈∞ =
8 m/s, at the root the local angle of attack is -8° and approaches zero from the mid span to 85%
radius. The local lift coefficients remain positive from 0.35𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 outward. Overall, for inflow speeds
less than or equal to 5 m/s the local angles of attack and lift coefficients vary smoothly along the
blade span. For higher inflow speeds the predicted local angles of attack at inwards blade sections
become highly negative giving rise to local 3D flow phenomena such as boundary layer separation
and stall.

Figure 5.54 – a) Local angle of attack vs normalized span, b) Sectional 𝑐𝑙 per unit width vs
normalized span

125

Using the sectional angles of attack and local velocities from the BEMT solution shown
above viscous XFOIL calculations are then performed on each blade section to compute the
boundary layer thickness and boundary layer displacement thickness which are two additional
required inputs for the BPM method. These quantities are extracted at x/c = 0.99. For prediction
of bluntness vortex shedding noise the trailing edge angle, 𝜓 𝑇𝐸 , and trailing edge bluntness, H
were computed for each blade section. These as a function of normalized radius are shown in
Figure 5.55b and Figure 5.55c. A single blade section at 75% blade radius is shown in Figure
5.55a. The blue line is located at x/c = 0.96 indicates where the trailing edge bluntness was taken
from. The interior angle between the red dashed lines indicates the trailing edge angle 𝜓 𝑇𝐸 . These
lines are parallel to the pressure and suction sides of the trailing edge of blade section as shown in
Figure 5.55a. Bluntness remains under 1 mm and decreases moving radially from the rotor hub to
tip. 𝜓 𝑇𝐸 varies from 4 to 12 degrees and increases moving towards the blade tip.

Figure 5.55 – a) TE bluntness and TE angle calculation overview, b) Blade section TE bluntness
as a function of normalized span, c) TE angle as a function of normalized span
Predictions of self-noise associated with the turbulent boundary layer of the suction side of
each blade section, TBL-TESS, separation-stall, and bluntness vortex shedding, Bluntness VS, are
shown in Figure 5.55. Here, SNTotal is the sum of all the individual sources identified in each of
the legends. Self-noise sources associated with laminar boundary layer vortex shedding predicted
using the BPM method were found to produce unrealistic spectral humps and were omitted from
these results. These differences may be a result of the fact that the BPM method was derived based
on 2D NACA 0012 blade sections and is not as effective for changes in local chord, pitch, and
blade thickness. The experimental data the predictions are compared to is the broadband
126

component that was separated from the measured total noise using the phase averaging technique
shown previously. For all inflow speeds, the broadband self-noise is predicted to be mainly due to
the interaction of turbulence within the boundary layer of the suction side of the rotor blades at the
trailing edge and boundary layer separation effects from 680 Hz to 11 kHz. At the highest inflow
speed of 8 m/s, the interaction of turbulence with the pressure side of the airfoil dominates the
predicted spectrum at low frequencies below 680 Hz. For 𝑈∞ = 5 m/s, predictions of TBL-TESS
exceed those associated with separation/stall by at most 5 dB from 1.4 kHz to 6 kHz respectively.
Above 11 kHz, bluntness vortex shedding noise is predicted to be the most dominant source of
self-noise for all cases. The dominance of bluntness vortex shedding noise at high frequencies for
a similar rotor in hover was also observed in Zawodny et al. [2]. Predictions of TBL-TESS for
𝑈∞ = 0 m/s exceed those associated with TBL-TEA by 3 dB from 680 to 1.8 kHz and by 6 dB
from 2.5 kHz to 5.5 kHz respectively. A broadband self-noise hump is predicted with maximum
levels at 6.5 kHz for 𝑈∞ = 0 m/s, that shifts to lower frequencies for 𝑈∞ = 2 m/s. This change in
spectral shape is also shown to occur in the experimental data with the addition of inflow where
the spectral peaks vanish, and a broadband hump appears at approximately 5.6 kHz. Here, the total
self-noise predictions are within 3 dB of the measured down to approximately 1.5 kHz for the
𝑈∞ = 2 m/s case. For the no inflow case, predictions of TBL-TESS are approximately 7 dB greater
than those associated with boundary layer separation/stall. Above 3.6 kHz, the predictions of selfnoise associated with TBL-TEA increase and become within 3 dB of TBL-TESS at 6 kHz
respectively. For both cases, the predictions of self-noise are almost exact to that of the measured
above 17 kHz. Although some speculation should be taken as this is approaching the maximum
response of the microphone of 20 kHz that gives minimal uncertainty of ±1dB. Increasing the
inflow speed to 5 m/s, the total predicted self-noise is maximum and within 3 dB of the measured
at 4 kHz respectively. The maximum levels of predicted separation noise are reduced by 5 dB
when compared to the lower inflow cases. Separation and stall noise is also predicted to produce
noise across a narrower range of frequencies. TBL-TESS dominates at low frequencies and exceeds
spectral levels of those associated with TBL-TEA by 10 dB at 4.2 kHz respectively. At the highest
inflow speed, 𝑈∞ = 8 m/s, self-noise is predicted to be dominated by that of TBL-TEPS below 680
kHz and at higher frequencies the predictions are dominated by TBL-TESS and bluntness VS
sources. For this case, predictions of the total self-noise spectrum exceed those of the measured by
at most 5 dB from 1.4 kHz to 6 kHz. Additionally, the predicted spectral levels of self-noise
sources associated with boundary layer separation and stall fall below the lower limits of the figure
and those associated with the interaction of turbulence with the pressure side of the trailing edge
are predicted to be most dominant at low frequencies. The predicted increase in broadband selfnoise associated with TBL-TEPS sources at higher inflow speeds would be expected as the thrust
is reduced; thus, the local blade section angles of attack are reduced as shown in Figure 5.54(a).
This decrease in local angles of attack causes the thickness of the boundary layer on the pressure
side of the rotor blades to increase, thus more noise is produced by this self-noise source. Overall,
the trend of the predicted self-noise spectra matches the experimental data well from 2 kHz to 5
kHz for all cases with the most significant differences occurring for inflow speeds greater than or
equal to 5 m/s. Although the differences between the predicted broadband self-noise and
experimentally measured broadband noise are significant at higher inflow speeds, the physics of
the flow on the rotor blades surface with increasing inflow speeds appear to be reflected when
performing broadband self-noise predictions with the BPM model. As stated previously, self-noise
associated with LBL-VS were omitted from these predictions as including this source produced
unrealistic spectral features. In future work, it would be recommended to take measurements with
127

and without trip installed on the upper surface of the rotor blades to determine if LBL-VS sources
are present.

Figure 5.56 – 9450S rotor self-noise predictions a) 𝑈∞ = 0 m/s, b) 𝑈∞ = 2 m/s, c) 𝑈∞ = 5 m/s,
d) 𝑈∞ = 8 m/s
5.4.5 CF30x10.5 and VT Swept Rotor Predictions of Total, Steady Loading, and Thickness
Noise at the BPF
In this section the fully corrected BEMT model is used to predict the steady thrust and
torque of the CF30x10.5 and VT Swept rotors operating at 2000 RPM in the open jet facility
without inflow. These are followed by predictions of the pure tonal steady loading and thickness
noise using F1A. Steady aerodynamic loading data from URANS calculations is provided using
the computational framework of Pisharoti and Brizzolara [95]. These computations used the
Reynolds stress transport-based-transition turbulence model, SGG/LRR-𝜔-𝛾 and previously been
validated on a smaller DJI Phantom 3 Quadcopter rotor [95]. From here on aerodynamic
performance and acoustic predictions using inputs from the URANS calculations will be referred
to as those of the CFD model. Thrust and torque predicted using each of the models are shown in
Table 5.9 where the measured values are listed in the two columns on the right. Predictions of
thrust and torque for CF30x10.5 rotor are within 5% percent of the measured for both models,
while torque is under predicted by 10% for the BEMT model and in excellent agreement for the
CFD model (< 2%). Predicted mean thrust for the VT Swept rotor from the CFD model is within
approximately 5% of the measured and approximately the same when compared to that of the
128

CF30x10.5 rotor. Predicted torque is in excellent agreement for the CFD model and differs by
approximately 10% from the measured for the BEMT model. The predicted loads per unit span
are plotted as a function of normalized radius in Figure 5.57. For the BEMT model predicted
sectional thrust reach a maximum value at approximately 80% of the blade span whereas the CFD
model shows a maximum in sectional thrust to occur at approximately 0.85𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 for each rotor.
Each model predicts a loading distribution of similar shape for each rotor. The CFD model
produces a linearly increasing elemental thrust up until 0.8𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 . At this spanwise radially location
the elemental thrust increases moving outward by approximately 30% and then falls rapidly to
similar those predicted by the BEMT model. The CFD model predicts less thrust from approx.
0.4𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 to 0.8𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 for each rotor. Over this same spanwise region the BEMT model predicts the
most thrust for the VT Swept rotor. From 0.4𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 radially inward the predicted thrust distributions
are similar for each rotor as well as prediction model. Predicted torque distributions shown in the
bottom of the figure are similar in shape and magnitude for each rotor and prediction model from
0.4𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 to 0.9𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 . More torque is predicted for VT Swept rotor near the rotor hub using CFD. In
comparison, the BEMT model predicts more torque from 0.4𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 to approximately 0.85𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 which
is similar to the results for the elemental thrust as well. Overall, the predicted loading distributions
of each rotor are similar in shape for each method from the blade hub to approximately 0.8𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝
here the CFD model predicts an increase and then decrease in thrust producing a bell-shaped
distribution in elemental thrust from 0.8𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 to 0.95𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 . Predictions of steady loading and
thickness noise using the spanwise thrust and torque distributions from each model are shown in
Figure 5.58.

Figure 5.57 – Predicted spanwise thrust and torques using the BEMT and CFD model for the

Figure 5.58 – Predicted directionalities at the BPF using inputs from BEMT or CFD model a)
CF30x10.5 rotor b) VT Swept
Predictions of total, steady loading, and thickness noise at the BPF exhibit the same shape
and respective amplitudes when computed using aerodynamic solutions from either method. For
𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≥ 12° the predictions of total noise at the BPF are within approximately 5 dB. For 30° ≤
𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≤ 45° plane the experimental directivities rise in amplitude up and decrease and then increase
forming a trough like feature in directivity from 12° ≤ 𝜃𝑜𝑏𝑠 ≤ 30°. This feature is not captured
with the predictions. Near the rotor plane, the predictions are in most disagreement with the
measured by approximately 10 dB for both rotors. These results highlight the effects of the facility
on the measured noise. Applying either the BEMT or CFD model to the CF30x10.5 rotor yields
excellent results in terms of aerodynamic performance although the predicted loading distributions
differ towards the blade tip. Performance predictions for the VT Swept are in excellent agreement
with the measured results when computed using the CFD model. In comparison, the BEMT model
over predicts the average thrust by approximately 20% when compared to that of the measured.
The predicted magnitudes of the sectional loads across the span for the VT Swept using the BEMT
130

model exceed those predicted by the CFD model for 0.4𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 ≤ 𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 ≤ 0.9𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 , but it does not
appear to have a significant impact on the F1A solution as shown. Loading distributions for each
the low order BEMT method and the CFD model are almost equal in magnitude for 𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 ≤ 0.9𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝
for the unswept rotor. Overall, the aerodynamic performance is well predicted by each model. On
the other hand, the predictions of steady loading and thickness noise at the BPF using F1A do not
capture the trough like features in directivities that are present in the measured data and differ
significantly near the rotor plane.
Comparing other measured directivities at the BPF of each rotor at other RPMs and with
the open jet tunnel on to predicted directivities using F1A also showed mixed results. Trough and
hump like features would appear in the measured directivities that would change location with
RPM and be present at all inflow speeds. These were not captured in the predictions and the most
significant disagreements were found to occur near the rotor plane. The limits of the facility are
being exceeded when measuring the noise at the BPF of 68 Hz for these larger rotors as the
anechoic limit of the open jet section of 420 Hz and the size of the open jet inlet is 30”. Constructive
and destructive interference could be resultant of the measured noise reflecting off the test section
walls and inside of the inlet. The diameter of these rotors is equal to that of the inlet allowing the
blade tips to interact with the shear layer that develops between the moving fluid in the core of the
open jet and the static fluid in the test section. Higher order harmonics did not vanish with the
addition of minimal inflow unlike those for the 9450S rotor suggesting that the rotor itself is still
interacting with sources associated with BWI or TIN. As these larger rotors are producing thrust,
the induced velocity can exceed that of the tunnel. A non-uniform velocity field is the result of this
and can become ingested by the rotor as well as the generation of turbulent structures. As these
turbulent structures become ingested unsteady loading occurs on the blades and noise is produced
at the higher order harmonics. For a much smaller rotor, noise sources associated with BWI or TIN
were mitigated even at very low inflow speeds, whereas for these larger rotors higher order
harmonic content was found to be present for inflow speeds less than 5 m/s regardless of RPM.
Given the simplicity of the BEMT model, it would be recommended for an assessment of the rotor
performance in the early design stages.

131

Chapter 6 Noise Produced by eVTOL Drive Systems
To analyze the noise associated with the electric drive system, three sets of measurements
were conducted to identify the source and frequency range of the noise and evaluate the effect of
the electronic speed controller (ESC) switching frequency. These measurements were all
conducted in the anechoic open jet using the experimental setup shown in Chapter 3 section 3.6.
The first set consisted of measuring the noise of the CF30x10.5 rotor with and without a small
acoustic enclosure installed around an outrunner U8 Lite KV150 brushless DC (BLDC) motor.
These measurements used a FLAME 60A ESC to control the motor which had a fixed switching
rate of 18 kHz. A second set of measurements were conducted with a Phoenix Edge HV 80A ESC.
The switching rates for this ESC were user programable and changed from 8 kHz to 24 kHz. A
summary of the measured rotational rate, mean thrust and torque, and ESC switching rates for
these two sets of measurements are shown in Table 6.1. This chapter concludes with a discussion
of the measured output voltage time history from a single leg of each ESC with no rotor installed
on the BLDC motor spinning at the same RPM as those of the rotor acoustic measurements.

6.1 Tones Associated with Electric Motor
To evaluate the effectiveness of a noise barrier designed to house the motor, an enclosure
was 3D printed and encased in an acoustic mass loaded vinyl material. The corresponding
enclosure and its associated dimensions are shown at the top of Figure 6.1. A zoomed in view of
the enclosure installed on the BLDC motor in the open jet test section is shown at the bottom of
Figure 6.1. The 3D printed enclosure was composed of two separate half cylindrical shells with
threaded inserts that were encased in acoustic dampening rubber. The rubber was attached to the
ABS material with M6 bolts. The two cylindrical shells were then placed around the BLDC motor
and attached to 80/20-aluminum sting and the four M6 bolts at the access point were then tightened
sealing the enclosure to form one solid part. The addition of the enclosure increased the distance
from the face of the motor rotor to the bottom of the rotor hub therefore a shaft extension was
necessary. This extension was 3D printed, 7.9 mm in length, and the same diameter as the through
hole in the center of the rotor hub. Four M6 bolts were then placed through mounting holes of the
rotor and threaded into the motor rotor.

132

Figure 6.1 – Noise barrier installed on T – U8 Lite KV150 brushless motor with CF30x10.5 rotor
a) Acoustic enclosure and associated dimensions b) Acoustic enclosure installed on respective
brushless motor in open jet
Noise sources associated with the BLDC motor can be decomposed into electromagnetic
and mechanical sources as shown in Figure 6.2. A BLDC motor and is composed of windings, an
airspace gap, magnetic poles, a stator with azimuthally spaced iron slots and a motor rotor as shown
in Figure 6.3. As the motor operates, a time varying electromagnetic field develops within the
airspace. This electromagnetic field causes tangential and radial forces to develop within the air
gap which can be decomposed into local and global forces. Where the local force is produced by
the magneto-motive force and the airgap permeance and the global forces are associated with
torque ripple and cogging torque. The frequency components associated with the global forces

133

such as torque ripple and cogging torque can be expressed as functions of the shaft rate as shown
in [78]:
𝑓𝑡𝑟,𝑘 = 6 × 𝑘 × 𝑁𝑝𝑝 × 𝑓𝑠ℎ𝑎𝑓𝑡
𝑓𝑐𝑡,𝑘 = 𝑘 × 𝑙𝑐𝑚(𝑁𝑝 , 𝑁𝑠 ) × 𝑓𝑠ℎ𝑎𝑓𝑡

(6.1)

Where, 𝑓𝑠ℎ𝑎𝑓𝑡 = 𝑅𝑃𝑀 ⁄60, 𝑘 is the harmonic number, 𝑙𝑐𝑚 is the least common multiple between
the number of slots, 𝑁𝑠 , and the number of poles, 𝑁𝑝 and 𝑁𝑝𝑝 is the number of pole pairs. For the
motor used in this study 𝑁𝑠 = 36 and 𝑁𝑝 = 42. Henderson et al. [30] showed that the unsteady
loading generated by the magnetic field could be computed via Maxwell’s stress tensor through
the superposition of pressures associated with the motor rotor and stator fields. Through this
derivation they showed the local forces generated by the magnetic field to produce noise at the
fundamental line frequency supply current, 𝑓𝑙 = 𝑓𝑠ℎ𝑎𝑓𝑡 𝑁𝑝𝑝 . Comparing this to Equation 6.1 tones
associated with torque ripple and cogging torque are harmonics of the frequencies associated with
local forces. If these resonant frequencies of the electromagnetic noise sources associated with the
local and global forces coincide with those generated aerodynamically by the rotor itself significant
increases in tonal amplitudes can occur [99]. If they do not, the radiated noise is purely related to
acoustic modes of the BLDC motor. Acoustically generated motor noise is resultant of the pressure
generated by the magnetic field within the airgap shown in Figure 6.3. As the magnetic field is
composed of many harmonics acoustically generated noise can occur over a wide range of
frequencies [30]. The last source of electromagnetically generated noise is associated with the
switching frequency of the motor driver itself, which in this study is an ESC. The ESC drives the
motor by switching voltage on and off in between the positive and negative supply voltage at the
switching rate of the ESC, 𝑓𝑒𝑠𝑐 . This switching causes the current to flow in two directions which
switches the polarity on the stators. The force associated with the charging of the stators is
impacted by this switching of polarity as they impact the current supplied to the motor [30]. Those
associated with electromagnetically generated local and global forces, structural modes, and
acoustic modes for the U8 Lite KV150 BLDC motor will be assessed in this section. Those
associated with the switching frequency of the ESC will be assessed in the section that follows.
Narrowband acoustic spectra from Mics 1, 7, and 14 are shown in Figure 6.4 for
measurements with and without the enclosure installed. Each of these microphone positions
correspond to an observer located 45.1°, 25°, and 0° below the rotor plane as shown previously in
Table 3.11. The shaft rate along with the fundamental frequencies associated with electromagnetic
sources are listed in Table 6.2. Comparison of spectra at each observer location shows tonal
harmonics of the BPF up to approximately 1400 Hz for both measurements and significant
differences in tonal content above 2.4 kHz when the enclosure is installed. Tones associated with
𝑓𝑆ℎ𝑎𝑓𝑡 and its harmonics increase when the enclosure is added for frequencies less than 350 Hz.
More specifically, for Mics 1 and 7, the 3rd and 5th harmonics of 𝑓𝑠ℎ𝑎𝑓𝑡 increase by more than 7
dB. For the in-plane location, Mic 14, the 3rd harmonic of 𝑓𝑠ℎ𝑎𝑓𝑡 increases by approximately 4 dB
while the 5th harmonic remains the same. The increased noise at harmonics of the shaft rate is
attributed to an imbalance in the motor shaft. This modified system was not balanced before
testing. Dominant tones are not present at the fundamental frequencies of the line current.
However, at higher frequencies, those associated with 𝑓𝑡𝑟 , and 𝑓𝑐𝑡 are dominate at high frequencies
for Mics 1 and 14. For all three observer positions a dominate tone is present at 𝑓𝑒𝑠𝑐 . For
measurements with the enclosure installed spectral levels at these frequencies become reduced. In
general, the acoustic enclosure highlights the significance of motor noise at higher frequencies.
134

The most significant differences in tonal content above 2.4 kHz were observed closer to the rotor
plane. At this observer position, contributions from aerodynamic noise sources are the least over
this range of frequency. These results are also consistent with Huff and Henderson [99] who found
the electric motor noise to have peak spectral levels for an in-plane observer.
Acoustic spectra from Mic 14 (in-plane) with and without the enclosure installed for the
frequency range 2.4 kHz to 7.2 kHz are shown in Figure 6.5. Two dominant tones are present for
measurements without the enclosure at 𝑓𝑡𝑟 and at the frequency 𝑓𝑎 = 5352 Hz which does not
coincide with those of electromagnetic sources. These tones vanish for measurements with the
enclosure installed and the broadband features underneath these tones are due to aerodynamic
sources such as self-noise or BWI [69]. Most of the tones in this frequency range are expected to
be produced by motor noise, thus the enclosure effectively reduces some of the noise associated
with the BLDC motor [2,30,100]. The tone at 𝑓𝑎 may be associated with structural resonance of
the motor. In Cluts [79], surface vibration measurements and finite element calculations were
conducted for a U13 T-motor operating at 3007 RPM. Two azimuthal vibrational modes for this
motor were reported to occur at 3273 Hz and 5513 Hz. Given this, the other prominent tones
produced in this range may be a resonant frequency of the motor. Ultimately, across this range of
frequencies, the installation of the cover eliminates much of the measured tonal content showing
the effectiveness of this simple design. However, it does add an additional weight of 0.561 kg. For
multirotor vehicles the added mass would increase with the number of rotors and may influence
performance.

6.2 Effects of ESC Switching Rate on the Radiated Noise
As stated previously, acoustic measurements were taken with two different square wave
type ESC’s. The process of how the ESC drives the motor is outlined in Figure 6.6. A pulse width
modulated (PWM) signal of constant frequency is sent to the ESC. The amount of voltage supplied
to the motor is based on the duty cycle which is the ratio of on-time to off-time. This controls the
rotational rate of the motor. The ESC’s drive the electric motor via pulse position modulation
(PWM). An ESC receives this PWM signal and turns power transistors on and off sequentially at
the specified switching rate of the ESC, 𝑓𝑒𝑠𝑐 . This specified switching rate determines how fast
these power transistors are turned on and off. This square wave voltage output is then applied to
each phase of the motor at specific times which causes current to flow through the windings in two
different directions creating opposite signed magnetic poles, which the cause the magnets on the
rotor motor to repel resulting in rotation.

Figure 6.6 – Brushless DC motor driving process
Narrowband acoustic spectra from Mic 14 for different ESC switching rates are shown in
Figure 6.7. Note that the presented measurement for the 18 kHz switching rate uses the FLAME
60 A HV ESC. All other measurements with varying switching rates were made using the Phoenix
Edge HV ESC. For all switching rates, amplitudes of the BPF and higher order harmonics remain
138

within 2-3 dB of each other up until approximately 2.7 kHz. Tones above 2.7 kHz were found to
vary significantly with switching rate and ESC. For clarity, the same acoustic spectra are shown
in Figure 6.8 over frequencies from 2.7 to 8 kHz. From approximately 2.8 to 4.8 kHz, the spectra
for measurements with the Phoenix Edge ESC have contain fewer tones for all switching rates.
The tone at 𝑓𝑡𝑟 remains consistent in amplitude for measurements with the Phoenix Edge ESC. In
comparison, the spectral content at frequencies less than 𝑓𝑡𝑟 is rich in tones and approximately 7
dB greater for measurements with the Flame 60A HV ESC. A hump like feature of tonal content
from approximately 5 to 6.5 kHz is present for both ESC’s and all switching rates.

Figure 6.7 - Narrowband acoustic spectra from Mic 14 with different ESC switching rates

Figure 6.8 - Narrowband acoustic spectra from Mic 14 from 2.7 to 8 kHz with different ESC
switching rates
Narrowband acoustic spectra for Mic 14 are shown in Figure 6.9 from 8 to 20 kHz. The
number of tones and their levels tend to decrease in this frequency range with increasing switching
139

rate. The FLAME ESC does produce a pair of dominant tones at 18 kHz and 19.4 kHz. The 18
kHz tone matches the switching frequency of this ESC. Tones associated with the switching
frequency of the Phoenix Edge ESC are not observed in the measurements. Tones associated with
the 7th and 8th harmonic of the local forces generated by electromagnetic sources are present and
remain approximately the same in amplitude for each ESC and tested switching rate. The
directionality of the noise integrated from 2.7 to 20 kHz is shown in Figure 6.10 for all microphone
positions listed in Table 3.11.

Figure 6.9 - Narrowband acoustic spectra from Mic 14 from 8 to 20 kHz with different ESC
switching rates

Figure 6.10 – Directionality integrated from 2.7 → 20 kHz with different ESC switching rates
An ESC switching rate of 24 kHz produces the lowest integrated noise at each observer location.
Although the highest switching rate produces the most noise reduction, the result isn’t completely
monotonic with switching frequency. Near the in-plane location, there is an increase in noise for
the 12 kHz switching rate compared to 8 kHz. In general, a higher switching rate reduces the
amount of motor noise observed at most locations. This indicates that significant noise reduction
140

can be attained by simply selecting an ESC with a higher frequency switching rate. To further
investigate the effects of the ESC on the measured noise the voltage outputs from a single leg of
the ESC were measured with the electric motor operating at 2000 RPM without the rotor installed.
Narrowband spectra of these signals with the motor only are shown in Figure 6.11. The spectra are
offset by 60 dB for clarity.

Figure 6.11 – Narrowband output voltage spectra of all ESCs and switching rates
At lower frequencies, < 2 kHz, the tonal content is similar. At higher frequencies the
spectral peaks tend to reduce with increasing switching frequency. For all cases with the Phoenix
Edge ESC strong spectral peaks that do not coincide with the shaft rate appear at the fundamental
frequency associated with the local forces as well as its 3rd and 9th harmonic. Also, the FLAME
ESC produces much more tonal content around its switching frequency, 18 kHz, than the Phoenix
Edge ESC which aligns with the tone observed in the acoustic spectra at this frequency. The time
series of the measured voltage from a single leg of each ESC is shown in Figure 6.12. The results
for the FLAME ESC are shown in red at the bottom of the figure and those of the Phoenix Edge
ESC start at the line plot in blue and move towards the top as the switching rate is increased from
8 to 24 kHz. For these plots the limits of the x axis correspond to the time scale associated with
𝑓𝑙 = 700 Hz. This represents the commutation interval which is the length of time over which the
energizing of a single phase completes one cycle of operation or electrical revolution. An electrical
revolution is defined as 𝜃𝑒 = 𝑁𝑝𝑝 𝜃𝑚 , where 𝜃𝑚 is the mechanical revolution which is equal to
360°. This implies that for the 3-phase BLDC motor used in this study twenty-one electrical
revolutions occur for one mechanical revolution which gives rise to the 3rd harmonic of 𝑓𝑙 as shown
in Figure 6.11. The selected switching frequency is also shown as the small peaks in each time
history within the square wave signal are present when voltage is being applied to this single phase
of the motor as indicated by 𝑓𝑒𝑠𝑐 . These results indicate that the ESC and its switching rate should
both be considered in the design of quiet eVTOL vehicles, particularly with regards to the noise

141

produced at higher frequencies and the relative acoustic performance may be evaluated through
comparison of the spectral content of the output motor drive signal.

Figure 6.12 – Single phase voltage output of each ESC over one electrical revolution for
different switching rates

142

Chapter 7 Conclusions
A new class of multi-rotor VTOL electric aircraft is becoming a dominant advanced vehicle
concept. Urban Air Mobility (UAM) vehicles are designed for short routes within urban
environments carrying only a few passengers during each flight. Other smaller Unmanned Aerial
Systems (UAS) are increasingly being used for delivery services or to perform tasks which are
more easily accessed with this technology like inspection or photography. Thus, these vehicles are
expected to operate in close proximity to the general populace exposing it to aircraft noise which
is currently limited to communities surrounding airports. For successful integration into the
airspace with minimal community annoyance, the mechanisms responsible for generation of the
noise must be understood. The FAA currently has acoustic models for conventional rotorcraft to
assess the impact of their noise on surrounding communities and well-defined methods for
experimentally measuring their noise. While these methods have been used to provide accurate
assessment of noise exposure levels, they do assume similarities between vehicle configurations
and conventional flight operations. eVTOL aircraft differ significantly in configuration from
conventional rotorcraft. The concepts (number of rotors and distribution, etc.) even differ greatly
from concept to concept. Traditionally, for conventional rotorcraft (one main rotor), the tonal noise
has been more of a concern than the broadband component. eVTOL vehicles are often equipped
with multiple rotors that are lightly loaded and operate at lower tip speeds which can be time
varying. Thus, there is an increased significance of broadband noise. Lastly, these aircraft are
equipped with an electric drive system that gives rise to an additional noise source that is not
present for conventional aircraft. Best practices for measuring eVTOL noise are not currently
established. Measurement of eVTOL rotor noise is complicated by the increased significance of
the broadband sources. These have been shown to be facility dependent. Given this, there is a need
for high quality experimental data and an analysis of experimental data in multiple facilities for
these rotors and drive systems. Capabilities of traditional models to predict conventional rotorcraft
noise also needs to be assessed for these rotors. These two issues have been assessed in this work;
a summary of the experiments and the conclusions are discussed below. This work provides an
experimental analysis of the acoustic footprint of a hexacopter in hover and low speed forward
flight, comparison of aerodynamic performance and noise of eVTOL rotors operating in multiple
facilities, and analysis of the noise associated with an outrunner brushless DC motor. Empirical
and low-order models are used to predict aerodynamic performance, tonal noise, and broadband
noise for isolated eVTOL rotors. In addition, a low noise, swept rotor design was evaluated.
The acoustic footprint of a DJI Matrice 600 Pro hexacopter in hover and low speed forward
flight was measured in the Virginia Tech Drone Park. Three distinct regions were observed in the
frequency spectra of the noise. At low frequencies below 800 Hz, the acoustic signature was
dominated by the rotor BPF and its higher order harmonics. A mid frequency broadband region
associated with interaction noise sources of BWI or TIN was shown to exist from 800 Hz to 5 kHz.
Above 5 kHz, self-noise sources were found to dominate. A-weighting of these spectra highlighted
the importance of mid-frequency broadband noise; thus, it must be considered in noise reduction
strategies and for an accurate assessment of community impact. The noise of this aircraft in lowspeed flyover was also shown to be like that in hover. Given this, significantly less complex noise
measurements of an aircraft in hover or those of an isolated rotor at static conditions may be used
to assess the acoustic footprint of an eVTOL aircraft in low-speed forward flight.
143

An experimental study of two commercially available eVTOL rotors was conducted in a
confined anechoic environment and an open environment, the Virginia Tech Drone Park. The noise
and thrust produced by each rotor operating at take-off conditions were investigated. For
measurements made in the anechoic chamber, recirculation effects were shown to significantly
increase harmonic amplitudes by more than 8 dB. The placement of two varying solidity screens
in the wake 4 radii apart may have delayed the onset of recirculation for the 9450S rotor slightly
but was not significant enough to be conclusive. Placing screens in the wake of the larger R2170
rotor did not appear to have any effect on or delay recirculation. Measurements of the BPF and
higher harmonics were more consistent in the drone park but were more similar to the elevated
levels measured in the anechoic chamber after the onset of recirculation. The directionalities
observed by calculation of the OASPL and noise at the BPF and first harmonic were also compared
between indoor and outdoor measurements for the 9450S and R2170 rotors. For the anechoic
chamber measurements, 4 and 28 second averaging periods for the FFT were considered. In-plane
measurements agreed for both averaging periods, but the sound pressure level for an observer
located well below the rotor plane increased for longer averaging periods. Results from the drone
park had similar directivity as the anechoic chamber measurements when considering longer
averaging periods. For lower averaging times, in particular, the 4 second averaging period, the
directivity of the 1st harmonic was found to disagree significantly in amplitude and shape for the
9450S rotor in the anechoic chamber when compared that of the drone park. Measurements in the
drone park contained increased background noise at low frequencies which influenced
interpretation of the acoustic spectra near the BPF. In indoor facilities, the rotor inflow becomes
contaminated due to recirculation shortly after the rotor reaches steady state. In outdoor
environments, the inflow to the rotor disc becomes distorted due to changing wind conditions and
turbulence in the atmosphere. While spectral levels of tones in the anechoic chamber were shown
to rise in amplitude as a function of averaging time, those from the measurements in the drone
park remained consistent in amplitude throughout the measurement and the amplitude of the higher
order harmonics were found to exceed those of the anechoic chamber. Given this, environmental
factors and recirculation were found to both increase the higher order harmonics. These facility
effects result in increased broadband noise which makes comparisons of experimental results
difficult. Additionally, at these static conditions, computational models do not compare well to
measured results. To ensure fair comparison between experimentally measured and predicted
noise, an anechoic chamber facility would be recommended when compared to an outdoor facility.
As for determining the onset of recirculation, crude actuator disk models like that of Glegg et al.
[101] for a rotor operating in a confined volume may be beneficial. An attempt was made to
eliminate these facility effects by studying the noise of these rotors in an anechoic open jet wind
tunnel.
Measurements of force and noise were then conducted for three commercially available
eVTOL rotors operating in the Virginia Tech Anechoic Open Jet Wind Tunnel. The 9450s and
R2170 were studied for comparison with the anechoic chamber and drone park measurements. In
addition, a CF30x10.5 T-motor rotor was studied and compared to the measured performance and
noise of a newly designed custom-fabricated low noise swept rotor. These measurements were
accompanied with predictions of aerodynamic performance and tonal and broadband noise. BEMT
was used to predict aerodynamic performance. Tonal noise associated with the rotor blade steady
loading and thickness was predicted using F1A and rotor broadband self-noise was predicted using
the model of BPM. The measured noise in this facility as well as that of the anechoic camber and
drone park was separated into tonal and broadband components by applying a phase averaging
144

technique to the measured acoustic pressure time history. For all rotors, the broadband noise was
shown to contribute significantly to the total measured far field noise and thus must be considered
when designing quiet eVTOL systems. The results also show that in this indoor facility the noise
produced at the BPF is dominated by tonal sources, but the higher harmonics can be attributed to
broadband interactions particularly at static conditions. This broadband noise was drastically
reduced by increasing the inflow velocity in the wind tunnel even by a small amount which
produced only a small reduction in thrust for the smallest rotor studied (𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 = 120 mm). For the
larger rotors (𝑅𝑡𝑖𝑝 ≥ 267 mm), broadband noise associated with BWI or TIN were not mitigated
at the low inflow speeds studied of 2 m/s and 5 m/s. Most of the mid frequency noise associated
with interaction noise sources were mitigated for 𝑈∞ ≥ 8 m/s. Predictions of the noise produced
at the BPF for the smallest rotor studied were accurate within 3 dB for all observer locations.
Predictions of the measured directivity of the BPF for the larger rotors were inaccurate although
predicted thrust of each rotor agreed with the measurement. The largest rotors tested were equal
in diameter to that of the open jet inlet. Thus, they exceeded the limits of the facility. Interaction
with the open-jet shear layer was the likely cause of the excess noise beyond the noise predictions.
All rotors exhibited a directivity pattern that changed with rotational rate. This shifting pattern was
not observed in predictions. Overall, these results show that introducing a small amount of inflow
may be a good option for analyzing the noise at hover conditions as the tonal component could be
analyzed independently of the broadband without sacrificing performance. However, this was also
shown to be heavily dependent on the rotor diameter with regards to the open jet inlet and
experimentalist must take this into consideration.
Self-noise predictions using the BPM model were also presented for the DJI 9450S rotor
operating at hover RPM with the open jet operating at multiple inflow speeds. For inflow speeds
less than 2 m/s the spectral shape was well predicted from 1 kHz to 5 kHz. While the spectral
shape was well predicted, the amplitudes of the broadband self-noise was under predicted by at
most 5 dB over this range of frequencies. Over this frequency range, noise associated with the
interaction of the turbulent boundary layer with the suction side of the rotor blade and separation
of the boundary layer was found to be most dominant. Above 11 kHz, bluntness noise was found
to dominate. While the predicted spectral shape for low inflow speeds were in reasonable
agreement with that of the measured, those for inflow speeds greater than or equal to 5 m/s were
not. The BPM method is an empirical model based on 2D NACA 0012 airfoil profiles encountering
smooth flow and is very sensitive to the required inputs of displacement thickness, angle of attack,
and boundary layer edge velocity [77]. Each of these inputs require accurate modeling of the flow
and have been poorly modeled as XFOIL lacks the ability to model flows within the transitional
regime as well as have difficulty converging at small angles of attack. A more accurate estimation
of input parameters that can accurately model the boundary layer transition would be
recommended. For calculation of broadband self-noise at higher advance ratios, CAA models may
be more beneficial than BPM. Calculations of this type account for the 3D spanwise flows that can
occur as the rotor blades become stalled. For these calculations, the transition and separation-stall
effects must be correctly modeled using transitional turbulence models like shown in Pisharoti
[95]. These predictions were also performed for the larger CF30x10.5 T-motor rotor but were
inaccurate.
The effects of yaw on aerodynamic performance and noise were also assessed for a DJI
9450S rotor. For increasing yaw at the examined wind tunnel velocities, thrust was found to
converge to the value for zero inflow velocity. Contours of SPL as a function of yaw angle and
frequency were presented for three observer positions with the open jet tunnel off and a freestream
145

speed of 8 m/s. For all three observer positions, spectral levels were a minimum for frequencies
from 5×BPF to 30×BPF when the observer location was in-plane. Significant increases in
broadband noise were observed for increasing yaw angles. Above the rotor plane, spectral levels
of broadband noise at frequencies above 30×BPF increased significantly at each of the presented
microphone positions with an 8 m/s inflow velocity compared to 0 m/s. Additionally, a narrow
region of increased broadband noise between 80 and 100×BPF appeared with the addition of
inflow at small yaw angles for observers located beneath the rotor plane.
The aerodynamic performance of a commercially available CF30x10.5 T-motor eVTOL
rotor was compared to that of an in-house designed low noise swept rotor. It was shown that the
sweep did not reduce aerodynamic performance as the measured thrust and torque at different
operating conditions were like the commercially available unswept rotor. Measured noise without
a tunnel-driven inflow was compared. At static conditions, the noise spectra shows that the swept
rotor has lower noise levels from 1-5 kHz. Separation of the tonal and broadband components
showed that introduction of sweep is effective at reducing the broadband noise over the frequency
range associated with noise most likely due to BWI and TIN.
Lastly, noise was measured with and without an acoustic enclosure installed around a
brushless DC motor. The effect of ESC switching rate on the measure noise was assessed. Motor
tones were found to be most significant with respect to the rotor noise for an in-plane observer
above 2.7 kHz. The ESC switching rates as well as the model of the ESC were shown to have
different effects on the radiated noise. The noise was shown to decrease with increasing switching
frequency. Thus, a higher switching rate is recommended for the design of quiet systems. Overall,
both the ESC and switching rate should be considered when designing a quiet eVTOL system.
Also, the addition of a simple acoustic enclosure was found to be effective at reducing noise
associated with the electric motor at high frequencies although it would add additional weight to
the overall system.
